  1. List out who actually needs to know when a spot opens: a sign, an app, maybe a pricing service — more than one, and the list is likely to grow.
  2. Reject the naive option first: having every interested system poll the spot's status on its own schedule works, but "who cares about this" ends up hardcoded, scattered across every one of them.
  3. Flip the direction of the relationship: instead of others repeatedly asking "did it change?", have the spot announce "it changed" once, to whoever's listening.
  4. Recognize the win: ParkingLot never needs to know what a "sign" or an "app" even is — it just publishes changes, and each subscriber decides what to do with them.

A real deployment usually has more than one thing that cares when a spot opens up — a digital sign at the entrance, a mobile app showing live availability, maybe a pricing service that raises rates as a lot fills up.

  • The Observer pattern fits directly: ParkingSpot (or Level) maintains a list of observers and notifies them on every state transition, instead of every interested party polling the spot's status on its own schedule.
  • What it decouples: ParkingLot never needs to know what a "sign" or an "app" even is — it just publishes state changes, and whatever's listening decides what to do with them.

This is the in-process ancestor of the pub/sub pattern used at the service level in the crawler and invoice-search examples — same idea, different scale.

Does it hold up?

Non-functional requirementHow this design satisfies it
Thread-safe concurrent entryAtomic spot assignment (lock or compare-and-swap) prevents two vehicles claiming one spot.
Fast spot assignmentEach Level maintains its own free-spot index rather than scanning every spot on every request.
Extensible to new vehicle typesVehicle is an interface — adding a type means adding a class, not modifying ParkingLot.
Extensible pricingFeeCalculator is a Strategy — swapping or adding pricing schemes doesn't touch parking logic.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
Optimistic (compare-and-swap) vs. pessimistic lockingCAS avoids blocking under low contention but needs retry logic on failure; a lock is simpler to reason about but serializes every access, even when contention is rare.
Singleton ParkingLotGuarantees one source of truth with simple global access, at the cost of hidden global state that's harder to unit test and assumes exactly one lot per process forever.
Per-Level free-spot indexNear-instant lookup for an available spot, in exchange for extra bookkeeping to keep that index in sync every time a spot's status changes.

Tools you might reach for

LLD problems live in one process — "tools" here means language primitives, not infrastructure products.

ComponentReal-world options
Concurrency controlJava's ReentrantLock / AtomicReference, Go's sync.Mutex, Python's threading.Lock
Persistence (if needed across restarts)SQLite or PostgreSQL for ticket/booking history — often skipped entirely for a pure in-memory answer
Worked Example E — Extreme Contention

Design Ticketmaster

high-contention inventory · demand spikes 1000x normal traffic
Click to expand

"Design a system that sells tickets to events, where a popular on-sale can see hundreds of thousands of people trying to buy a few thousand seats in the same 60 seconds." The parking lot taught single-process concurrency; this teaches the distributed version, plus the pattern for surviving a demand spike the architecture wasn't sized for.

1
Requirements
Functional
  • Browse events and see real-time seat availability.
  • Hold a seat temporarily while the buyer checks out.
  • Confirm purchase — the seat is sold, never to two people.
  • Release the hold automatically if checkout isn't completed in time.
Non-functional
  • Zero overselling — a seat is sold at most once, no exceptions.
  • Survive a traffic spike orders of magnitude above steady-state (a popular on-sale).
  • Fair-ish ordering under load — not first-request-wins in a way that just rewards whoever has the fastest bot.

Worth asking: is overselling truly zero-tolerance, or is a rare, immediately-refunded double-sale acceptable? The answer changes whether strict locking or optimistic retry is the right default. We'll assume zero-tolerance.

2
Core Entities
  • Event (id, venue, start_time) — what people are buying tickets to.
  • Seat (id, event_id, status, hold_expires_at) — status is Available, Held, or Sold.
  • Hold (seat_id, user_id, expires_at) — a short-lived reservation, typically 5–10 minutes.
  • Order (user_id, seat_ids, payment_status) — created once checkout succeeds.
3
API

POST /events/{id}/holds {seat_ids} → hold token, or 409 if already held/sold
POST /orders {hold_token, payment} → confirmed order, or 410 if the hold expired
GET /events/{id}/seats → real-time availability map

4
Data Flow

Skipped for the core purchase flow — it's synchronous request/response. (Hold expiry, covered in the deep dive below, is the one async piece worth tracing separately.)

5
High-Level Design

A waiting room gates entry before anyone reaches the part of the system that can actually contend for seats — diagrammed and walked through below.

6
Deep Dives

Three below: (a) preventing overselling under distributed contention, (b) the virtual waiting room pattern for demand spikes, and (c) bot and scalper prevention.

A waiting room gates entry — only admitted buyers ever reach the seats that can actually contend
← scroll to see the full diagram →
Successful purchase
  1. Buyer is admitted from the Waiting Room at a throttled rate.
  2. Buyer selects seats; Booking Service requests a Hold on Seat Inventory.
  3. Inventory atomically flips the seat from Available to Held, with a short TTL.
  4. Buyer completes checkout within the TTL; Payment Service confirms payment.
  5. Seat flips from Held to Sold — permanently, an Order is created.
Expired hold
  1. Buyer is admitted and holds a seat, but abandons checkout or is too slow.
  2. The hold's TTL expires — checked lazily on the next access, or swept periodically.
  3. The seat flips back from Held to Available, visible to the next buyer.
  4. No Order is created; nothing needed to be explicitly cancelled.
Key deep dive — preventing overselling under distributed contention
FRHold a seatFRConfirm purchaseNFRZero overselling
Thought process
  1. Recognize this is the same shape of bug as the parking lot's race — check then act — just spread across many service instances instead of one process.
  2. Notice why a simple lock doesn't scale the same way here: coordinating a distributed lock across many instances adds real overhead, and a new failure mode — what happens if the lock-holder crashes?
  3. Flip the strategy: instead of asking permission before acting, attempt the write and let the database's own atomicity decide who wins — no lock to coordinate, no lock-holder that can crash mid-hold.
  4. Confirm this actually closes the gap: the conditional write only succeeds if the row still matches the expected state, so two simultaneous attempts can't both succeed — one gets 0 rows affected.

Unlike the parking lot's single-process lock, holds here are checked and set across possibly many Booking Service instances hitting the same inventory row at once. A naive "read status, then write Held" is the same check-then-act race from the parking lot, just distributed.

Illustration — the conditional writeUPDATE seats SET status='held' WHERE id = ? AND status = 'available' → succeeds, 1 row affected → this caller wins the seat → 0 rows affected → someone else already holds it

This is optimistic concurrency: don't ask permission first, attempt the write and let the database's atomicity guarantee reject the losers. It scales better than a distributed lock because there's no lock to hold or release — just one conditional write per attempt.

Key deep dive — the virtual waiting room
FRBrowse / see availabilityNFRSurvives a traffic spike
Thought process
  1. Ask where the actual load comes from during a big on-sale — not from people buying seats, but from far more people just looking.
  2. Recognize that's a fundamentally different problem than the seat-hold correctness fix — you can have a perfectly correct hold mechanism and still get crushed by read traffic before anyone even reaches it.
  3. Reframe the goal: don't try to make the backend handle unlimited simultaneous lookers — control how many people are even allowed to reach it at once.
  4. Admit people to the booking flow at a rate the backend can sustain, and give everyone else something better than a broken page — a queue position.

The actual bottleneck in a ticket on-sale usually isn't the seat-holding logic — it's that far more people want to look at seats than could ever buy one, and all that read traffic can crush the same database the holds depend on.

  • What it decouples: "how many people are interested" from "how many people are actively contending for inventory right now," by admitting buyers to the booking flow at a fixed rate the backend can actually sustain.
  • What everyone else sees: a queue position instead of a broken page.

Same shape as the rate limiter's job, aimed at a different goal: the rate limiter protects a service from too much traffic; the waiting room protects fairness and stability during a spike its designers knew was coming.

Key deep dive — bot and scalper prevention
NFRFair-ish ordering under load
Thought process
  1. Ask whether the concurrency fix — the conditional write — actually solves the fairness problem, not just the correctness one. It doesn't: a script can still win hundreds of individually correct writes faster than any human could click.
  2. Recognize this needs a different kind of defense — not "is this write correct," but "is this behavior even human."
  3. Attack it from multiple angles, since no single check reliably distinguishes a fast human from a script: limit how much one account can hold, add friction at entry, and slow down rapid-fire attempts specifically.
  4. Accept that none of these are airtight alone — a determined bot adapts to any single one — so layer several imperfect defenses instead of chasing one perfect check.

Even a perfectly correct hold-and-purchase mechanism doesn't stop a script from acquiring holds on hundreds of seats faster than any human could click — the concurrency fix prevents overselling, not unfair buying.

  • Per-account purchase limits enforced at the same layer as the seat hold — one account can hold at most N seats for a given event at a time.
  • Friction at the waiting-room entry — a CAPTCHA or proof-of-work check adds just enough cost to make large-scale automation expensive, without meaningfully slowing down a real buyer.
  • Rate limiting the hold endpoint itself per account/IP — the same rate-limiter pattern from earlier in this framework, aimed specifically at slowing rapid-fire hold attempts.

None of these are airtight alone — bots adapt to each one — which is why real systems layer several imperfect defenses rather than searching for one perfect one.

Key deep dive — releasing an expired hold
FRRelease hold automatically
Thought process
  1. State the requirement plainly: a hold nobody completes has to eventually stop blocking that seat for everyone else.
  2. Consider a periodic sweep alone: a background job scans for expired holds and releases them. Ask what happens between expiry and the next sweep — the seat still shows as held even though the hold is already dead.
  3. Consider checking at the point of use instead: before trusting a seat's stored status, check whether its hold has already expired.
  4. Notice neither is complete alone — a lazy check only fires when someone looks at that exact seat again; a sweep alone leaves a staleness window. Run both.

A Hold carries its own expires_at — nothing needs to actively cancel it for the reservation to become logically invalid the moment that time passes.

  • Lazy check on access: whenever a seat's status is read or written, check hold_expires_at first — if it's passed, treat the seat as Available regardless of what the stored status literally says, and update the row opportunistically.
  • Periodic sweep: a background job releases expired holds even for seats nobody's actively querying, so aggregate availability (the browse page) doesn't drift stale indefinitely.

The lazy check keeps the path that matters most — someone requesting that exact seat — instantly correct without waiting on a sweep interval; the sweep keeps everything else roughly honest in the meantime. Same "correctness on the hot path, eventual consistency elsewhere" split as the URL shortener's async click recording.

Does it hold up?

Non-functional requirementHow this design satisfies it
Zero oversellingConditional writes on seat status — the database's atomicity is the source of truth, not application logic.
Survives extreme demand spikesWaiting room throttles admission to booking, decoupling interest from contention.
Holds don't leak inventory foreverTTL-based holds expire automatically; no explicit cancellation required.
Fair-ish under loadWaiting room queue position, not raw request speed, determines admission order.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
Optimistic concurrency (conditional write) vs. a distributed lockScales without lock contention and nothing to release on a crash, but every caller needs retry logic to handle a rejected write gracefully.
Virtual waiting room vs. no admission controlKeeps the backend stable and buyers treated fairly during a spike, at the cost of extra infrastructure to build and — if left in the path permanently — added friction on ordinary, non-spike days.
TTL-based holds vs. explicit-release-onlyGuarantees inventory can't be locked up forever by an abandoned checkout, but the TTL length is itself a trade-off — too short frustrates slow buyers, too long wastes inventory during a hot sale.

Tools you might reach for

ComponentReal-world options
Waiting Rooma Redis-backed token queue, or a managed virtual-queue product
Seat InventoryPostgreSQL (conditional UPDATE) or DynamoDB (conditional writes)
PaymentStripe, Braintree — integrated, not designed here
Worked Example F — Real-Time Delivery

Design WhatsApp

push, not pull · server initiates delivery
Click to expand

"Design a messaging system where users send messages that arrive on the recipient's device in real time, with delivery and read receipts." Every example so far has been client-initiated request/response. This one flips it: the server has to push to a client that never asked, which is a genuinely different problem.

1
Requirements
Functional
  • Send a message to another user; it arrives in real time if they're online.
  • If the recipient is offline, the message is delivered when they reconnect.
  • Sender sees delivery status: sent, delivered, read.
  • Support group chats — one message fans out to many recipients.
Non-functional
  • Low latency for online-to-online delivery (sub-second).
  • No message loss — offline delivery must be reliable, not best-effort.
  • Messages arrive in order within a single conversation.
  • Scale to a persistent connection per active user, globally.

Worth asking: end-to-end encrypted? Does that change where message content can be inspected/indexed? We'll note it as a constraint without designing the crypto itself.

2
Core Entities
  • User (id, last_seen) — a participant.
  • Conversation (id, participant_ids) — 1:1 or group.
  • Message (id, conversation_id, sender_id, content, sent_at, seq, status) — status is Sent, Delivered, or Read; seq is a per-conversation monotonic counter, the actual ordering key.
  • Connection (user_id, gateway_node, connected_at) — which server currently holds this user's live socket, if any.
3
API

A persistent connection, not stateless HTTP — the "endpoints" are message types over a WebSocket. send(conversation_id, content) → ack with a message id
on('message') → pushed to the client when one arrives
on('receipt') → pushed when a message's status changes

4
Data Flow

Skipped for a single message — it's one hop, sender to recipient. Fan-out to a group is closer to a pipeline and gets its own treatment in the deep dive below.

5
High-Level Design

The online and offline paths diverge right after the message is persisted — diagrammed and walked through below.

6
Deep Dives

Three below: (a) finding which server holds a user's live connection, (b) group chat fan-out, and (c) syncing one account across multiple devices.

The message is always persisted first — delivery is a separate concern from durability
← scroll to see the full diagram →
Recipient online
  1. Sender's client sends the message over its WebSocket to Gateway A.
  2. Message Service asks the Connection Registry which gateway holds the recipient's live socket.
  3. Message is persisted to the Message Store — durability first, always.
  4. Message Service pushes the message directly to Gateway B, which forwards it over the recipient's open socket.
  5. Recipient's client acks receipt; status flows back to Sent → Delivered.
Recipient offline
  1. Same first three steps — the Connection Registry has no entry for the recipient.
  2. A push notification wakes the recipient's device (without the message content, typically).
  3. When the recipient's app reconnects, it fetches undelivered messages from the Message Store.
  4. Status updates to Delivered once the client acks; no message is ever silently dropped.
Key deep dive — finding which server holds a live connection
FRReal-time delivery if onlineNFRSub-second online delivery
Thought process
  1. Start from the normal assumption for most web services: any server behind the load balancer can handle any request. Test whether that holds here.
  2. It doesn't — a WebSocket connection is long-lived and pinned to one specific server the moment it's established. Sending a message means finding the one server holding that person's open socket.
  3. Solve the "which server" question with a lookup: a shared registry mapping user to gateway, checked before every send.
  4. Decide how strict that lookup needs to be. A slightly stale answer isn't catastrophic — it just means one failed push and a safe fallback — so it needs to be fast, not strongly consistent.

A user's WebSocket is a stateful, long-lived connection pinned to one specific gateway server — unlike a stateless HTTP request that any instance behind a load balancer can serve.

Illustration — the two-hop sendsend(message, to: user_42) → Connection Registry: user_42 is on Gateway B → call Gateway B directly → push over user_42's open socket
  • The Connection Registry (a fast shared store, typically Redis) maps user_id to gateway_node, updated on every connect/disconnect — the load balancer's normal "any instance will do" assumption doesn't apply here.
  • A slightly stale registry entry just means one extra failed push attempt and a fallback to the offline path — a safe failure mode, worth stating explicitly rather than over-engineering strong consistency into this lookup.
Key deep dive — group chat fan-out
FRGroup chats
Thought process
  1. Notice the send logic built for one recipient doesn't obviously generalize — a group message needs that same online/offline decision made independently, once per participant.
  2. Recognize the scale question that creates: for a 500-person group, that's 500 individual lookups and pushes per message, a fundamentally bigger job than a 1:1 send.
  3. Consider the simplest option first: push to every online participant immediately. Simple, but the cost at send time now scales directly with group size.
  4. Reconsider for the extreme case: push immediately only to people active recently, and let everyone else catch up on reconnect instead of paying the full fan-out cost upfront every time.

A group message needs the same online/offline delivery decision made independently for every participant, not just one recipient — for a 500-person group, sending is now closer to the fan-out problem than a single message.

  • Fan-out-on-write: look up and push to every online participant's gateway immediately — keeps the read side simple but costs more at send time.
  • Hybrid, for very large groups: push immediately to a subset (e.g. participants active in the last few minutes) and let the rest pull on reconnect.

Same push-vs-pull trade-off as the framework's fan-out glossary entry — a group chat is just a case where "how many recipients" varies by four orders of magnitude between a 1:1 chat and a large group, which is exactly when a fixed strategy stops being the right default.

Key deep dive — syncing one account across multiple devices
FRDelivery status: sent/delivered/readNFRScale, one connection/user, globally
Thought process
  1. Revisit an assumption baked into everything so far: one connection per user. Ask whether that's actually true for a real messaging app — it isn't.
  2. Trace what breaks because of that assumption: the registry currently maps a user to one gateway — that has to become a one-to-many mapping, or a send only reaches whichever device happened to register last.
  3. Realize sending the message isn't the only thing that needs to fan out — read status does too, or a message read on the phone won't show as read on the laptop.
  4. Recognize this is the same mechanism as the group-chat fan-out above, just aimed at one person's own devices instead of many different people.

The design so far assumes one connection per user; real messaging apps need the same account open on a phone, a laptop, and a tablet at once, all showing the same messages.

  • The registry needs a one-to-many mapping: user_id maps to potentially several gateway connections, not one — a send fans out to every currently-connected device for that user, not just the first one found.
  • Status has to reconcile across devices too: a message read on the phone should show as read on the laptop, which means status changes fan out across a user's own devices, not only to the other side of the conversation.

This is the same fan-out shape as the group-chat deep dive above, just fanning out to one person's devices instead of many people — the mechanism doesn't change, only who's on the receiving end.

Key deep dive — no lost messages, correct order
NFRNo message lossNFRCorrect ordering
Thought process
  1. Ask what "no message loss" actually rules out: a delivery attempt is allowed to fail — a gateway can crash mid-push — but the message itself must never disappear because of that.
  2. Locate the one step that has to be non-negotiable: the message must be durably written before the system does anything else with it, including trying to deliver it.
  3. Once that's true, delivery becomes a retry problem, not a data-loss problem — a failed push just means try again from the durable copy, not "the message is gone."
  4. For ordering, don't assume it's free: two messages sent close together can arrive at the store slightly out of send order, so ordering needs its own explicit key, not just "whichever wrote first."

Both guarantees come from the same discipline: the Message Store is written to first, before any delivery attempt, and every message gets an ordering key that doesn't depend on when a push happened to succeed.

  • Write before you push: a message is durably persisted the moment it's accepted, before the system attempts the WebSocket push or the offline path. A crashed gateway or a failed push is retried against the durable copy — it never means the message itself was lost.
  • Order by a monotonic key, not wall-clock time alone: a per-conversation sequence number (or a timestamp with a tie-breaking id) survives clock skew and near-simultaneous sends; sorting purely by sent_at from different servers can't guarantee that.

Same instinct as Ticketmaster's seat hold and the URL shortener's click recording: identify the one write that has to be correct and durable, and let everything after it — delivery, retries, display — be the part that's allowed to be eventually consistent.

Does it hold up?

Non-functional requirementHow this design satisfies it
Low-latency online deliveryDirect gateway-to-gateway push via the Connection Registry, no polling.
No message loss when offlineMessage Store persists before any delivery attempt — delivery failure never means data loss.
In-order delivery per conversationMessages within one conversation are sequenced and delivered in that order to each recipient.
Scales to millions of live connectionsStateful gateways horizontally scaled by connection count, coordinated through the shared Connection Registry.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
Push (stateful WS gateway) vs. pull/pollingReal-time, low-latency delivery, but gateways are stateful — harder to scale, rebalance, and deploy than stateless HTTP servers.
Fan-out-on-write for groupsFast delivery to every online member, but the send cost is paid once per member — expensive at send time for very large groups.
At-least-once delivery vs. exactly-onceA much simpler system with no complex dedup/consensus protocol, in exchange for the client needing to handle occasional duplicate messages itself.

Tools you might reach for

ComponentReal-world options
WS Gatewaya Socket.IO cluster, or a managed WebSocket gateway (AWS API Gateway WebSockets)
Connection RegistryRedis
Message StoreCassandra, DynamoDB
Push NotificationAPNs (iOS), FCM (Android)
Worked Example G — Geospatial Matching

Design Uber

geospatial index · high-write location stream · state machine
Click to expand

"Design a ride-hailing system that matches riders with nearby available drivers and tracks a ride from request to completion." New territory: finding the nearest of many moving things, fast, under a constant flood of location updates.

1
Requirements
Functional
  • Rider requests a ride from their current location.
  • System matches them with a nearby available driver.
  • Both parties track the ride's progress in real time.
  • Ride moves through a clear lifecycle: requested → matched → in-progress → completed.
Non-functional
  • Match a rider to a driver in seconds, not tens of seconds.
  • Handle a continuous, high-volume stream of driver location updates (every few seconds, per active driver).
  • Nearby search must be fast — never scan every driver in the city on every request.

Worth asking: what counts as "nearby" — straight-line distance, or actual driving time? We'll assume straight-line for matching, with driving time estimated afterward.

2
Core Entities
  • Rider, Driver (id, current_location, status) — driver status is Available, EnRoute, or Offline.
  • Ride (id, rider_id, driver_id, status, pickup, dropoff) — status is the state machine: Requested, Matched, InProgress, Completed.
  • LocationPing (driver_id, lat, lng, timestamp) — the high-frequency write that keeps the geospatial index current.
3
API

POST /rides {pickup, dropoff} → ride id, matched driver (or queued if none nearby)
POST /drivers/{id}/location {lat, lng} → ack — called every few seconds by the driver's app
GET /rides/{id} → current status and live driver location

4
Data Flow

Skipped for the matching request itself — it's synchronous. The location-ping stream (deep dive below) is closer to a pipeline: ingest → update index → nothing further downstream per ping.

5
High-Level Design

Matching reads from a constantly-updating geospatial index that's written by an entirely separate, much higher-throughput path — diagrammed below.

6
Deep Dives

Three below: (a) finding nearby drivers fast, (b) surviving the location-ping write volume, and (c) avoiding two drivers accepting the same ride.

Two very different load profiles meet at one shared index — reads asking "who's nearby," writes constantly updating "where is everyone"
← scroll to see the full diagram →
Request a ride
  1. Rider requests a ride from their current location.
  2. Matching Service queries the Geospatial Index for available drivers near the pickup point.
  3. Nearest available driver is selected; a Ride record is created in the Requested/Matched state.
  4. The matched driver is notified and can accept.
Location updates (continuous)
  1. Every active driver's app pings their current location every few seconds.
  2. Location Service writes the update into the Geospatial Index — this never touches Ride records.
  3. The index's view of "who's where" is always slightly behind reality, and that's an accepted trade-off, not a bug.
Key deep dive — finding nearby drivers fast
FRMatch with nearby driverNFRMatch in secondsNFRNearby search stays fast
Thought process
  1. Start with the naive approach: for a given rider, check the distance to every driver and pick the closest ones. Test it against scale.
  2. It breaks down fast — checking distance to every driver in a city means the cost of one match grows with the total number of drivers, not just the number actually nearby.
  3. Ask whether every driver genuinely needs comparing, or just the ones that could plausibly be close. Only the latter.
  4. Pre-organize drivers by location so "who's nearby" becomes a lookup instead of a scan — divide the map into cells, and only check drivers in the same or neighboring cells as the rider.

Scanning every driver's coordinates and computing distance to each one doesn't scale past a small city.

  • Geohash grid: divides the map into cells identified by short string prefixes — drivers in the same or neighboring cells share a prefix, so "find drivers near this point" becomes a lookup of a handful of cells instead of a scan of every driver everywhere.
  • Quadtree: the other common answer to the same problem — a bit more implementation complexity, in exchange for cells that adapt to driver density (small cells downtown, large cells in sparse areas).

Either structure is usually kept in memory (Redis has native geospatial commands built on exactly this idea) — the index needs to answer in milliseconds, and it's rebuilt continuously anyway from the location stream, so durability isn't the point of this particular store.

Key deep dive — surviving the location-ping write volume
FRRider requests from current locationNFRHigh-volume location stream
Thought process
  1. Notice this path is different from most of the system: it's not triggered by a user action, it's a constant stream — every active driver, every few seconds, whether or not anyone's requesting a ride.
  2. Recognize what that means for scale: this is very likely the highest-QPS write path in the whole system, worth explicit attention rather than assuming "writes are writes."
  3. Ask what the actual requirement is for this specific data — does a location ping need the same durability as a completed ride record? No — matching just needs "roughly current," not "never lost."
  4. Use that gap deliberately: route pings to a fast, in-memory index instead of durable storage, since losing one ping is a non-event when the next one arrives seconds later.

With every active driver writing every few seconds, this is easily the highest-QPS write path in the whole system — worth running the math toolkit on explicitly rather than assuming.

  • The design move: location pings never touch the same storage as Ride records. They update an in-memory index that only needs to be "roughly current," not durably persisted per-ping.
  • Why that's safe: losing the last location update if a node restarts is a non-event, since a fresh one arrives within seconds.

This is relaxing a guarantee (durability, per-write) as the correct engineering call, not a shortcut — the NFR was never "never lose a location ping," it was "matching sees roughly current locations." Satisfy the requirement actually stated, not a stricter one nobody asked for.

Key deep dive — avoiding two drivers accepting the same ride
FRMatch with nearby driver
Thought process
  1. Notice the shape of what's being proposed: notify multiple nearby drivers at once to reduce rider wait time.
  2. Recognize that's the exact same race already seen twice on this page — multiple actors, one resource, a gap between "check" and "act."
  3. Consider the simple fix first: only ever ask one driver at a time. It avoids the race completely, but costs latency waiting on drivers who don't respond.
  4. If speed matters more, notify several at once but apply the fix already proven for the seat hold — an atomic conditional write that lets only the first acceptance claim the ride.

If the Matching Service notifies more than one nearby driver at once to shorten the rider's wait, it's created the same race condition seen twice already on this page.

  • Sequential offering: notify only the single nearest driver, wait briefly, move to the next if they decline. Avoids the race entirely, at the cost of added latency waiting on drivers who don't respond.
  • Notify several, take the first accept: faster for the rider, but needs the same atomic conditional write as Ticketmaster's seat hold — the Ride record only transitions to Matched for whichever driver's acceptance lands first; every other acceptance is rejected because the row no longer matches the expected state.

Third time this exact shape has shown up on this page — a parking spot, a seat, and now a ride request are all the same problem: two actors racing to claim one thing.

Key deep dive — tracking a ride in real time
FRTrack progress in real timeFRClear ride lifecycle
Thought process
  1. Notice this splits into two different questions that get conflated easily: how does a status change reach both people, and what are the actual valid statuses to move between?
  2. For delivery, recognize this is the exact same problem WhatsApp already solved — pushing an update to a specific online user's device. Reuse that, don't re-invent it.
  3. For the states themselves, list what's actually possible: a ride can't go from Requested straight to Completed, and once Completed it can't go anywhere. That's not a free-text status field, it's a constrained set of transitions.
  4. Model that constraint directly — a state machine — so an invalid transition (marking a Requested ride as Completed) is rejected by the model itself, not caught by a scattered if-check somewhere in application code.

Rider and driver apps both need to see the ride move through its states as they happen — not by polling, and not by re-deriving delivery from scratch.

  • Delivery reuses WhatsApp's push mechanism: each status change (Matched, driver arriving, InProgress, Completed) is a small message pushed to both the rider's and driver's live connections — the same connection-registry lookup and gateway push from the WhatsApp example, aimed at ride events instead of chat messages.
  • The lifecycle is a state machine, not a status string: Requested → Matched → InProgress → Completed is an explicit set of allowed transitions. The Ride Record only accepts a write that moves it to a state reachable from its current one — the same discipline as the parking spot's status field, applied to a longer chain.

Splitting these apart matters: the state machine is about what changes are even valid; the push mechanism is about how a valid change reaches the people who need to see it. Conflating them tends to produce code where an invalid state transition and a failed notification look like the same kind of bug.

Does it hold up?

Non-functional requirementHow this design satisfies it
Fast matchingGeohash grid or quadtree turns nearby-search into a handful of cell lookups, not a full scan.
High-volume location writesLocation pings update an in-memory index, decoupled entirely from the durable Ride record store.
Real-time ride trackingSame push-delivery pattern as the messaging example — the rider's client is pushed location updates, not polling for them.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
In-memory, non-durable geospatial indexMillisecond lookups that match the "roughly current" requirement, but a node restart loses recent pings until drivers re-ping — acceptable here, wouldn't be for a system that actually needed durability.
Geohash grid vs. quadtreeGeohash is simpler to implement and good enough for most cases; a quadtree adapts to uneven driver density at the cost of extra implementation complexity.
Straight-line "nearby" vs. actual driving-time distanceFast to compute with no routing engine needed for the first match pass, but can suggest a driver who's close as the crow flies yet far by road — a river or highway between them.

Tools you might reach for

ComponentReal-world options
Geospatial IndexRedis Geo commands, Elasticsearch geo-queries
Location ingestionKafka
Ride RecordPostgreSQL, DynamoDB
Worked Example H — Conflict Detection

Design a Meeting Room Booking System

interval-overlap queries · recurring events
Click to expand

"Design a system that lets employees book meeting rooms for a time range, without double-booking, including recurring meetings." Smaller stakes than Ticketmaster, but the same correctness question wearing a calendar costume — and a genuinely different algorithmic core: not a single point of contention, but a range that has to be checked against every other range.

1
Requirements
Functional
  • Book a room for a specific time range.
  • Check a room's availability before booking.
  • Search for available rooms across a given time range — not just check one specific room, but find any room (optionally filtered by capacity or location) that's free.
  • Support recurring meetings (e.g. a weekly standup).
  • Cancel or modify a booking — including a single occurrence of a recurring series.
Non-functional
  • Never double-book a room — the same correctness bar as Ticketmaster, at far smaller scale.
  • Overlap checks stay fast even for a room with a dense calendar.
  • Searching across many rooms for a free slot stays a single query, not one check per room.
  • Recurring meetings shouldn't require pre-generating years of instances up front.

Worth asking: single building/timezone, or multi-office? Can one meeting need two rooms at once? Should room search return every free match, or just the best fit? We'll assume single timezone, one room per meeting, and return every free match sorted by capacity — smallest first.

2
Core Entities
  • Room (id, capacity, location) — the resource being booked.
  • Booking (id, room_id, start_time, end_time, organizer_id, status, recurrence_rule?) — a single reservation, or the anchor of a series. Status is Confirmed or Cancelled — record-keeping, not the conflict guard; a cancelled row stays for history instead of being deleted, and the overlap check simply excludes it.
  • RecurrenceRule (frequency, interval, end_condition) — e.g. "every Monday until Dec 31."
3
API

POST /rooms/{id}/bookings {start, end, recurrence_rule?} → booking id, or 409 with the conflicting slot
GET /rooms/{id}/availability?from&to → free/busy blocks for one room
GET /rooms/available?from&to&capacity_min?&location? → every free room matching the filters, sorted by capacity
DELETE /bookings/{id}?scope=single|series → cancels one occurrence or the whole series

4
Data Flow

Skipped — this is synchronous request/response, same reasoning as the URL shortener and rate limiter. Recurring-meeting expansion is a real design question, but it's answered as a storage strategy in the deep dive below, not a pipeline.

5
High-Level Design

Every booking attempt passes through the same overlap check before it's allowed to write — diagrammed below.

6
Deep Dives

Three below: (a) efficient overlap detection, (b) storing recurring meetings, and (c) time zones.

Check-then-write, same shape as Ticketmaster's hold — the correctness question is identical, the stakes and scale aren't
← scroll to see the full diagram →
Book a meeting
  1. Client requests a room for a time range.
  2. Booking Service asks the Availability Checker whether any existing booking in that room overlaps the range.
  3. No conflict found — Booking Service performs an atomic conditional write to the Bookings DB.
  4. Booking confirmed, returned to the client.
Handle a conflict
  1. Client requests a room for a time range.
  2. Availability Checker finds an existing overlapping booking.
  3. Booking Service returns a 409 with the conflicting slot — no write occurs.
  4. Client retries with a different time or room; nothing needed to be rolled back, since nothing was written.
Key deep dive — efficient overlap detection
FRBook a roomFRCheck availabilityFRSearch available roomsNFRNever double-bookNFRFast overlap checksNFRRoom search stays one query
Thought process
  1. Write down the overlap condition precisely: two ranges conflict when each one starts before the other ends. A simple comparison — not the hard part of this problem.
  2. Test the naive way to use that comparison: check a new booking against every existing booking in the room. Ask what happens as a room's calendar fills up — the check gets slower with every booking added.
  3. Narrow the search before comparing: an index on room and start time means only bookings anywhere near the requested window even get looked at. This is enough for almost every room.
  4. Ask what "almost every room" leaves out: a genuinely high-traffic room's calendar is still large even indexed, and every check still costs a database round trip. For that specific case, skip the database at read time entirely.
  5. Notice a fast single-room check doesn't answer a different question: "which of N rooms are free" isn't "run the single-room check N times" — it's the same predicate, expressed as one query instead of N.
  6. Realize none of the above touches correctness under concurrent writes: a fast, correct read can still race against another booking's write — a separate problem, needing its own fix.
The full journey — from search to a confirmed booking
  1. Search: the user asks for any free room matching a time and a filter (capacity, location). Read-only — no conflict is possible yet, since nothing has been claimed. Scales as one anti-join query across every room, not one check per room. Detailed below.
  2. Pick: the user selects one specific room and slot from the results and clicks "Book." This is the exact moment a race becomes possible — another user could have searched a second earlier and be about to pick the same slot. A UI action, not a mechanism — no dedicated section below.
  3. Check: the Booking Service asks "does anything on this one room overlap this range" — fast, via an index or a database constraint's own index. This step only makes the question fast; it does not, by itself, prevent anything on its own. Detailed below, together with write — they're one decision, not two.
  4. Write: the actual prevention happens here, and only here — one of two complete paths depending on what your database supports. Detailed below.
  5. Resolve: exactly one booker's write succeeds. The other needs a real answer, not a stack trace. Detailed below — and it reuses step 1's exact query, no new mechanism required.

Check makes "is this free" fast; write is the only step that actually stops two people from getting the same slot — worth keeping those two questions separate while designing this, even though the section below covers them together, since which check you use determines which write mechanism you need.

Two ranges [s1, e1) and [s2, e2) overlap exactly when s1 < e2 AND s2 < e1 — a simple comparison, the building block every step below is built on.

Illustration — the overlap condition itselfExisting: [10:00, 11:00) New request: [10:30, 11:30) 10:00 < 11:30 AND 10:30 < 11:00 → true → conflict

Worth heading off directly: why not a status column on Room, the way Ticketmaster flags a seat Available or Held? A seat has exactly one state at a time — the column is the answer. A room is free at 9am, booked at 10, free again at 2 — one column on the room can't hold that. Booking does get a status (Confirmed/Cancelled, added to Core Entities above), but it's record-keeping, not the conflict guard: the check is never "does a row exist with this status," it's "does any row's range overlap this one" — the same range comparison above, status column or not. A cancelled booking just gets excluded from that check with one added clause, not a different mechanism.

Step 1 — search: the same predicate, as an anti-join

"Is this one room free" is a single query; "which of N rooms are free" doesn't mean running that query N times.

One query — rooms with no matching row in an overlap subquery, filtered by capacity or location — answers it in a single pass, against the exact same (room_id, start_time) index and the same materialized near-term bookings the recurring-meetings strategy below already produces.

Illustration — one query, not one check per roomSELECT room.id FROM rooms room WHERE room.capacity >= 6 AND room.location = 'floor-3' AND NOT EXISTS ( SELECT 1 FROM bookings b WHERE b.room_id = room.id AND b.start_time < :to AND b.end_time > :from ) → every matching free room, in one round trip — not N overlap checks for N rooms

Step 2 — the user picking one room and clicking "Book" — sits between here and the next section. It's a UI action, not a mechanism, so nothing to design; the walkthrough below picks back up at check and write.

Steps 3 & 4 — check and write: one fork, not six options

These were named as separate steps above, but "how do we check fast" and "how do we prevent a conflict" aren't independent choices — which one you use for the check determines what you need for the write. It's one fork, then one complete path to follow, not a checklist of every technique.

The fork

Does your database support a native exclusion constraint for overlapping ranges (Postgres's EXCLUDE, or an equivalent)?

Yes → Path A

One constraint on the table. Done — no separate index for this query, no SERIALIZABLE, no lock. The database is the whole guarantee.

No → Path B

An index for the fast check, a conditional insert for the write, and one guard (SERIALIZABLE or a lock) wrapped around it — three pieces, working together.

Either path, optionally: layer an in-process tree on top for one genuinely hot room — that part is the same regardless of which path you're on, detailed at the end.

Path A — the EXCLUDE constraint (recommended, on Postgres)

A database-enforced rule: no two bookings for the same room may have overlapping ranges. Its own index handles the fast check too — this is the entire path, nothing else in this deep dive needs to be added on top of it.

Illustration — the constraint, and the ordinary insert it protectsCREATE TABLE bookings ( id SERIAL PRIMARY KEY, room_id INT NOT NULL, during TSRANGE NOT NULL, EXCLUDE USING GIST (room_id WITH =, during WITH &&) ); The write is a completely ordinary insert — no WHERE, no NOT EXISTS: INSERT INTO bookings (room_id, during) VALUES (:room_id, tsrange(:start, :end)); If a row already exists for this room whose "during" range overlaps — Postgres REJECTS the insert outright, atomically, at the storage engine level. No SERIALIZABLE isolation, no lock required — the guarantee is built into the schema, enforced the same way a UNIQUE constraint is. Two bookers, same slot, same instant: one INSERT succeeds, the other fails with a constraint violation — the database decided who won, not the app.

Why this is the recommendation, not just one option among several: it protects you even if a future developer writes a careless query, since the guarantee lives in the table, not in any one statement someone has to remember to write correctly every time.

Path B — index, conditional insert, and a guard (when EXCLUDE isn't available)

Three pieces that only work together — an index makes the check fast, a conditional insert bundles the check into the write, and one guard closes the race the conditional insert alone can't.

1 — the index
Illustration — the B-tree index, and the query it speeds upCREATE INDEX idx_bookings_room_start ON bookings (room_id, start_time); SELECT 1 FROM bookings WHERE room_id = :room_id AND start_time < :end AND end_time > :start; → the index narrows this to rows for the right room, near the right time — fast, but still just a SELECT. Two concurrent transactions can both run it, both see zero rows, and both then insert. The index makes the check fast; it does nothing to prevent the race below.
2 — the conditional insert

Ticketmaster's fix doesn't transfer as-is: a seat is one row with one status flag — a single conditional UPDATE is enough. A room's calendar has no single row to flip, so the check-across-many-rows and the insert have to happen as one atomic step — the pattern commonly called a conditional insert, the insert-side counterpart to Ticketmaster's conditional update.

Illustration — INSERT … WHERE NOT EXISTSINSERT INTO bookings (room_id, start_time, end_time, organizer_id) SELECT :room_id, :start, :end, :organizer_id WHERE NOT EXISTS ( SELECT 1 FROM bookings WHERE room_id = :room_id AND start_time < :end AND end_time > :start ); → one statement: the check and the insert happen together, not two round trips

Still not automatically safe on its own — a building block, not the complete guarantee. At the commonly-default READ COMMITTED isolation level, two concurrent transactions can both evaluate NOT EXISTS against the same pre-insert snapshot, both see "nothing conflicts," and both insert. Piece 3 is what actually closes this.

3 — the guard: pick exactly one
Illustration — the race this guard closesBooker A: checks [10:30, 11:30) → free Booker B: checks [10:30, 11:30) → free (both pass the check) Booker A: writes the booking Booker B: writes the booking → double-booked, same room, same slot
SERIALIZABLE isolation — optimistic

Run the conditional insert inside a transaction at SERIALIZABLE isolation. Neither booker locks anything upfront — both proceed as if uncontended, and the database catches the real conflict at commit time, aborting one.

  • No explicit locking code — the database enforces it.
  • The loser gets a serialization error and has to retry, which the app must handle.
  • Costs more under real contention.
An explicit lock — pessimistic

Acquire a lock scoped to the room before the check-and-insert, held until the transaction ends — assume contention and prevent it upfront. Two ways to take it, below.

  • Only bookers on the same room ever wait on each other.
  • Simpler to reason about — the second booker just waits its turn.
  • Must be released on every code path, including a crash, or it becomes its own outage.

Why not a Redis lock instead of either of these? Because the lock and the write it protects would live in two separate systems — if the app crashes between acquiring a Redis lock and committing to Postgres, the lock needs a TTL to self-clean, and that TTL becomes its own tuning problem. Both mechanisms above live inside the same transaction as the write — Postgres releases the lock automatically the instant that transaction ends, crash or not, with nothing to tune. Redis earns its place coordinating something that spans more than one database transaction, not for guarding one insert Postgres can already guard for free.

Two ways to take that lock
Advisory lock — an arbitrary token

pg_advisory_xact_lock(room_id) — Postgres-specific. Locks a number that means "room 101" only by convention; works against any schema, needs no existing row to lock.

SELECT ... FOR UPDATE — the actual row

Standard SQL, portable beyond Postgres. Locks a real row in the rooms table — the thing the contention is actually about, not a synthetic hash standing in for it.

Illustration — the advisory lock, traced across two bookersBEGIN; SELECT pg_advisory_xact_lock(:room_id); -- acquire FIRST, before checking INSERT INTO bookings (room_id, start_time, end_time, organizer_id) SELECT :room_id, :start, :end, :organizer_id WHERE NOT EXISTS ( SELECT 1 FROM bookings WHERE room_id = :room_id AND start_time < :end AND end_time > :start ); COMMIT; -- lock releases automatically, tied to the transaction Booker A: BEGIN → lock(101) acquires instantly → check+insert → COMMIT (released) Booker B: BEGIN → lock(101) BLOCKS, waiting — B's query hasn't even run yet ...the moment A commits... B's lock acquires → check+insert now correctly sees A's booking → 0 rows affected → app tells B "someone got it first" B never raced A — B's check didn't start until A's transaction had already finished. That's the actual difference from SERIALIZABLE: the lock prevents the interleaving, rather than catching it afterward. Caveat: pg_advisory_xact_lock takes a bigint, so a UUID/string room_id is hashed down first — a hash collision means two different rooms could occasionally, harmlessly wait on each other.
Illustration — SELECT ... FOR UPDATE, catching a deleted or mistyped room_id for freeBEGIN; SELECT 1 FROM rooms WHERE id = :room_id FOR UPDATE; -- lock the real row -- if that returned 0 rows, room_id doesn't exist — caught right here, -- in the same round trip that acquired the lock. Bail out before ever -- attempting the insert. INSERT INTO bookings (room_id, start_time, end_time, organizer_id) SELECT :room_id, :start, :end, :organizer_id WHERE NOT EXISTS ( SELECT 1 FROM bookings WHERE room_id = :room_id AND start_time < :end AND end_time > :start ); COMMIT; pg_advisory_xact_lock has no such signal — it "succeeds" and grants a lock even for a room_id that was deleted or never existed, since it's locking an arbitrary number, not checking against any row. FOR UPDATE can't grant a lock on a row that isn't there, so the existence check comes free.

Between the two locks, concretely: FOR UPDATE if you need this to run on a non-Postgres database too, or you want a deleted/mistyped room_id caught in the same round trip that acquires the lock, instead of a silent no-op. The advisory lock if what you're protecting isn't tied to one row at all — locking a whole floor for a bulk operation, or a business rule keyed on something that spans tables — where there's no single row to lock against in the first place. A plain UNIQUE constraint on (room_id, start_time) is worth adding either way, as a cheap backstop against the exact-duplicate case — the same room, the same exact start time, submitted twice. It won't catch a genuine partial overlap (10:00–11:00 against 10:30–11:30) the way EXCLUDE does — a narrower guarantee, but one available on databases that don't support range exclusion at all.

Escalation — an in-process tree, on top of either path

Not a third path — an optional speed layer for one genuinely hot room (a popular shared space, an all-hands room), sitting in front of whichever path above is doing the actual guaranteeing.

Illustration — the tree speeds up the check; the path above still does the guaranteeingNew booking request, handled by the Booking Service instance that owns this room: 1. Acquire an in-process lock scoped to this one room_id (a mutex inside THIS process — not a database lock, not Redis) 2. tree.overlaps(start, end) → true/false ← the fast, local check 3. If false: INSERT into the database (still protected by Path A or Path B above, as the real backstop) AND insert into the tree 4. Release the lock The tree makes step 2 fast by skipping a database round trip — it doesn't, by itself, stop two requests on the SAME instance from both reaching step 2 before either finishes step 3. The in-process lock prevents that; the tree just makes the thing being guarded cheap to check. One requirement this creates, easy to miss: if multiple Booking Service instances sit behind a load balancer, this room's bookings must always route to the SAME instance — otherwise each instance holds a different, incomplete view of its calendar, and the in-process lock only protects against other requests on the same instance. The same partition-by-key discipline as the price alert system pinning BBCA to one Detector instance, applied here to a room.

Step 5 — resolve: what the loser actually sees

Every mechanism above produces exactly one outcome for the losing request: a failure. What happens next is a real product decision, not an afterthought — a bare 500 or a generic "booking failed" is a worse experience than the race itself.

Illustration — a specific, actionable failure, not a stack traceBooker B's write fails (constraint violation, or a serialization error): Bad: 500 Internal Server Error Good: 409 Conflict { "reason": "room_already_booked", "conflicting_slot": ["10:30","11:30"] } The response tells B exactly what happened and why — not just that "something" went wrong.

The genuinely good version doesn't stop at explaining the failure — it immediately re-runs step 1's exact anti-join query, scoped to the same time window and filters B already searched with, and returns what's still available. No new mechanism required: the same query that found B a room the first time finds B an alternative the second.

Illustration — closing the loop back to step 1409 response, extended: { "reason": "room_already_booked", "conflicting_slot": ["10:30","11:30"], "alternatives": [ /* step 1's search, re-run automatically */ ] } B never has to manually start over — the system already knows what else is free.
Key deep dive — storing recurring meetings
FRRecurring meetingsFRCancel/modify a single occurrenceNFRNo pre-generating years of instances
Thought process
  1. Consider the simplest option: generate every future occurrence the moment the series is created. Test it against "forever" — a weekly meeting with no end date means either an arbitrary cutoff or unbounded storage.
  2. Consider the opposite extreme: store only the rule, compute occurrences on the fly whenever someone asks. Test it against the common case — now every availability check does that computation, every single time.
  3. Notice neither extreme is right on its own — one wastes storage on meetings nobody will query yet, the other repeats the same computation on every read.
  4. Split the difference deliberately: materialize a bounded near-term window for fast, simple queries, and only compute on demand for anything further out.
  • Materialize on write: generate every instance of the series up front. Simple to query, but wasteful — a weekly meeting "forever" means either an arbitrary cutoff or unbounded storage, and changing the series means rewriting every future instance.
  • Expand on read: store just the rule, compute instances when a range is queried. Cheap to store, but every availability check now does more work, and modifying or canceling a single occurrence needs an "exception" record layered on top of the rule — either "this date is cancelled" or "this date is rescheduled to X" — rather than a row to simply edit or delete.

Most real systems land on a hybrid: materialize a bounded near-term window (say, the next 90 days) for fast queries, and expand on demand beyond that — the same instinct as the crawler's priority scoring, spending effort where it's actually needed.

Key deep dive — time zones
NFRNever double-book
Thought process
  1. State the requirement the way someone would actually phrase it: "book the 2pm slot." Notice that's ambiguous the moment more than one office exists.
  2. Trace what happens if a bare local time gets stored as-is — the overlap check from the earlier deep dive assumes two comparable ranges, and "2pm New York" and "2pm London" aren't comparable at all without a shared reference.
  3. Fix it at the source, not at display: store every timestamp in one absolute format, and only convert for a human to read — never before a comparison runs.
  4. Check for the sharp edge case this doesn't automatically solve: daylight saving shifts a recurring meeting's UTC time twice a year, so the recurrence expansion has to account for that explicitly, not just repeat a raw time-of-day.

"Book the 2pm slot" means something different in every office once a company has more than one, and a design that stores a bare local time will silently double-book or show the wrong availability the moment someone in a different time zone looks at the same room.

Illustration — always store UTC"2pm Monday, New York office" → stored as → 2026-03-02T19:00:00Z "2pm Monday, London office" → stored as → 2026-03-02T14:00:00Z
  • Store every booking's start/end in UTC, always — never let a "local time" be the source of truth. Convert to the viewer's or the room's local time only at display time, never before the overlap check runs.
  • Daylight saving is the sharpest edge case: a recurring "every Monday at 9am" meeting can shift by an hour relative to UTC twice a year, which the Recurring Expander needs to account for when generating instances — not just the raw time-of-day.

The overlap-detection math from the earlier deep dive assumes two comparable ranges — time zone bugs are almost always this assumption quietly breaking, not the comparison itself being wrong.

Does it hold up?

Non-functional requirementHow this design satisfies it
Never double-book a roomAtomic conditional write gated by the overlap check — same pattern as Ticketmaster's seat hold.
Fast overlap checksIndex on (room_id, start_time), or an in-process interval tree (inside the Booking Service, not Redis) for high-traffic rooms.
Searching many rooms stays one queryA single NOT EXISTS anti-join across rooms, filtered by capacity/location — not N separate per-room checks.
Recurring meetings don't require years of pre-generated rowsHybrid: materialize a near-term window, expand on demand beyond it.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
Hybrid materialize-near-term + expand-on-readFast queries for the common case (near-term bookings) without unbounded storage, at the cost of maintaining two code paths and the boundary logic between them.
Anti-join search across all rooms vs. checking rooms one at a timeScales far better as room count grows and stays a single round trip, but needs a solid (room_id, start_time) index to keep the query planner honest — for a large multi-building portfolio, worth partitioning by location so a search doesn't scan rooms nobody's asking about.
In-process interval tree per room vs. database index aloneFaster for very hot rooms — no network call at all, not even to a cache — but requires that room's bookings always route to the same Booking Service instance, or different instances hold different, incomplete views of its calendar. Only worth it once a room's traffic justifies both the complexity and that routing constraint.
SERIALIZABLE isolation vs. an advisory lock per room_idSERIALIZABLE needs no explicit locking code but costs more under real contention and requires the app to handle retry-on-abort; an advisory lock is simpler to reason about (bookers on the same room just wait their turn) but must be released on every code path, including a crash mid-transaction, or it becomes its own outage for that room.
One room per meeting (assumed)A much simpler booking transaction — one row, one check — but doesn't support a meeting that genuinely needs two connected rooms, which would require an atomic multi-row transaction if added.

Tools you might reach for

ComponentReal-world options
Availability CheckerPostgreSQL range types + exclusion constraints — a genuinely exact fit for this problem — or an in-process interval tree inside the Booking Service itself for the rare hot room
Bookings DBPostgreSQL
Worked Example I — Read Scalability at Scale

Search Invoices Across 30+ Products

CQRS · async indexing · federated data, unified reads
Click to expand

"A company has 30+ separate product lines, each with its own invoicing service and database. Users subscribe to a subset of these products — one user might follow 10, another 5 — and should only ever search invoices from products they're actually subscribed to. Build one search box: type something like an invoice number, and see matching invoices grouped by which subscribed product each one belongs to." A pure read-scalability problem, plus a real access-control constraint that has to be baked into the design, not bolted on after.

1
Requirements
Functional
  • One free-text search box — type something like an invoice number or customer name, no separate filter fields required to get a result.
  • Results only ever include products the querying user is subscribed to — a different user with a different subscription set sees different results for the identical query.
  • Products the user previously subscribed to still surface in results — tagged as no-longer-active — rather than disappearing from search the moment access ends.
  • Results are grouped by product — the user sees "Product A: 3 matches, Product C: 1 match," not one flat undifferentiated list.
  • Each product line keeps owning its own invoice writes independently.
Non-functional
  • Search stays fast as product count and invoice volume both grow.
  • Search must not add latency or coupling to the 30+ product services' write paths.
  • A subscription change (gaining or losing access to a product) must take effect immediately, not after some indexing delay.
  • Search results need to be reasonably fresh, not necessarily instant.

Worth asking: should the query match partial or fuzzy invoice numbers (e.g. "2024-0088" matching "INV-2024-0088-A1"), or exact only? We'll assume partial/prefix matching for typed-so-far input — a single search box is only actually useful if the user doesn't have to type the exact full string. Genuine typos (a transposed prefix like "vin-" for "inv-") are a related but separate case — see the Caching Playbook for the fuzzy-fallback approach and why it's kept out of the primary result set.

2
Core Entities
  • Invoice (id, product_id, customer_id, amount, issued_at, status) — owned and written by each product's own service; the source of truth stays scattered on purpose.
  • SearchDocument (invoice fields, denormalized, product_name) — a read-optimized copy living in the search index, carrying enough context to group results by product at display time.
  • Subscription (user_id, product_id, subscribed_at, unsubscribed_at) — the access-control source of truth. A row is closed out on unsubscribe, never deleted, so the system can tell "never subscribed" from "used to be."
  • IndexEvent (invoice_id, product_id, change_type) — emitted whenever an invoice is created or changed, the only thing connecting write side to read side.
3
API

GET /invoices/search?q=INV-2024-0088 → authenticated call, results grouped by product and pre-filtered to the caller's subscribed products. Each product's own invoice CRUD API is unchanged and out of scope — this system only adds a read path, never touches how products write.

Illustration — same query, different usersuser_42 subscribes to: [Product A, Product C, Product K] user_77 subscribes to: [Product A, Product F] q = "INV-2024-0088" (for user_42) result: Product A - INV-2024-0088-A1 Acme Corp $1,240 Product C - INV-2024-0088-C7 Acme Corp $860 Product K - INV-2024-0088-K2 Beta LLC $2,010 q = "INV-2024-0088" (for user_77, same query) result: Product A - INV-2024-0088-A1 Acme Corp $1,240 (Product C and K matches exist, but user_77 never sees them)
4
Data Flow — genuinely earns its place here
  1. Each product's invoice database emits a change event whenever an invoice is created or updated.
  2. Events from all 30+ products land on one shared Event Stream.
  3. An Indexer consumes the stream and writes a denormalized copy — including product name — into the Search Index.
  4. The read path never touches the 30+ source databases — only the one shared index — and separately consults Subscriptions to know which products a given caller may see.
5
High-Level Design

Write side and read side are fully separate systems, connected only by an async event stream — diagrammed below.

6
Deep Dives

Three below: (a) why not query all 30+ databases live, (b) keeping the index fresh without coupling to the source, and (c) enforcing subscriptions on every query.

Write side (30+ independently-owned services) and read side (one shared index) meet only at the event stream — never at query time. Every query is filtered by Subscriptions before results are returned.
← scroll to see the full diagram →
Write path (per product, unchanged)
  1. A product's invoice service creates or updates an invoice in its own database, exactly as it always has.
  2. That change emits an event onto the shared Event Stream — the only new thing each product team has to do.
  3. The product's own service never waits on search, never calls it, never knows it exists.
Read path (search)
  1. Client types a free-text query — e.g. a partial invoice number or customer name.
  2. Search Service checks the Cache for this exact query text — the cache key is the query alone, shared across every user, not per-caller.
  3. On a miss, it queries the Search Index for the raw, unfiltered match set across all 30+ products and writes it to cache — still unfiltered, since the text-match itself doesn't depend on who's asking.
  4. Before returning anything, the Search Service filters that result down to the caller's ever-subscribed products (looked up from Subscriptions, cached separately) and tags each one's status. This filter step runs on every request, whether the query above was a cache hit or a miss.
  5. Client groups the results by product before displaying — one aggregated view of only what this user is allowed to see.
Key deep dive — why not query all 30+ databases live
FRResults grouped by productFRProducts keep owning writesNFRNo coupling to write paths
Thought process
  1. Start with the most direct-sounding idea: fan a search request out to all 30+ product databases and merge whatever comes back.
  2. Trace what happens to latency: the response can only be as fast as the slowest of 30+ independent calls — with that many in flight, something is always slow.
  3. Trace what happens to ownership: each product team now has to build and maintain search capability on their own schema, for a use case they don't actually own.
  4. Realize the fix isn't "make the fan-out faster" — it's not fanning out at query time at all. Separate the write side (30+ databases, unchanged) from a single purpose-built read copy that every search hits instead.

The naive approach — fan a search request out to all 30+ product databases and merge the results — fails for reasons that compound as the product count grows.

  • Tail latency amplification: the response is only as fast as the slowest of 30+ services on that request — with enough independent calls, something is always slow.
  • Permanent coupling: each product team now has to expose and maintain ad hoc search/filter capability on their own schema, for a use case they don't own.
  • Schema fragmentation: 30+ teams' slightly different ideas of "customer" or "amount" turn a simple query into a fragile integration problem, repeated 30+ times.
  • Grouping gets hard too: merging and grouping 30+ separate, differently-shaped result sets into one "Product A / Product B / …" view is real work, done on every single search.

The fix is CQRS: separate the write model (30+ independently-owned databases) from the read model (one denormalized, purpose-built search index). Reads never touch the write side at query time — and since every product's data already lives in the same index with the same shape, grouping by product is a trivial reshape of one result set, not a live merge of 30+.

Key deep dive — keeping the index fresh without coupling to the source
NFRNo coupling to write pathsNFRReasonably fresh resultsNFRSearch stays fast at scale
Thought process
  1. Given the read side is now a separate copy, ask the obvious follow-up: how does it ever find out about a new or changed invoice?
  2. Reject the direct-call option first: if the Indexer called each product service directly, a slow or down Indexer would now be able to block someone else's invoice write — exactly the coupling CQRS was meant to remove.
  3. Decouple the two sides properly: producers publish what happened, a consumer picks it up on its own schedule — neither side waits on the other.
  4. Choose how the "what happened" gets published: either read it straight from each database's own change log (CDC), or have each service explicitly emit an event — both land on one shared stream either way.

Change Data Capture (CDC) reads each product database's write-ahead log directly, or each product service explicitly publishes an event on every invoice change — either way, changes land on one shared event stream that the Indexer consumes to keep the Search Index current.

  • Same idea as a message queue: producers (30+ product services) and the consumer (the Indexer) never call each other directly, so a slow or down Indexer doesn't block anyone's invoice writes.
  • Schema freedom: a product team can change their own internal schema freely, as long as the event contract stays the same.
  • The Cache pays off here too: a small number of queries (a customer's own recent invoices, a common date-range report) repeat often enough that caching them costs little and saves real load on the index — see the Caching Playbook for the free-text-specific strategy (normalization, negative-result caching) and the hot-key handling this design actually needs.
Key deep dive — enforcing subscriptions on every query
FROnly subscribed products in resultsFRLapsed subscriptions still findableNFRSubscription changes take effect immediately
Thought process
  1. Start with the obvious-seeming fix: bake a product_id IN (…) filter into the search query itself, scoped to whatever the caller is subscribed to.
  2. Trace the side effect of that choice: the query's result now depends on who's asking, not just what they typed — two users searching identical text get different results.
  3. Connect that to the caching work already done: a result that differs per user can't be cached once and shared — the key has to become (user, query), which is exactly the key-space explosion already ruled out.
  4. Move the filter instead of removing it: let the text-match stay caller-blind and shareable, then apply the subscription filter to that (usually small) result afterward, on every single request, cache hit or miss.

Different users subscribe to different subsets of the 30+ products, a subscription change has to take effect immediately, and losing access to a product shouldn't erase the ability to find invoices from when access was active — all while the search cache itself has to stay shared, not per-user, or it collapses under real traffic (see the Caching Playbook).

  • Filter after the fetch, not baked into the query — on purpose: the obvious-seeming alternative is a product_id IN (…) clause baked into the search query itself. That works, but it makes the query's result a function of who's asking, not just what they typed — two users searching identical text get different results, so the cache key has to become (user, query), and that's exactly the key-space explosion the Caching Playbook walks through. Instead, the text-match query never mentions the caller, so it's cacheable once and shared across everyone searching similar text — and the Search Service filters that (usually small) result down to this caller's ever-subscribed products afterward, on every request, cache hit or miss, nothing skipped.
  • Tag status after filtering, not instead of it: for each product that survives the filter, a null unsubscribed_at means "Active"; a set one means "Subscription ended [date]." The tag is a label on an already-permitted result, not a second access check.
  • Cache the subscription history separately, keyed by user: it changes rarely relative to how often a user searches, so this specific lookup — user_id to product list — is cached (Redis, short TTL). This is the one place a user_id belongs in a cache key here; the search-result cache stays query-only and shared, precisely so it doesn't multiply by user count the way this smaller lookup safely can.
Illustration — lapsed subscription, still findable, clearly taggeduser_42's subscriptions: Product A — subscribed 2025-11-01, unsubscribed 2026-01-15 (lapsed) Product B — subscribed 2025-11-01, active Product C — subscribed 2026-01-01, active q = "INV-2024-0088" result: Product B [Active] - INV-2024-0088-B3 Acme Corp $980 Product C [Active] - INV-2024-0088-C7 Acme Corp $860 Product A [Subscription ended 2026-01-15] - INV-2024-0088-A1 Acme Corp $1,240

This is a deliberate product decision, not a default — "unsubscribe" here means "stop seeing new activity from," not "revoke my own history." A system where that distinction matters for compliance or security should make it explicit, not inherit whichever reading was easiest to build.

Does it hold up?

Non-functional requirementHow this design satisfies it
Read scalability as products growNew products just emit events into the same stream — the query path and index schema don't change shape as product count grows.
Search stays fastOne denormalized, purpose-built index instead of live scatter-gather across 30+ heterogeneous databases.
No coupling to 30+ write-side schemasThe event contract is the only integration point — product teams keep full ownership of their own database internals.
Reasonable freshnessAsync indexing lag measured in seconds, matching the stated requirement rather than over-engineering real-time sync.
Aggregated, grouped resultsEvery product's invoices already share one schema in one index — grouping by product is a reshape of one result set, not a live merge of 30+.
Subscription changes take effect immediatelyQuery-time filtering against a cached (short-TTL) subscription lookup — no reindex needed for access to change.
Historical access preserved, clearly distinguishedSubscription rows are closed out (unsubscribed_at set), never deleted — past access stays searchable and tagged separately from active access.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
CQRS (separate read/write models) vs. one shared databaseThe read side scales independently with zero coupling to 30+ write schemas, but search results are now eventually consistent — lagging real invoices by seconds — and there are two systems to operate instead of one.
CDC vs. application-level explicit eventsCDC requires no code change from product teams, but needs infrastructure access to each database's write-ahead log; explicit events are simpler to reason about, but every product team must remember to emit them correctly.
One shared search index vs. per-product indices + a federated query layerOne schema and trivial cross-product grouping, at the cost of a bigger blast radius — the whole search experience degrades if that one index has a problem, and a schema change affects every product's data at once.
Query-time subscription filtering vs. index-time ACL taggingQuery-time makes access changes effective immediately with no reindex, at the cost of an extra (cached) lookup on every search; baking permissions into each document at index time avoids that lookup, but a user who just lost access keeps seeing results until the affected documents are reindexed.
Surfacing lapsed subscriptions (tagged) vs. a hard cutoff on unsubscribePreserves a user's ability to reference their own history, at the cost of a broader exposure surface over time — "unsubscribed" no longer means "invisible," which has to be an explicit choice for any system where losing access is meant to fully revoke visibility.

Tools you might reach for

Event StreamKafka
Indexera Kafka consumer service, or Kafka Connect
Search IndexElasticsearch, OpenSearch

SubscriptionsPostgreSQL as source of truth, Redis for the cached user_id → product_ids lookup
Worked Example J — Fan-out at Uneven Scale

Design a News Feed

fan-out-on-write vs. fan-out-on-read · the celebrity problem
Click to expand
