Load every Active alert for this ticker from the durable User Alert Index — the actual source of truth, since the tree itself never was. 2. Rebuild the tree from that list. 3. Only then resume consuming ticks from this ticker's Kafka partition. Consuming ticks before step 2 finishes means querying an incomplete tree — a real crossing could be missed. The safe choice is a brief pause in detection for that one ticker during rebuild, not silently detecting against partial data.
Illustration — option 4: why one tick can span more than one price-band shardShards for BBCA, split by price band: bbca_alerts:0 → thresholds 0–3,000 bbca_alerts:1 → thresholds 3,000–6,000 bbca_alerts:2 → thresholds 6,000–9,000 ... Tick: price moves 2,990 → 3,120 — crosses the boundary between band 0 and band 1. Unlike ticker-sharding (a BBCA tick only ever touches BBCA's own shards), this single tick's range spans TWO price-band shards — the query has to check both and merge results, not query one shard and be done. A tick landing near a boundary is routine, not an edge case, so this multi-shard query is the common path, not a rare one. The bands aren't static either: if alerts cluster heavily around one round number, that band's own shard can go hot again — the same problem, one level down, needing its own re-split.
The actual fork: option 2 vs. option 3
Option 2 — Lua script on Redis

The alert data lives in Redis, reached over the network — but the query and every removal run as one atomic script.

  • Survives a Detector crash outright — the data lives in Redis, not in the process that died.
  • Every tick pays a small network round trip (~0.1–1ms) to reach Redis.
  • The safe default for essentially every ticker, hot or not.
Option 3 — in-process tree

The alert data lives inside the Detector's own memory — no network call, but nothing survives if that process dies.

  • A tick becomes a local function call — no network round trip at all.
  • A crash means the tree is gone — requires the load → rebuild → resume sequence above before it's safe to consume ticks again.
  • Only worth it once a ticker is provably hot enough that the network round trip itself matters.

Recommendation: option 2 as the default for every ticker, hot or cold — it fixes a genuine correctness gap in option 1 for free, using infrastructure already in place, so there's no real reason to skip it. Option 3 earns its added complexity only for tickers provably hot enough that the network round trip itself eats into the sub-second budget — and only alongside the crash-safe rebuild sequence above, since a stateful in-memory structure with no persistence isn't a free upgrade, it's a new failure mode to design for. Option 4 is worth knowing about, not worth building until a real ticker's alert count actually forces the question — the multi-shard query it introduces is real added complexity for what starts out solving a problem most tickers never have.

Key deep dive — partitioning the market feed so one Detector doesn't see every tick
NFRSub-second detectionNFRNo missed crossingsNFRScales to thousands of tickers
Thought process
  1. Notice everything in the deep dive above assumed a tick simply "arrives" at the Detector — worth checking what actually delivers it, especially with hundreds of liquid tickers all ticking through market hours at once.
  2. Consider one Detector process handling every tick for every ticker. Test it against real volume: that single process now has to keep every ticker's Threshold Index warm and reachable, and process a genuinely continuous, high-throughput stream alone — the exact bottleneck this whole design exists to avoid, just moved one step earlier.
  3. Consider the opposite: spread ticks across many Detector instances arbitrarily (round-robin). Test it against correctness: BBCA's own ticks could now land on different instances out of order, and the range query (old_price → new_price) depends on knowing the true previous price — an instance that missed the last tick has no reliable "old_price" to query from.
  4. Fix both problems with one choice: partition the stream by ticker symbol. Every tick for a given ticker always routes to the same partition, in the order it happened — that gives correctness (true order) and locality (one instance can keep that ticker's index warm) for free.

A price tick doesn't just "arrive" — with hundreds of liquid tickers ticking through market hours simultaneously, something has to decide which Detector instance handles which tick, and get the ordering right while doing it.

Illustration — partition by ticker, not round-robinKafka topic "price-ticks", key = ticker symbol Round-robin (wrong): BBCA's ticks scatter across partitions 0, 3, 1, 2, 0, 3... → no single Detector instance sees BBCA's ticks in order → "old_price" for the next tick is unreliable, range query can be wrong Partition-by-ticker (correct): key="BBCA" always hashes to the same partition, say partition 2 → every BBCA tick lands on partition 2, in the exact order the exchange emitted it → the Detector instance consuming partition 2 always has the true old_price → and can keep BBCA's Threshold Index shards warm, since it's the only instance touching them Is one consumer actually enough for BBCA's own volume? Check the numbers, don't assume: even an extremely liquid stock ticking every 50–100ms ≈ 10–20 ticks/sec one Kafka partition/consumer sustains tens of thousands of small messages/sec → BBCA's raw tick rate is 3–4 orders of magnitude below that ceiling — nowhere close to a bottleneck
  • Order is a correctness requirement here, not a nicety: Kafka guarantees order only within a partition, which is exactly why the key has to be the ticker symbol — anything else risks the Detector computing a crossing against the wrong "old_price."
  • A hot ticker needs isolation, not necessarily more consumers: hashing alone can still place BBCA on a partition shared with several moderately active tickers — a genuine problem, since one consumer now serves the combined tick rate of all of them. Pinning BBCA to its own partition fixes that, and one consumer is enough to keep up with a single ticker's actual volume; ingestion throughput was never the scarce resource here.
  • Don't confuse this with the Threshold Index's sharding — that bottleneck is real, this one usually isn't: a tick arriving is a tiny message; the range query it triggers against hundreds of thousands of alerts is genuinely expensive CPU/memory work. That's why the Threshold Index gets sharded across multiple Redis keys — the query workload, not the raw ingestion rate, is where a hot ticker's cost actually lives.

This is what actually makes the main diagram's single "Ingestion → Detector" arrow honest — not every tick funneling through one process, and not one dedicated instance per hot ticker either. It's a shared pool of Detector instances, each consuming one or more partitions, with known-hot tickers isolated onto their own partition specifically so their ticks never queue up behind a noisy neighbor's.

How "only one instance reads BBCA" is actually guaranteed

Not something the Detector's own code has to enforce — it's a built-in Kafka guarantee, in two parts: routing (which partition a tick lands on) and exclusivity (who's allowed to read that partition).

Illustration — BBCA, BBRI, BMRI, BBNI, routed and assignedPart 1 — routing (which partition does each ticker's ticks land on): key="BBCA" → pinned → partition 2 (dedicated — BBCA is known-hot) key="BBRI" → hash("BBRI") % 32 → partition 9 key="BMRI" → hash("BMRI") % 32 → partition 17 key="BBNI" → hash("BBNI") % 32 → partition 9 (shares a partition with BBRI — fine, neither is hot enough to need isolation) Part 2 — exclusivity (consumer group "detectors" — Kafka's own broker-side guarantee): partition 2 → Detector instance A, exclusively (BBCA's ticks — nothing else) partition 9 → Detector instance B, exclusively (BBRI + BBNI's ticks, in arrival order) partition 17 → Detector instance C, exclusively (BMRI's ticks) Kafka's group coordinator enforces this — two instances can never both be assigned partition 2 at the same time. Instance A never even sees a BBRI or BMRI tick; they were never routed to its partition in the first place.
  • Instance A doesn't filter out other tickers — it never receives them: exclusivity isn't a check inside the Detector's code ("is this BBCA? ignore it if not") — it's that BBRI, BMRI, and BBNI's ticks are routed to different partitions entirely, before any Detector ever sees them.
  • If instance A crashes, Kafka rebalances automatically: a missed heartbeat triggers reassignment of partition 2 to a different living instance — which starts with an empty local state and has to run the load → rebuild → resume sequence from the range-query deep dive above before it's safe to consume partition 2 again.
Key deep dive — decoupling detection from push delivery
FRPush notification on triggerNFRSub-second detectionNFROffline delivery, bounded staleness
Thought process
  1. Consider the simplest possible pairing: the moment an alert crosses, look up the user's device and send the push right there, in the same step as detection.
  2. Trace what a slow or unreachable push provider does to that: the Detector is now waiting on a network call to a third party before it can move on to the next tick — the exact bottleneck this system exists to avoid.
  3. Separate the two concerns explicitly: detection's job is "identify the crossing, fast" — delivery's job is "get the notification to the device, however long that takes" — and neither should block on the other.
  4. Once they're separate, decide the harder question: does marking the alert Triggered happen before or after delivery is confirmed? If before, a failed push means the alert is gone and the user never finds out. If after, a crash mid-delivery could leave a dangling Triggered-but-undelivered alert. Pick a default and be explicit about the failure mode either way.

The same "don't let the slow path block the fast path" instinct as the URL shortener's click analytics and Ticketmaster's confirmation email — except here the slow path is the entire point of the feature, which makes getting the failure mode right matter more, not less.

  • What "simple in-app push" actually means, mechanically: each device registers a push token with Apple (APNs) or Google (FCM) once, at install or login — that token is stored in DeviceToken Store as (user_id → token, platform). Delivering a notification means one API call to APNs/FCM with that token and the message; the provider handles finding and waking the actual device. This system never holds a live connection to anyone — that's the provider's job, not ours.
  • Kafka's role here is decoupling, not sharding: the Detector publishes a small "alert X triggered" message to a Kafka topic and immediately moves to the next tick — it never calls the Delivery Worker directly. Kafka's own internal partitioning (splitting the topic so multiple Delivery Worker instances can each consume a slice in parallel) is what lets delivery scale its own throughput independently — a different mechanism from sharding the Threshold Index, solving a different problem (parallel consumers, not one hot key's write throughput).
  • Deactivate at trigger time, retry delivery independently: the Alert is marked Triggered the moment the crossing is detected — that's the correctness guarantee ("fires exactly once"). Delivery is retried against the Kafka topic on its own schedule, decoupled from whether the alert itself is still Active; a failed push doesn't get a second detection, only a second delivery attempt.
  • Bounded staleness needs an actual mechanism, not just a stated limit: unlike a chat app, there's no "open the conversation and see what you missed." Each trigger event carries its own timestamp; the Delivery Worker checks it before every retry and simply drops the attempt once it's older than the bound (minutes, not hours) — the same short-TTL instinct as a cache entry expiring, applied to a queued notification instead of a stored value. If APNs/FCM reports the device unreachable and the window has already passed, it doesn't retry at all.

"Fired" and "delivered" are tracked as two different facts about the same alert — worth stating explicitly, since conflating them is exactly how a system ends up either double-firing on retry or silently losing a notification on failure.

Key deep dive — hot tickers and illiquid tickers need opposite treatments
NFRScales to thousands of tickersNFRSub-second, when a tick occurs
Thought process
  1. Notice the design so far quietly assumed every ticker behaves the same: ticks constantly, has a meaningfully sized alert index, needs to be fast at all times.
  2. Check that against thousands of real tickers: a handful trade every few hundred milliseconds with huge alert counts; most trade rarely, some go days between trades, with a handful of alerts each.
  3. Realize treating them identically wastes resources in both directions — a popular ticker's single index becomes a bottleneck under real load; an illiquid ticker's always-warm, always-sharded index costs the same as a hot one for almost no benefit.
  4. Split the strategy by actual behavior, not by a fixed list: let recent activity — not a static classification — decide which tier a ticker is in right now, and let it move between tiers as its trading pattern changes.

Thousands of tickers don't share one load profile — a fixed strategy tuned for either extreme wastes resources on the other. The fix is treating "how active is this ticker right now" as the thing that decides the strategy, not the ticker's identity.

Hot ticker (BBCA, ticks sub-second)

The bottleneck is throughput on a single ticker's own index — sharding spreads that one ticker's load, the same key-splitting instinct as the Caching Playbook's hot-key section.

  • Threshold Index is split into multiple shards for this ticker specifically; a tick queries all shards for that ticker in parallel and merges the matches.
  • A popular round-number threshold (3,000 on a major stock) can hold thousands of alerts — one crossing fans out that many trigger events at once, the same shape as the News Feed's celebrity fan-out, just aimed at a price level instead of a follower graph.
  • Kept always in memory — the cost is justified by the tick rate.
Illiquid ticker (barely trades)

There's no throughput problem to solve — the risk is the opposite one: paying hot-path memory cost for thousands of tickers that rarely justify it.

  • Its alert index lives in a cheaper, colder tier by default — not sharded, not necessarily in memory.
  • The sub-second NFR simply doesn't apply while there's no tick to measure latency from — worth stating explicitly rather than treating a dormant ticker as a violation.
  • On the rare tick, the ticker is promoted to the hot tier, its index warmed, and demoted back after a period of inactivity — activity decides the tier, not a fixed list.

Neither tier is the "correct" default for every ticker — the actual design decision is the promotion/demotion rule itself, since getting that threshold wrong either wastes memory on dormant tickers or adds a cold-start delay right when an illiquid ticker finally starts moving.

Key deep dive — the User Alert Index at 10–20M users
FRView and cancel own alertsNFRFast indexed lookup, never a scanNFRScales to 10–20M users
Thought process
  1. Notice this scale axis has gone unaddressed so far: every other deep dive scales with ticker count or tick rate — this one scales with user count, a completely different number, and it deserves its own check, not a free pass because "keyed by user_id" sounds like enough on its own.
  2. Estimate the real size: 10–20M users, a few alerts each, lands around 40–60M rows. Ask whether a plain B-tree index on user_id still holds up at that count.
  3. It does, for a reason worth stating rather than assuming: a B-tree index's lookup cost grows logarithmically with row count, so going from 1M to 60M rows adds a small, bounded number of extra comparisons — nothing close to the linear cost of a scan.
  4. Identify the one thing that actually could go wrong at this scale, and it isn't the index — it's a single unsharded PostgreSQL table absorbing 10-20M users' worth of concurrent connections and writes. That's a standard, well-understood scaling problem (read replicas, then partitioning by user_id range), not a novel one this design has to solve from scratch.

"Keyed by user_id" is repeated a few times in this design without ever being defended — worth checking it actually holds at 10–20M users rather than trusting the phrase on its own.

  • The index itself is not the bottleneck: a B-tree lookup on user_id is O(log n) — at 40–60M rows that's roughly 26 comparisons versus roughly 20 at 1M rows, an immaterial difference. This NFR was never actually at risk from row count alone.
  • The real scaling lever is standard relational-database practice, not something specific to this design: read replicas absorb GET /alerts traffic (reads dominate writes here — most users check their alerts far more often than they set or cancel one), and the table can be partitioned by a hash of user_id if a single primary node's write throughput ever becomes the actual constraint.
  • Why this doesn't need the Threshold Index's sharding treatment: that index gets hammered by every price tick across every alert on a ticker — write-heavy, latency-critical, sub-second. This one is read-mostly, and "fast" here means "a normal indexed query," not "sub-second under continuous write pressure" — a materially easier bar.

Not every NFR needs a novel mechanism — sometimes the honest answer is "a well-indexed table already clears this bar," and saying so explicitly is more useful than silence that reads as an oversight.

Does it hold up?

RequirementHow
No missed crossings, including price gapsDetection is a range query (thresholds between old and new price), not an equality check against the new price alone.
Sub-second detection on active tickersA sorted, per-ticker threshold index answers the range query directly; hot tickers are sharded so one ticker's tick rate never bottlenecks on one structure.
Scales to thousands of tickers with wildly uneven activityTier by recent activity, not by fixed ticker identity — hot tickers stay warm and sharded, illiquid ones live cold until they start moving.
Listing/canceling a user's alerts stays fastA separate index keyed by user_id, maintained on every write alongside the detection index — never a scan.
Offline delivery doesn't silently vanish, but doesn't stay relevant forever eitherTrigger and delivery are tracked as separate facts; undelivered triggers are retried within a bounded staleness window, not indefinitely.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
Range-query detection vs. per-tick equality checkCorrect even when price gaps across a threshold, at the cost of needing a sorted, indexable structure per ticker instead of a simple list — more upfront design, but the equality check isn't actually a cheaper correct alternative, it's a faster wrong one.
Lua script (atomic Redis) vs. in-process tree (no network call)The Lua script fixes a real atomicity gap for free, on infrastructure already in place — the right default everywhere. An in-process tree removes the network round trip entirely, which only matters once a ticker's tick rate makes that round trip a meaningful share of the latency budget; applying it by default means every Detector instance carries bespoke in-memory state most tickers never justify.
Deactivate at detection time vs. deactivate only after confirmed deliveryDetection-time deactivation guarantees "fires exactly once" cleanly, at the cost of needing separate tracking and retry logic for delivery, so a failed push doesn't silently mean the user never finds out.
Activity-based tiering vs. one uniform strategy for every tickerAvoids wasting hot-path resources on thousands of dormant tickers, at the cost of promotion/demotion logic and a cold-start delay the first time a long-quiet ticker starts moving again.

Tools you might reach for — and what each one is actually doing

Ingestion — Kafka, partitioned by ticker symbol

A separate topic (e.g. price-ticks) from the Trigger stream below — same technology, deliberately different partitioning strategy, for a different reason.

  • Its actual job: take the raw, continuous market data firehose and split it so no single Detector instance has to process every tick for every ticker — while guaranteeing each ticker's own ticks stay in order.
  • Why partitioned by ticker specifically, not round-robin: Kafka guarantees order only within a partition. Keying by ticker symbol means BBCA's ticks always land on the same partition in the order they happened — which the Detector's range query depends on, since it needs a reliable "old_price" for every new tick.
  • A known-hot ticker gets a dedicated partition, assigned deliberately, not left to hashing: even with partition-by-ticker, a hash could still place BBCA on a partition shared with several other active tickers — pinning it explicitly is what actually isolates its load.

Threshold Index (hot tier) — Redis Sorted Sets

One ZSET per hot ticker (e.g. bbca_alerts), split into shards (bbca_alerts:0…bbca_alerts:7) once one ticker's alert count justifies it.

  • Its actual job: answer "which alert IDs have a score (threshold) between X and Y" in O(log n + k) — internally a skip list, which is precisely the structure that makes a range query cheap instead of a scan.
  • Accessed via a Lua script (EVAL), not separate ZRANGEBYSCORE/ZREM calls: the query and every resulting removal run as one atomic step on the server, closing the gap where another operation could otherwise land between "find the matches" and "remove them."
  • Why sharded, not one key: Redis executes one command at a time per key. A single popular ticker's ZSET becomes a serialization point under real tick volume — splitting it lets a tick fan out to N shards in parallel and merge results, the identical key-splitting instinct from the Caching Playbook's hot-key section, applied to a sorted set instead of a cache entry.
  • What it does NOT do: it isn't durable storage — a Redis restart without persistence configured loses it. The alert's true state (Active/Triggered) lives in a durable store; this structure exists purely to make detection fast, and can be rebuilt from that source of truth if lost.

Threshold Index (cold tier) — PostgreSQL

A B-tree index on (ticker, threshold) for illiquid tickers that don't justify memory-resident, sharded storage.

  • Its actual job: the same range query as the hot tier (WHERE ticker = ? AND threshold BETWEEN ? AND ?), answered by a standard B-tree index — slower than a Redis skip list, but the ticker isn't ticking often enough for that gap to matter.
  • Why not just use Redis for everything: keeping thousands of rarely-active tickers' alerts warm in memory, sharded, costs real infrastructure for close to zero benefit — this tier is the deliberately cheaper default, promoted to the hot tier only on actual trading activity.

Trigger stream — Kafka

A second, separate topic (e.g. alert-triggers) from the Ingestion stream above — that the Detector publishes to and the Delivery Worker consumes from.

  • Its actual job: decouple detection from delivery — the Detector's only interaction with Kafka here is a fire-and-forget publish, so a slow or unreachable push provider can never add latency to the next price tick's detection.
  • Partitioned for consumer parallelism, not ordering: unlike Ingestion above (which must preserve per-ticker order), this topic's partitioning just needs to let multiple Delivery Worker instances each consume a slice concurrently — a genuinely different requirement from Ingestion's, even though both get loosely called "sharding" in conversation.
  • Why a queue and not a direct call: the Detector calling the Delivery Worker synchronously would reintroduce exactly the coupling this design exists to avoid — a queue lets each side fail, retry, and scale independently.

Push delivery — APNs / FCM

Apple's and Google's own push notification services — this system calls them, it doesn't reimplement what they do.

  • Their actual job: given a device's registered push token and a message payload, find and wake that specific device, even if it's asleep or the app isn't running — infrastructure this system deliberately doesn't build itself.
  • What this system is responsible for instead: storing the (user_id → push_token) mapping in DeviceToken Store, calling the provider's API with the right token, and handling the provider's response (delivered, token invalid, device unreachable) to decide whether to retry.
  • Why no connection registry is needed here: unlike a system that holds its own live WebSocket connections, a push token isn't a live connection to track — it's a stable identifier the provider resolves on their end. That's the whole reason this design is simpler than a system built around persistent server-held connections.

User Alert Index — PostgreSQL

A standard table with a B-tree index on user_id — this is the durable source of truth for every Alert, not just a read-optimized copy.

  • Its actual job: serve GET /alerts as a fast, indexed lookup, and hold the authoritative status (Active/Triggered) for every alert — the Threshold Index is a performance optimization built on top of this, not a replacement for it.
  • At 10-20M users: the index itself stays cheap (logarithmic lookup cost), so the real scaling levers are ordinary — read replicas for GET /alerts traffic, and partitioning by a hash of user_id only if write throughput on the primary ever becomes the actual constraint.
Worked Example P — One Hot Resource Becomes Many

Design a Flash Sale System

per-SKU admission · adaptive hold expiry · randomized anti-bot admission
Click to expand

"Multiple hyped products go on sale at the exact same announced instant. Each has severely limited inventory relative to demand — a popular sneaker might be 4,000 buyers for every unit; a less-hyped accessory might be 50:1. A buyer who wins a unit gets a short window to complete checkout; if they don't, it releases back to everyone else waiting." Ticketmaster already solved "many buyers, one hot resource, someone loses" — this is the same race, but with dozens of independently hot resources spiking at the identical instant, an idle hold that's ruinously expensive at these ratios, and an adversary organized enough to time itself to the second.

1
Requirements
Functional
  • Multiple SKUs go live simultaneously at one announced instant, each with independent, limited inventory.
  • A buyer joins a per-product queue; admission is randomized among current arrivals, not strictly speed-ordered.
  • Winning admission reserves one specific unit and starts a checkout window; payment is confirmed asynchronously (charge, then a provider webhook) — not a synchronous hold on the request.
  • An abandoned or expired reservation releases its unit back to the pool automatically.
  • A buyer sees clear status at every stage: queued (with position), admitted (with time remaining), purchased, or sold out.
Non-functional
  • Never oversell a single unit of any product, even at the instant-spike traffic this event is built for.
  • Each product's admission rate is calibrated to its own demand — a 4,000:1 product and a 50:1 product never share one undifferentiated queue.
  • The checkout window adapts to how contended that specific product is — an idle hold is far more expensive at 4,000:1 than at 50:1.
  • Resistant to pre-sale connection priming — bots opening connections before the announced instant specifically to win on reaction time alone.
  • One shared ceiling protects downstream infrastructure (inventory database, payment service) even if many products spike in the identical instant.

Worth asking: can a buyer queue for multiple products at once, or one at a time? We'll assume multiple — queues are per-product and independent, so nothing stops a buyer holding a queue position in several simultaneously.

2
Core Entities
  • Product (id, inventory_count, price, sale_start_time, sale_end_time) — one SKU's sale window and stated inventory.
  • InventoryUnit (product_id, unit_id, status) — status is Available, Reserved, or Sold. A specific unit gets claimed, never just a decremented counter — the same lesson as Ticketmaster's seat rows.
  • Reservation (id, product_id, unit_id, user_id, expires_at, status) — status is Active, Purchased, or Expired; the hold, kept separate from the payment itself.
  • Purchase (id, reservation_id, product_id, user_id, status, created_at) — status is Pending, Complete, or Failed. Confirmed asynchronously by a payment provider webhook, not a synchronous response.
3
API

POST /products/{id}/queue → joins that product's queue, returns a queue token
GET /queue/{token}/status → queued (position) / admitted (reservation + time remaining) / expired
POST /reservations/{id}/checkout → fires the charge, does not block on payment confirmation
POST /webhooks/payment → the provider's async confirmation, completes or fails the Purchase

4
Data Flow — genuinely earns its place here
  1. A buyer joins a product's queue; a bot-defense check runs first, ahead of the queue itself, flagging connections primed suspiciously early relative to the announced instant.
  2. Admission for that product is randomized among current arrivals, not first-come-first-served — calibrated to that product's own demand, independent of every other product's queue.
  3. Admission claims one specific InventoryUnit and creates a Reservation with an expiry scaled to that product's contention ratio.
  4. Checkout fires a charge to the payment provider and returns immediately; the provider's webhook, arriving later, is what actually completes the Purchase.
  5. An expired, unpurchased Reservation releases its InventoryUnit back to Available, where the next round of admission can claim it.
5
High-Level Design

One shared queueing infrastructure hosts many independent, per-product admission rates — not one undifferentiated line, and not a separate deployment per product either — diagrammed below.

6
Deep Dives

Three below: (a) per-SKU admission on one shared infrastructure, (b) adaptive hold expiry, scaled to contention, and (c) randomized admission as the actual anti-bot mechanism.

One Redis-backed Waiting Room, keyed per product — the infrastructure is shared, but each product's admission rate is independent, calibrated to its own demand. Bot Defense sits ahead of the queue, not inside it, and payment is confirmed asynchronously so a slow card charge never holds a request open.
A buyer wins a unit
  1. The buyer's queue-join request passes Bot Defense first — flagged connections never reach the queue at all.
  2. The Waiting Room admits from that product's own queue, at that product's own calibrated rate, independent of every other product's queue.
  3. Admission triggers a conditional claim on one specific InventoryUnit and creates a Reservation with an expiry scaled to that product's contention.
  4. Checkout fires a charge to the Payment Provider and returns immediately — the request never blocks on the card actually clearing.
A hold expires unused
  1. The buyer is admitted but never completes checkout — distracted, changed their mind, or the tab timed out.
  2. The Reservation's adaptive expiry elapses; a sweep (or a lazy check on next access) marks it Expired.
  3. The InventoryUnit releases back to Available, visible to the next round of admission from that same product's queue.
  4. The next admitted buyer for that product claims it — no manual intervention, no stuck inventory.
Key deep dive — per-SKU admission on one shared infrastructure
FRMultiple SKUs, simultaneousNFREach product's own admission rateNFRShared ceiling for downstream infra
Thought process
  1. Start from Ticketmaster's waiting room: one event, one queue, one admission rate calibrated to that event's own backend capacity.
  2. Add a second product to the sale. Test the naive extension: one shared queue for both. A 4,000:1 product and a 50:1 product now compete for the same admission slots — one starves, or the other wastes them.
  3. Consider the opposite extreme: a fully separate queue deployment per product. Test it against a sale with dozens of SKUs — that's dozens of things to provision, monitor, and scale independently, for a problem that's really about admission *rate*, not separate infrastructure.
  4. Separate the two concerns: one shared queueing technology, but a distinct key and a distinct admission rate per product — infrastructure reuse without rate coupling.
  5. Notice a residual risk even with per-product rates: if every product spikes in the same instant, the sum across all of them can still overwhelm something they all share — the database, the payment provider. Add one global ceiling as a backstop, not the primary mechanism.

A shared queue with one admission rate either starves a hyped product or wastes slots on an unpopular one; a fully separate deployment per product is infrastructure sprawl for what's fundamentally a rate problem, not an isolation problem.

Illustration — one Redis, independent keys and rateswaiting_room:sneaker_x → admits 50/sec (4,000:1 demand, deliberately slow) waiting_room:charger_y → admits 400/sec (50:1 demand, far less contended) Both keys live in the same Redis cluster — no separate infrastructure per product. Only the admission RATE differs, tuned to each product's own demand, not a shared average across the whole sale. Global ceiling, checked independently of either key: total_active_checkouts < MAX_CONCURRENT_CHECKOUTS → protects the database and payment provider even if sneaker_x AND charger_y both spike at the exact same instant.
  • The per-product rate is the real fix — calibrated to that product's own inventory and expected demand, not derived from a shared pool that has to be split ahead of time.
  • The global ceiling is a backstop, not the primary mechanism — it exists specifically for the case no per-product rate alone can catch: many independently well-managed queues admitting into the same downstream systems at once.

Same two-layer instinct as the rate limiter's per-client limit plus a system-wide backstop — a per-key rate handles the common case, one shared ceiling catches the case where many well-behaved keys collide on a resource none of them individually knows about.

Key deep dive — adaptive hold expiry
NFRExpiry scales with contention
Thought process
  1. Start from Ticketmaster's fixed checkout window — a flat, say, 10-minute hold, the same for every seat regardless of demand.
  2. Compute what a flat window actually costs at flash-sale ratios: a 4,000:1 product with a 10-minute window means an idle, abandoned hold locks out roughly 4,000 waiting buyers for the full 10 minutes, every time someone doesn't complete checkout.
  3. Compare that to a 50:1 product with the identical window: the same 10 minutes idle costs 50 waiting buyers their turn, not 4,000 — the flat window is wildly miscalibrated for one of the two.
  4. Fix it directly: make the window a function of contention, not a constant — the more people waiting behind a given hold, the more expensive that hold's idle time is, so the shorter it should be.

A flat checkout window, copied from Ticketmaster, is calibrated for one contention ratio and wrong for every other one — and a flash sale runs many different ratios simultaneously, in the same event.

Illustration — expiry as a function of contention, not a constantexpiry_seconds = base_window / max(1, queue_depth / inventory_remaining) sneaker_x: queue_depth=4,000 inventory_remaining=1 → ratio 4,000 → ~3 sec window charger_y: queue_depth=50 inventory_remaining=1 → ratio 50 → ~2 min window Same base_window, same formula — the ratio between waiting buyers and remaining units is what actually drives how expensive an idle hold is, and the expiry shrinks accordingly.
  • The window has to stay usable, not just short: a 3-second checkout window is only fair if the buyer already has payment details saved and ready — worth pairing with a UI that pre-fills or one-clicks checkout specifically for high-contention products, or the "fix" just moves the unfairness from admission to checkout.
  • Recompute per reservation, not once per sale: a product's contention ratio shifts as inventory depletes and the queue drains — the formula reads current queue_depth and inventory_remaining at the moment each reservation is created, not a value fixed at sale start.
Key deep dive — randomized admission as the actual anti-bot mechanism
NFRResistant to pre-sale connection priming
Thought process
  1. Start from Ticketmaster's waiting room: admission is roughly first-come-first-served, throttled to a sustainable rate. Ask what that ordering rewards.
  2. Trace the answer honestly: strict speed-ordering rewards whoever arrives first — and a script that opens a connection microseconds after the announced instant will always beat a human clicking a button. The ordering itself is the vulnerability.
  3. Consider Ticketmaster's usual defenses (CAPTCHA, per-account limits, rate limiting) layered on top of speed-ordering. Notice they mitigate volume, not the core unfairness — a single well-timed script still reliably wins over a human, every time, even with those in place.
  4. Remove the advantage at its source: stop ordering by arrival at all. Admit randomly from everyone who arrived within a short window, so being fastest stops being the thing that wins.
  5. Add the piece specific to a flash sale's known-instant timing: detect connections opened suspiciously early, before the announced start, since that's a stronger bot signal here than in Ticketmaster's less precisely-timed on-sales.

Admitting strictly by arrival order rewards whoever's fastest — and against a timed, scripted adversary, that's never the human. The fix isn't a better speed check; it's removing speed as the advantage entirely.

Illustration — a lottery window, not a finish lineSale announced for 12:00:00.000. Arrivals in [12:00:00.000, 12:00:00.500] — a 500ms window: 1,200 requests arrive, scripts and humans mixed together Speed-ordered admission: the fastest 50 win — overwhelmingly scripts, since a script's reaction time beats any human's by orders of magnitude. Randomized admission: 50 winners drawn uniformly at random from all 1,200 arrivals in the window — a script's only edge is being IN the window at all, not being first inside it. A human who clicks within 500ms of the announced instant has the same odds as a script that fired in 2ms.
  • Pre-sale connection priming gets its own signal: a connection opened well before sale_start_time, specifically to be ready the instant the queue opens, is flagged independently of anything that happens inside the randomized window — this is the piece Ticketmaster's less-precisely-timed on-sales don't need as sharply.
  • Ticketmaster's other defenses still apply, layered on top: per-account limits and rate limiting the queue-join endpoint remain worth doing — randomized admission fixes the ordering unfairness specifically, not every form of abuse.

This is a genuine philosophical fork from Ticketmaster's waiting room, worth stating plainly: Ticketmaster's admission answers "who's been waiting longest," which is a reasonable notion of fairness when arrival times are naturally staggered. A flash sale's arrivals cluster at one instant by design, which turns "longest waiting" into "fastest script" — a different notion of fairness for a genuinely different arrival pattern.

Does it hold up?

RequirementHow
Never oversell, even at instant-spike trafficA specific InventoryUnit is conditionally claimed per reservation — the same claim-a-row, not decrement-a-counter discipline as Ticketmaster's seat hold.
Each product's admission calibrated to its own demandOne Redis instance, per-product keys, independent admission rates — no shared pool to split ahead of time, no forecast required.
Idle holds cost proportional to actual contentionExpiry is computed per reservation from live queue_depth and inventory_remaining, not a flat window copied across every product.
Resistant to pre-timed bot advantageAdmission is a random draw from a short arrival window, not a speed-ordered queue — plus explicit detection of connections primed before the announced instant.
Downstream infra survives many products spiking at onceA global concurrency ceiling, independent of any single product's own rate, bounds total load on the shared database and payment provider.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
Per-product admission rate vs. one shared queueMatches each product's actual demand without needing a forecast, at the cost of one more parameter (the rate itself) to set and monitor per product, instead of one setting for the whole sale.
Adaptive expiry vs. a flat checkout windowKeeps an idle hold's cost proportional to real contention, at the cost of a shorter, less forgiving window for the most popular products — genuinely harder for a buyer without saved payment details to complete in time.
Randomized admission vs. strict speed-orderingRemoves reaction speed as a decisive advantage, at the cost of a human who arrives right at the announced instant having no better odds than one who arrives a moment later within the same window — "first" stops meaning anything, on purpose.

Tools you might reach for

ComponentReal-world options
Waiting Room (per-product keys)Redis — sorted sets or lists, one key per product, independent admission workers per key
Bot Defensea rate limiter and connection-age check in front of the queue-join endpoint — same pattern as the Rate Limiter example, aimed at pre-sale timing specifically
DatabasePostgreSQL — a conditional claim on InventoryUnit, the same optimistic-concurrency pattern as Ticketmaster's seat hold
PaymentStripe or similar — charge fired async, confirmed via webhook
Reservation expiry sweepa scheduled worker plus a lazy check on read, the same hybrid as Ticketmaster's Hold Expiry
Worked Example Q — When One Answer Doesn't Fit Both Callers

Design an OAuth Authorization Service

token validation at scale · revocation vs. statelessness · one service, two threat models
Click to expand

"Design the authorization service behind a company's entire API surface. Internal microservices call each other constantly — high volume, latency-sensitive, trusted network. External partners call the same public APIs — lower volume, untrusted, and a leaked token there is a genuine incident." It's tempting to answer this by listing OAuth grant types, but that's protocol trivia, not design. The actual architectural question is narrower and much harder: how does every single API call in the company get its token validated without the auth service becoming either a bottleneck or a liability? — and the honest answer turns out to be different for those two callers.

1
Requirements
Functional
  • Issue access tokens to both internal services (service-to-service) and external partner applications (on behalf of a user).
  • Validate a presented token on every API call — the gateway must be able to decide allow/deny.
  • Support scopes: a token grants access to specific operations, not blanket access to everything.
  • Revoke a token before its natural expiry — a leaked credential, a terminated employee, a user withdrawing an app's access.
  • Refresh a long-lived session without forcing re-authentication, and detect when a refresh credential has been stolen.
Non-functional
  • Token validation must not add meaningful latency to internal service-to-service calls, which happen constantly.
  • The auth service must not become a hard synchronous dependency whose outage takes down every API in the company.
  • Revocation of an external partner's token must take effect quickly — measured in seconds, not "whenever it expires."
  • Signing keys must be rotatable without invalidating tokens already in flight.

Worth asking: how fast is "quickly" for revocation? The answer drives the whole design. We'll assume seconds for external partners (a real security requirement) and tolerate token-lifetime delay for internal services (where the network is already trusted and the blast radius is smaller).

2
Core Entities
  • Client (id, secret_hash, type, allowed_scopes, redirect_uris) — type is Internal or External; the two get materially different treatment everywhere downstream.
  • AccessToken (jti, client_id, subject, scopes, expires_at) — jti is the unique token id, the handle revocation needs.
  • RefreshToken (id, client_id, subject, family_id, used_at, expires_at) — family_id links every token descended from one original grant, which is what makes theft detection possible.
  • RevocationEntry (jti, revoked_at, expires_at) — expires_at matches the token's own expiry, so the list self-prunes rather than growing forever.
  • SigningKey (kid, public_key, private_key, status) — status is Active, Retiring, or Retired; multiple keys valid at once is what makes rotation possible without downtime.
3
API

POST /oauth/token → issues an access token (and refresh token, for external flows)
POST /oauth/introspect → validates a token, returns active/inactive plus claims — the authoritative check
POST /oauth/revoke → revokes a token immediately
GET /.well-known/jwks.json → the public keys gateways use to verify signatures locally, keyed by kid

4
Data Flow — genuinely earns its place here
  1. A client authenticates and receives a signed access token; external clients also receive a refresh token.
  2. The client calls an API; the Gateway intercepts and must decide whether this token is valid.
  3. For an internal token, the Gateway verifies the signature locally against a cached JWKS public key — no network call to the auth service at all.
  4. For an external token, the Gateway additionally checks the token's jti against a revocation cache before allowing it through.
  5. On revocation, the auth service writes a RevocationEntry and pushes it to every Gateway's revocation cache, so the next call carrying that token is rejected.
5
High-Level Design

The Gateway validates most traffic without ever touching the auth service — the exception is deliberate, and scoped to exactly the traffic that needs it.

6
Deep Dives

Three below: (a) stateless JWT vs. introspection, and why the answer differs by caller, (b) revocation without giving up statelessness, and (c) refresh token rotation as a theft-detection mechanism.

The Auth Service is deliberately off the hot path — the Gateway verifies token signatures locally against cached public keys, so an auth-service outage doesn't take down every API in the company. External tokens pay one extra check (the revocation cache) that internal tokens skip, because their threat models genuinely differ.
An internal service calls another
  1. Service A presents its token to the Gateway.
  2. The Gateway reads the token's kid, looks up that public key in its local JWKS cache, and verifies the signature — no network call.
  3. Claims check out and aren't expired; the request forwards to the product API with the claims attached.
  4. Total added latency: a signature verification, measured in microseconds. The auth service was never contacted.
A partner's token gets revoked
  1. A partner reports a leaked credential; an operator calls POST /oauth/revoke.
  2. The Auth Service writes a RevocationEntry keyed by the token's jti, with expires_at matching the token's own expiry.
  3. The entry is pushed to every Gateway's revocation cache — seconds, not minutes.
  4. The next request carrying that token passes signature verification but fails the revocation check, and is rejected.
Key deep dive — stateless JWT vs. introspection, and why the answer differs by caller
FRValidate a token on every callNFRNo meaningful latency internallyNFRAuth service isn't a hard dependency
Thought process
  1. Start with the simplest correct design: the Gateway calls the auth service to validate every token. Trace it against real volume — every internal API call in the company now makes an extra network hop, and the auth service's outage becomes a total outage.
  2. Consider the opposite: a self-contained signed token the Gateway verifies locally with a public key. No network call, no shared dependency. Test it against the revocation requirement — and it fails outright, because a token that validates itself can't be un-validated before it expires.
  3. Notice the two requirements pulling in opposite directions aren't equally weighted for every caller: internal traffic is enormous and on a trusted network; external traffic is a fraction of the volume but carries real leak risk.
  4. Stop looking for one answer. Give internal callers stateless JWTs (their volume demands it, their trusted network tolerates the revocation delay), and give external callers the same JWT plus one revocation check (their volume makes the extra lookup affordable, their risk profile requires it).

This is the fork the whole design turns on, and the interesting answer is that picking one globally is the mistake — the right choice depends on which caller you're serving, within the same system.

Illustration — same token format, different validation pathINTERNAL (service-to-service, ~100k req/s across the fleet) verify signature with cached public key → ~50 microseconds, no network check exp claim → local → allow. Auth service never contacted. Revocation delay: up to the token lifetime (kept short — 5 min). EXTERNAL (partner apps, ~500 req/s) verify signature with cached public key → ~50 microseconds, no network check exp claim → local check jti against revocation cache → one local cache lookup → allow. Auth service still not contacted on the hot path. Revocation delay: seconds (as soon as the push lands). The extra check is affordable precisely BECAUSE external volume is small. Applying it to all internal traffic would mean a revocation-cache lookup on every one of those 100k req/s — real cost, for a threat model that doesn't warrant it on a trusted internal network.
  • Short internal token lifetimes are what make the trade-off acceptable: a 5-minute internal token means the worst-case window where a revoked-but-still-valid token works is 5 minutes — bounded, and on a network where the attacker would already need to be inside.
  • Neither path calls the auth service synchronously — that's the property worth protecting. The auth service issues and revokes; it is never in the request path of a normal API call, so its outage degrades token issuance, not every API in the company.

Same instinct as the stock price alert system's hot-vs-illiquid tiering and the meeting room's index-vs-tree escalation — match the mechanism to what that specific case actually needs, rather than paying the strictest case's cost everywhere.

Key deep dive — revocation without giving up statelessness
FRRevoke before natural expiryNFRExternal revocation in seconds
Thought process
  1. Name the contradiction directly: a self-validating token is valid because its signature says so. Revocation means declaring it invalid anyway — which requires state the token itself can't carry.
  2. Consider keeping a full list of valid tokens and checking membership. Test it — that's introspection with extra steps, and it reintroduces exactly the dependency statelessness removed.
  3. Invert it: keep a list of revoked tokens instead of valid ones. Revocations are rare; valid tokens are the overwhelming majority — so the list stays small by construction.
  4. Bound it further: a revoked token only matters until it would have expired anyway. Give each revocation entry the same expiry as its token, and the list prunes itself with zero maintenance.

A revocation list is the standard answer, and it works because of an asymmetry worth stating plainly: listing what's valid would be enormous and constantly changing; listing what's revoked is small, rare, and self-expiring.

Illustration — why the list stays smallExternal tokens issued per day: ~2,000,000 External tokens actually revoked: ~50 (leaks, offboarding, user withdrawal) Revocation list holds only those ~50 — and each entry self-deletes when its token would have expired anyway: RevocationEntry(jti="a1b2...", revoked_at=12:00:03, expires_at=12:15:00) → at 12:15:00 the token is dead on its own merits; the entry is now redundant and can be dropped. Steady-state list size: revocations per token-lifetime window, not per day. At a 15-minute lifetime, that's a handful of entries — small enough to replicate to every gateway and check in memory.
  • Pushed to gateways, not pulled per request: the auth service publishes revocations to every Gateway's local cache. The check stays a local memory lookup — the whole point is not to reintroduce a network call.
  • The propagation window is the honest weak point: between the revoke call and the push landing on every gateway, the token still works. Bounding that (seconds) is the actual design target, and worth stating explicitly rather than claiming revocation is instant.
Key deep dive — refresh token rotation as theft detection
FRRefresh without re-authenticationFRDetect a stolen refresh credential
Thought process
  1. Notice the asymmetry: access tokens are short-lived by design, but refresh tokens live for weeks — which makes the refresh token, not the access token, the thing actually worth stealing.
  2. Consider a static refresh token, reusable until expiry. Test it against theft — an attacker with a copy can mint fresh access tokens indefinitely, and nothing in the system ever notices.
  3. Rotate instead: each refresh consumes the old token and issues a new one. Now a stolen token is only useful until the legitimate client refreshes next.
  4. Extract the detection signal that falls out for free: if a refresh token that was already used gets presented again, there are two copies in circulation. That's not ambiguous — it's proof of theft, and the response is to kill the entire token family.

Rotation is usually described as a hardening measure, but its real value is the detection signal it creates: a reused old token is unambiguous evidence that someone has a copy they shouldn't.

Illustration — the reuse that proves theftLegitimate client: RT1 → (refresh) → RT2 → (refresh) → RT3 RT1 and RT2 are now marked used. Attacker stole RT2 earlier and tries it: presents RT2 → server sees RT2.used_at is already set → two parties hold RT2. One of them is not the legitimate client. → revoke the ENTIRE family_id: RT1, RT2, RT3, and every access token descended from that original grant. Both parties are now logged out — deliberately. The legitimate user re-authenticates; the attacker gets nothing. Ending the session for the real user is the correct trade, because the alternative is guessing which of the two is genuine, and guessing wrong leaves the attacker inside.
  • family_id is what makes the response decisive: without it, you could only kill the one reused token, leaving the attacker's other descendants alive. Linking every token back to its original grant means one signal kills the whole lineage.

Does it hold up?

RequirementHow
No meaningful latency on internal callsLocal signature verification against a cached public key — no network call to the auth service at all.
Auth service isn't a hard dependencyIt issues and revokes; it's never in the request path of a normal API call, so its outage degrades issuance, not every API.
External revocation takes effect in secondsA small, self-pruning revocation list pushed to every gateway's local cache, checked in memory on external tokens only.
Stolen refresh credentials are detectedRotation makes reuse of an already-used token unambiguous evidence of theft; family_id makes the response cover the whole lineage.
Keys rotate without breaking tokens in flightJWKS serves several keys at once, each identified by kid — a token signed with a retiring key still verifies until it expires.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
Stateless JWT vs. introspection on every callRemoves the auth service from the hot path entirely — no latency, no shared point of failure — at the cost of revocation not being instant. Bounded by keeping token lifetimes short, and closed for external tokens with a revocation cache.
Different validation paths for internal vs. externalEach caller pays only what its threat model warrants, at the cost of two code paths to maintain and reason about instead of one uniform rule.
Killing the whole family on refresh reuseGuarantees the attacker is ejected, at the cost of also logging out the legitimate user — accepted deliberately, because the alternative is guessing which party is genuine.

Tools you might reach for

ComponentReal-world options
Auth Servicea managed provider (Auth0, Okta, Keycloak) or built in-house when the platform needs bespoke scope/tenant semantics
Token formatJWT signed with asymmetric keys (RS256/ES256) — asymmetric specifically so gateways verify with a public key and never hold signing material
Revocation cacheRedis with TTL matching token expiry, or an in-process cache fed by a pub/sub push
Key storageAWS KMS, HashiCorp Vault — private keys never leave the auth service
Token StorePostgreSQL for clients, refresh families, and key metadata
Worked Example R — Where Strong Consistency Is the Wrong Answer

Design a Service Registry & Discovery System

client vs. server-side discovery · stale entries as a given · AP over CP
Click to expand

"Hundreds of microservices, each running many instances that scale up, scale down, crash, and get redeployed constantly. Any service needs to find a healthy instance of any other service, without anyone hardcoding an IP address." The mechanism sounds trivial — a lookup table of service names to addresses — but two things make it a real design problem: the registry is on the path of essentially every internal call, so its availability characteristics matter more than its correctness ones, and an entry can be wrong the instant after it's read.

1
Requirements
Functional
  • An instance registers itself on startup and deregisters on graceful shutdown.
  • A caller can resolve a service name to a list of currently-healthy instance addresses.
  • Instances that die without deregistering (crash, network loss, OOM kill) are removed automatically.
  • Support metadata on registrations — version, zone, weight — so callers can filter or prefer certain instances.
Non-functional
  • Resolution must be fast enough to sit in front of every internal call — no meaningful added latency.
  • The registry must stay available during a network partition, even at the cost of serving stale data.
  • Detection of a dead instance should take seconds, not minutes — but callers must tolerate the window where it hasn't happened yet.
  • Registry load must not grow unmanageably as instance count grows into the thousands.

Worth asking: is a stale entry actually dangerous here? For discovery, a caller handed a dead address retries another instance and moves on — recoverable. That tolerance is what licenses the availability-over-consistency choice below, and it wouldn't hold for a system where a stale read causes an unrecoverable wrong action.

2
Core Entities
  • Service (name, instances[]) — the logical name callers resolve against, e.g. "billing-api".
  • Instance (id, service_name, address, port, metadata, status, last_heartbeat_at) — one running process; last_heartbeat_at is what liveness is actually derived from.
  • HeartbeatLease (instance_id, ttl, renewed_at) — a registration is a lease that expires unless renewed, not a permanent record.
3
API

PUT /instances → register, returns a lease
PUT /instances/{id}/heartbeat → renew the lease
DELETE /instances/{id} → graceful deregistration
GET /services/{name} → healthy instances, usually served from the caller's local cache rather than hitting the registry

4
Data Flow — genuinely earns its place here
  1. An instance starts, registers itself, and receives a lease with a TTL.
  2. It heartbeats on an interval well shorter than the TTL; missing enough heartbeats lets the lease expire and the entry is evicted.
  3. Callers hold a locally cached copy of the registry, refreshed on an interval — resolution is a local lookup, not a network call per request.
  4. A caller picks an instance from its cached list and connects directly; if that instance is dead, the caller fails fast and retries a different one.
5
High-Level Design

The registry is replicated and eventually consistent, and callers cache it locally — so a registry outage degrades freshness, not the ability to route at all.

6
Deep Dives

Three below: (a) client-side vs. server-side discovery, (b) heartbeats, eviction, and why stale entries are a given, and (c) why AP beats CP for a registry specifically.

The registry is not in the request path. Callers cache it locally and connect directly to instances — so a registry outage means routing data gets stale, not that routing stops. Registry nodes replicate to each other without a consensus round trip, which is what lets any node keep serving reads during a partition.
An instance joins and leaves
  1. The instance registers on startup and gets a lease with a TTL of, say, 30 seconds.
  2. It heartbeats every 10 seconds — comfortably inside the TTL, so transient hiccups don't evict a healthy instance.
  3. On graceful shutdown it deregisters explicitly, which removes it immediately rather than waiting for the lease to lapse.
  4. If it crashes instead, heartbeats simply stop; the lease expires and the registry evicts it without anyone telling it to.
A caller resolves and connects
  1. The caller's local cache already holds the instance list, refreshed on an interval in the background.
  2. Resolution is an in-memory lookup — no network call to the registry on the request path.
  3. The caller picks an instance (round-robin, weighted, or zone-preferring) and connects directly.
  4. If that instance is dead but not yet evicted, the connection fails fast and the caller immediately retries a different one from the list.
Key deep dive — client-side vs. server-side discovery
FRResolve a name to healthy instancesNFRNo meaningful added latency
Thought process
  1. Start with the most literal reading: the caller asks the registry "where is billing-api" on every call, then connects. Trace the cost — that's an extra network round trip on every single internal request, and the registry is now on the hot path of everything.
  2. Fix the round trip without changing the model: let the caller cache the instance list locally and refresh it in the background. Resolution becomes an in-memory lookup, and the registry leaves the hot path.
  3. Consider the alternative shape: put a load balancer in front of each service, and let callers just address the balancer. Clients get simpler — no registry logic, no cache, no library per language.
  4. Weigh what that costs: the balancer is an extra hop on every call and becomes its own thing to scale and keep alive. The choice isn't which is correct — it's whether you'd rather own discovery logic in every client, or own a routing tier.

Both designs are legitimate and widely used; the deciding factor is usually organizational, not technical — how many languages your services are written in, and whether you already run a service mesh.

Client-side discovery

The caller holds a cached instance list and connects directly to a chosen instance. This is the design diagrammed above.

  • No extra network hop — the registry is fully off the request path.
  • The caller controls instance selection, so zone-preference or weighting is easy.
  • Costs a discovery client library in every language your fleet uses — the real tax, and it grows with language diversity.
Server-side discovery

The caller addresses a stable load balancer or mesh sidecar; that tier resolves and forwards.

  • Clients stay dumb — no registry logic, no cache, nothing language-specific.
  • Routing policy changes centrally, without redeploying callers.
  • Costs an extra hop per call, and the routing tier is now infrastructure you must scale and keep highly available.

A service mesh is the modern reconciliation of these two: a sidecar proxy next to each service gives you server-side discovery's dumb clients, while the sidecar being local means the extra hop never leaves the host. It trades the client-library tax for a per-pod proxy to operate instead.

Key deep dive — heartbeats, eviction, and why stale entries are a given
FRRemove instances that die silentlyNFRDetection in secondsNFRRegistry load stays manageable
Thought process
  1. Start with the registry polling every instance to check liveness. Test it at scale — with thousands of instances, one registry now originates thousands of outbound health checks on a fixed interval, and that load grows linearly with the fleet.
  2. Invert the direction: let each instance push a heartbeat to renew a lease. The work distributes across the fleet, and the registry only has to notice absence rather than actively probe.
  3. Pick the intervals deliberately: heartbeat well inside the TTL (say every 10s against a 30s lease) so one dropped packet never evicts a healthy instance, but the lease is short enough that a real death is noticed in seconds.
  4. Accept what that leaves: between the moment an instance dies and the moment its lease lapses, the registry advertises a dead address. That window can be shrunk but never eliminated — so the caller, not the registry, has to be the thing that handles it.

Every liveness scheme has a detection window, so the design question isn't how to eliminate stale entries — it's who is responsible for coping when one is handed out.

Illustration — the unavoidable window, and who absorbs itt=0s Instance 3 crashes. It cannot deregister — it's gone. t=0-30s Its lease has not yet expired. The registry still lists it. Callers refreshing their cache in this window receive a dead address. t=30s Lease lapses. Registry evicts it. New refreshes no longer include it. t=30-60s Callers that cached BEFORE eviction still hold it until their next refresh — the staleness window is lease TTL PLUS cache refresh interval. So a caller WILL be handed dead addresses. The registry cannot prevent this. What makes the system work is the caller's behavior: connect → fails fast → immediately try the next instance in the list → mark that instance locally bad so this request's siblings skip it → succeed on instance 2, with the user never noticing
  • Fail fast, then retry elsewhere — short connect timeouts matter here specifically, because the recovery path depends on discovering the dead instance quickly rather than blocking on it.
  • Local negative caching — a caller that just failed against an instance should skip it for a short period rather than rediscovering the same failure on every subsequent request.
  • Graceful deregistration closes the common case — a planned deploy or scale-down deregisters explicitly, so the window above only applies to genuine crashes, not routine operations.

Same shape as the stock price alert system's in-process index and the meeting room's interval tree: the fast local copy is allowed to be briefly wrong, and correctness comes from how the system behaves when it is.

Key deep dive — why AP beats CP for a registry specifically
NFRAvailable during a partitionNFRRegistry not a single point of failure
Thought process
  1. Reach for the instinctive answer first: the registry is infrastructure everything depends on, so make it strongly consistent — a Raft-backed store where every node agrees exactly.
  2. Trace what strong consistency actually does during a network partition: nodes that lose quorum must refuse reads to preserve correctness. Callers on that side of the partition now resolve nothing.
  3. Ask what the failure actually costs. Refusing reads means services that are perfectly healthy and reachable can no longer find each other — a partition in the registry has become a total outage in the fleet.
  4. Compare that against the cost of being wrong instead of unavailable: an AP registry serves a possibly-stale list, the caller may get one dead address, retries, and succeeds. Recoverable in milliseconds.
  5. Conclude against the instinct: for discovery, availability dominates. Slightly-wrong routing data is dramatically better than no routing data, so the registry should stay up and be occasionally wrong.

This is the place where the reflex answer — "make critical infrastructure strongly consistent" — is actively harmful, and it's worth being able to say why out loud.

Illustration — the same partition, two registry designsNetwork partition splits the cluster: 2 registry nodes | 3 registry nodes CP registry (Raft/quorum): minority side (2 nodes) loses quorum → refuses reads → every caller in that zone resolves NOTHING → healthy, reachable services cannot find each other → partition in the registry = outage in the fleet AP registry (peer replication, no quorum): both sides keep serving from their own copy → minority side serves data that may be seconds stale → a caller might get one address that's since been evicted elsewhere → connection fails → retry next instance → succeeds → partition in the registry = slightly degraded freshness The AP failure is recoverable in milliseconds by the caller. The CP failure is not recoverable by the caller at all.
  • Self-preservation is the counterintuitive safeguard: if a large fraction of instances stop heartbeating at once, that is far more likely a network problem than the simultaneous death of most of the fleet. A registry that blindly trusts its own eviction logic would empty itself and take everything down. The correct response is to stop evicting and keep serving the last known-good data until heartbeats recover.
  • The tolerance has limits worth naming: this reasoning holds because a stale discovery read is cheaply recoverable. The same argument would not justify AP for something like leader election or distributed locking, where acting on stale data causes damage you cannot retry your way out of — the fencing-token problem, not this one.

The general principle worth carrying out of this example: consistency requirements come from what a wrong answer costs, not from how important the system feels. Discovery is critical infrastructure that specifically does not need strong consistency.

Does it hold up?

RequirementHow
No meaningful added latencyCallers resolve from a locally cached copy — the registry is never on the request path.
Available during a partitionPeer replication without a quorum requirement, so any reachable node keeps serving reads even when cut off from the rest.
Dead instances removed automaticallyRegistration is a lease; heartbeats renew it, silence lets it lapse, and eviction follows without anyone reporting the death.
Stale entries don't break callersFail-fast connects, immediate retry against another instance, and local negative caching so the same dead address isn't retried repeatedly.
Registry load scales with the fleetInstances push heartbeats rather than the registry polling thousands of targets; caller reads are absorbed by local caches.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
AP registry vs. CP registryStays available and useful during a partition, at the cost of occasionally handing out an address that's already dead — acceptable only because the caller can retry, and explicitly not a template for locking or leader election.
Client-side vs. server-side discoveryClient-side removes a hop and gives callers routing control, at the cost of a discovery library in every language; server-side keeps clients dumb, at the cost of a routing tier to run and an extra hop per call.
Heartbeat leases vs. registry-initiated pollingDistributes liveness work across the fleet instead of concentrating it, at the cost of trusting instances to report themselves — an instance that is hung but still heartbeating looks healthy, which deeper application-level health checks exist to catch.

Tools you might reach for

ComponentReal-world options
AP-leaning registryEureka — built for exactly this trade-off, including self-preservation mode
CP-leaning registryConsul, etcd, ZooKeeper — the right call when the same store also does locking or leader election, where stale reads genuinely are dangerous
Service mesh (server-side, local hop)Istio, Linkerd — sidecar proxies resolving on the client's behalf
Kubernetes-nativeServices and Endpoints backed by etcd, with kube-proxy or a mesh doing the routing — discovery you inherit rather than build
Worked Example S — The Cost of a Shared Chokepoint

Design an API Gateway

filter ordering · blast radius isolation · config without restarts
Click to expand

"One entry point in front of hundreds of backend services. Every external and partner API call passes through it — authenticated, rate limited, routed, observed." The individual jobs are already solved elsewhere on this page: token validation in the OAuth Authorization Service, instance lookup in Service Registry & Discovery, throttling in Rate Limiter. What makes the gateway its own design problem is that it is a single component every request must traverse — which turns three otherwise-mundane questions into hard ones: what order the work happens in, what stops one bad backend from taking down all the others, and how routes change while it keeps serving.

1
Requirements
Functional
  • Route an incoming request to the correct backend service based on path, host, or header.
  • Enforce authentication and per-consumer rate limits before the request reaches any backend.
  • Let product teams add, change, or remove routes without a gateway restart or redeploy.
  • Emit consistent logs, metrics, and traces for every request, regardless of which backend served it.
Non-functional
  • Added latency must be small relative to the backend call it wraps — single-digit milliseconds.
  • One slow or failing backend must not degrade requests routed to any other backend.
  • Rejecting an unauthenticated or over-quota request must cost far less than serving a legitimate one.
  • A route configuration change must propagate to every gateway instance within seconds, without dropping in-flight requests.

Worth asking: does the gateway aggregate responses from several backends into one, or strictly forward one request to one service? We'll assume strict forwarding — aggregation is a backend-for-frontend concern, and folding it in here would put business logic in a component whose whole value is being generic.

2
Core Entities
  • Route (id, match_rules, target_service, filters[], timeout_ms) — one routing rule; filters is the ordered chain applied to matching requests.
  • Filter (type, config, order) — a single unit of work in the chain: auth, rate limit, header rewrite, logging.
  • Consumer (id, type, quota, allowed_routes[]) — who is calling; the identity rate limits and authorization are evaluated against.
  • BackendPool (service_name, instances[], circuit_state, concurrency_limit) — resolved from the registry; circuit_state and concurrency_limit are what keep one bad backend contained.
3
API

The data plane has no API of its own — it serves whatever the routes describe. The control plane does:
PUT /routes/{id} → create or update a route (used by product teams, usually via CI)
GET /routes → current route table
GET /health → gateway liveness, distinct from any backend's health

4
Data Flow — genuinely earns its place here
  1. A request arrives; the gateway matches it against its in-memory route table.
  2. The filter chain runs in a deliberate order — cheapest and most likely to reject first, so junk traffic is dropped before expensive work happens.
  3. The gateway resolves the target service to an instance using its cached registry copy, then checks that backend's circuit state and concurrency budget.
  4. The request is forwarded with a per-route timeout; the response passes back through the chain for logging and header rewriting.
  5. Separately and continuously, the control plane pushes route changes to every instance, which swap them in atomically without dropping in-flight work.
5
High-Level Design

A stateless data plane that can be scaled horizontally, and a control plane that is never on the request path — diagrammed below.

6
Deep Dives

Three below: (a) why filter order is a security and cost decision, (b) stopping one slow backend from starving every other route, and (c) changing routes without restarting.

The filter chain runs cheapest-rejection-first, so junk traffic dies before it costs a signature verification. Both external dependencies — key material and instance lists — are locally cached, so neither the auth service nor the registry is on the request path. The control plane pushes route changes and is never on it either.
A legitimate request
  1. Matched against the in-memory route table — a prefix-tree lookup, not a scan of every rule.
  2. Passes the cheap anonymous IP limit, then signature verification against the cached public key.
  3. Now that the consumer is known, their per-consumer quota is checked and decremented.
  4. An instance is picked from the cached registry list, the backend's concurrency budget is taken, and the request is forwarded under a per-route timeout.
A backend goes slow
  1. Service B's latency climbs; its in-flight requests start consuming the gateway's concurrency budget for B.
  2. The budget for B fills. Further requests to B are rejected immediately rather than queued — the gateway stops waiting on it.
  3. Sustained failures trip B's circuit breaker; requests to B now fail instantly with a clear error instead of consuming a slot at all.
  4. Service A's budget was never touched. Requests to A are served at full speed throughout — the failure stayed inside B's compartment.
Key deep dive — why filter order is a security and cost decision
FRAuth and rate limit before backendsNFRRejection costs less than service
Thought process
  1. Start with the obvious ordering: authenticate first, since nothing should happen for an unidentified caller. Trace the cost under attack — every junk request now forces a signature verification before it can be rejected, so an attacker sets the gateway's CPU bill.
  2. Invert it: rate limit first, because a counter increment is orders of magnitude cheaper than verifying a signature. But rate limit by what? Per-consumer quotas need an identity, which only exists after authentication.
  3. Notice the apparent deadlock isn't one — there are two different limits being conflated. An anonymous, coarse limit keyed on IP needs no identity at all and can run first; the precise per-consumer quota runs after auth, when identity exists.
  4. Generalize the rule that falls out: order filters by cost ascending and rejection likelihood descending. Anything that can reject a request cheaply belongs before anything expensive.

Filter order looks like a configuration detail and is actually a security property — it decides how much work an attacker can force the gateway to do per junk request.

Illustration — the same four filters, two orderingsWRONG — authenticate first: 1. verify JWT signature ← ~50-100µs of CPU, on EVERY junk request 2. per-consumer quota 3. IP rate limit 4. route + forward → an attacker sending garbage tokens sets your CPU bill. 100k junk req/s = 100k signature verifications you chose to perform. RIGHT — cheapest rejection first: 1.
