IP rate limit ← one counter increment, ~1µs, no identity needed 2. verify JWT signature ← only for traffic that survived step 1 3. per-consumer quota ← needs identity, so it must come after auth 4. route + forward → the same attacker is rejected at step 1. The expensive filter never runs for them. The two rate limits are NOT redundant: step 1 is coarse and anonymous (protecting the gateway itself), step 3 is precise and per-consumer (enforcing what a customer actually paid for).
  • Routing comes last for a reason — it's the step that commits a backend's resources. Everything that might reject the request should have already run.
  • Response-side filters run in reverse — logging and header rewriting wrap the call, so the chain unwinds outward on the way back, capturing the real outcome including timeouts.

This is the same instinct as the rate limiter's fail-fast enforcement layer, applied to a chain rather than a single check: reject as early and as cheaply as the information available allows.

Key deep dive — stopping one slow backend from starving every other route
NFROne bad backend doesn't degrade othersNFRSmall added latency
Thought process
  1. Notice the structural risk of a shared chokepoint: the gateway has one finite pool of connections and worker slots, and every backend draws from it.
  2. Trace what one slow backend does to that shared pool. Requests to it don't fail — they wait. Each waiting request holds a slot. Slots fill up with traffic bound for one sick service.
  3. Follow it to the conclusion: requests for perfectly healthy services now can't get a slot. One slow backend has taken down the entire API surface, without ever returning an error.
  4. Fix the root cause, which is the sharing: give each backend its own bounded budget of concurrent requests. When B's budget is exhausted, B's requests are rejected — A's budget was never touched.
  5. Add the second layer for sustained failure: once a backend is failing consistently, stop even occupying a slot to discover that again. Trip a circuit and fail instantly until a probe shows recovery.

This is the failure mode that only exists because there's a shared chokepoint, which makes it the gateway's own problem rather than one inherited from the services behind it.

Illustration — shared pool vs. per-backend bulkheadsSHARED POOL (200 worker slots, no isolation) Service B degrades: p99 goes 50ms → 30s (not failing, just slow) B receives 20 req/s × 30s in flight = 600 requests waiting → 200 slots all held by B traffic within ~10 seconds → requests for A, C, D can't get a slot → ENTIRE gateway is down. B never returned a single error. PER-BACKEND BULKHEADS (budget per service) A: limit 80 in-flight 12 ✓ serving normally B: limit 40 in-flight 40 ✗ budget full → new B requests rejected immediately (503, ~0ms, no slot held) C: limit 60 in-flight 8 ✓ serving normally → B's blast radius is exactly B. 120 slots remain untouchable by it. After B fails consistently → circuit OPENS → B's requests now fail instantly without consuming even a B slot, until a periodic probe finds B healthy again.
  • Timeouts are what make budgets finite in practice — without a per-route timeout, a slot held by a hung request is held forever, and a bounded budget just becomes a smaller pool to exhaust. The timeout is what guarantees slots come back.
  • Shedding fast is better than queueing — a request rejected in microseconds lets the caller retry or degrade gracefully; one queued behind a 30-second backend usually has an upstream client that already gave up.
  • The circuit's half-open probe matters — a circuit that opens must let a trickle of traffic through periodically, or it can never discover the backend recovered and stays open forever.

The name is borrowed from ships: compartments that flood independently so a breach in one doesn't sink the vessel. The design question a gateway forces you to answer is which compartment boundary matters — here it's per backend service, because that's the unit that fails together.

Key deep dive — changing routes without restarting
FRAdd or change routes without redeployNFRPropagates in seconds, no dropped requests
Thought process
  1. Start from configuration as a file baked into the image. Test it against the actual requirement — every product team shipping a new API now needs a gateway redeploy, which makes the platform team a bottleneck for everyone.
  2. Consider reading route config from a database on each request instead. That guarantees freshness and is immediately disqualifying: a database lookup on the hot path of every request, plus a hard dependency whose outage is a total outage.
  3. Split the two concerns: keep the route table in memory for lookup speed, and update that in-memory copy out of band. Routes change on the order of times per day; requests arrive thousands of times per second, so serving from memory and refreshing occasionally is obviously right.
  4. Make the swap safe: build the new route table fully, validate it, then replace the pointer atomically. A request in flight either sees the whole old table or the whole new one, never a half-applied mixture.

This is the control-plane / data-plane split that shows up in most infrastructure: the thing that changes slowly and the thing that runs constantly should not be the same code path.

Illustration — atomic swap, not in-place editWRONG — mutate the live table: routeTable.remove("/v1/billing") ← a request arriving RIGHT HERE routeTable.add("/v1/billing", newCfg) gets a 404 for a route that exists both before and after RIGHT — build, validate, swap: newTable := buildFrom(config) // built off to the side if err := validate(newTable); err != nil { keep serving the old table // a bad config CANNOT take the fleet down return } atomic.Store(&activeTable, newTable) // one pointer write → in-flight requests finish against the table they started with → new requests see the complete new table → no window where a route is missing Instances converge independently, so for a few seconds different gateway instances may serve different route versions. For routing that's fine — same reasoning as the registry in example R: brief disagreement is acceptable when both answers are individually valid.
  • Validation before swap is the load-bearing step — a malformed route pushed to every instance simultaneously is one of the few ways a stateless gateway fleet can fail all at once. Rejecting it at the instance, and continuing on the last known-good table, contains that.
  • Config changes are an audited write path — since a route change redirects production traffic, it deserves the same review and rollback story as a code deploy, even though it isn't one.

Does it hold up?

RequirementHow
Small added latencyIn-memory route matching, locally cached keys and instance lists — no network call to any dependency on the request path.
Rejection costs less than serviceFilters ordered cheapest-rejection-first, so an anonymous IP limit drops junk before any signature verification runs.
One bad backend doesn't degrade othersPer-backend concurrency budgets plus circuit breakers, with per-route timeouts guaranteeing slots are released.
Routes change without a restartControl plane pushes config; instances build and validate a new table off to the side, then swap a pointer atomically.
Consistent observabilityLogging and tracing filters wrap every route uniformly, so telemetry doesn't depend on each backend implementing it.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
A gateway at all vs. clients calling services directlyCentralizes auth, throttling, and observability so every service doesn't reimplement them — at the cost of introducing a shared chokepoint whose failure modes (this example's second deep dive) you now own.
Per-backend bulkheads vs. one shared poolContains a slow backend to its own compartment, at the cost of lower peak utilization — budgets reserved for a quiet service sit unused while a busy one is throttled.
Cached config and keys vs. reading them liveKeeps every dependency off the request path, at the cost of a propagation window where instances briefly disagree — acceptable here for the same reason it was in the registry example.
Strict forwarding vs. response aggregationKeeps the gateway generic and free of business logic, at the cost of clients making several calls where one aggregated response would have done — which is what a backend-for-frontend layer exists to solve instead.

Tools you might reach for

ComponentReal-world options
Data planeEnvoy, NGINX, Kong, or a custom Go service when filter logic is bespoke enough to justify it
Control planeEnvoy xDS, or a small service watching config in etcd / Consul and pushing updates
Circuit breaking & bulkheadsbuilt into Envoy and most mesh sidecars; library-level (gobreaker, resilience4j) in a custom gateway
Rate limit countersRedis for cross-instance limits, in-process counters where approximate per-instance limits suffice
ObservabilityOpenTelemetry for traces, Datadog or Prometheus for metrics — emitted uniformly by the gateway, not per backend
Worked Example T — When the Bottleneck Is Organizational

Design an API Developer Portal

spec as source of truth · governance without review queues · breaking-change gates
Click to expand

"Dozens of product teams need to publish, document, and version APIs on a shared platform. A platform team of eight owns the standards. Teams must ship without waiting on that team, and the result still has to look like one coherent API surface rather than dozens of independently-invented ones." Every other example on this page is bound by traffic. This one is bound by people: the binding constraint is that a small team cannot manually review everything, and the failure mode isn't an outage — it's an API surface that drifted into inconsistency one reasonable local decision at a time.

1
Requirements
Functional
  • A product team can publish a new API version without the platform team performing a manual step.
  • Publishing generates documentation, client SDKs, a mock server, and the gateway's route configuration from one source.
  • Consumers can browse a catalog, find an API, see who owns it, and get credentials to call it.
  • Changes that would break existing consumers are caught and blocked before publication.
  • Every published API is discoverable with consistent metadata — ownership, lifecycle stage, support contact.
Non-functional
  • The platform team must not be in the critical path of any team's release.
  • Standards must be enforced consistently — the same rule cannot be applied strictly to one team and waived for another.
  • Documentation must never drift from the API's actual behaviour.
  • A team must be able to tell, before merging, whether a change is breaking — not discover it from a consumer's incident report.

Worth asking: is the spec written by hand, or generated from the implementation? It decides whether drift is a discipline problem or a structural impossibility. We'll treat the spec as the committed artifact that both code and docs derive from, and say why below.

2
Core Entities
  • ApiProduct (id, name, owning_team, support_contact, lifecycle_stage) — the logical API a consumer subscribes to, stable across versions.
  • ApiVersion (product_id, semver, spec_document, status, published_at) — one immutable published spec; status is Draft, Published, Deprecated, or Retired.
  • PolicyRule (id, severity, applies_to, check) — one machine-checkable standard; severity decides whether a violation warns or blocks.
  • Subscription (consumer_id, product_id, version_range, credentials_id) — who is calling what, which is also the list of who a breaking change would hurt.
3
API

POST /versions → submit a spec for validation and publication (called from CI, not clicked in a UI)
POST /versions/validate → run policy and compatibility checks without publishing
GET /catalog?q= → search published APIs by name, team, or domain
POST /subscriptions → request access, issuing credentials against the auth service from example Q

4
Data Flow — genuinely earns its place here
  1. A team commits a spec change; CI calls validate before the pull request can merge.
  2. Policy rules run against the spec, and the spec is diffed against the currently published version to classify the change.
  3. A breaking change without a version bump fails the build — the team sees it in their own pipeline, not in someone else's incident.
  4. On merge, publication generates docs, SDKs, a mock server, and the gateway route config from that one spec.
  5. Subscribed consumers of the affected product are notified, with the deprecation timeline if one applies.
5
High-Level Design

One spec artifact fans out into every downstream output, and the only gate is automated — diagrammed below.

6
Deep Dives

Three below: (a) the spec as single source of truth, (b) governance without a review queue, and (c) detecting breaking changes programmatically.

The platform team appears nowhere in this flow — that's the design goal, not an omission. Their leverage is expressed as policy rules inside the CI gate, applied identically to every team, rather than as a review queue that scales with headcount.
A team ships a new version
  1. They edit the spec in their own repository and open a pull request.
  2. CI runs policy rules and diffs the spec against the published version; violations and breaking changes appear as build failures with specific line references.
  3. They fix what failed and merge — no ticket filed, no approval waited on.
  4. Publication regenerates docs, SDKs, mocks, and gateway routes from the merged spec, and notifies subscribed consumers.
A consumer adopts an API
  1. They search the catalog and find the API, its owning team, and its lifecycle stage.
  2. They generate an SDK in their language and point it at the mock server, building before the real API is even deployed.
  3. They subscribe, which issues credentials through the auth service from example Q.
  4. That subscription is also how they'll be notified if a future version deprecates something they depend on.
Key deep dive — the spec as single source of truth
FROne source generates all outputsNFRDocs never drift from behaviour
Thought process
  1. Start with the common arrangement: code is written, and documentation is maintained separately alongside it. Ask what happens under normal pressure — a field is added in code, the doc update is deferred, and the docs are now subtly wrong with no signal that they are.
  2. Notice that "keep them in sync" is a discipline requirement, and discipline requirements fail silently and eventually always. The goal should be making drift structurally impossible, not asking people to prevent it.
  3. Pick one artifact to be authoritative and derive everything else from it. If docs are generated from the spec, a stale doc cannot exist — there's no second place for the truth to live.
  4. Extend the same logic outward: gateway routes, client SDKs, and mock servers are all restatements of the same contract. Each one hand-maintained is another surface that can disagree, so generate them all.

The value isn't that generation saves typing — it's that a derived artifact cannot contradict its source. Drift stops being something to police.

Illustration — what one spec change producesTeam adds an optional "currency" field to the invoice response. One commit to invoices.openapi.yaml, and on merge: → docs regenerated, field appears with its description → SDK (Go) regenerated, struct gains a Currency field → SDK (TS) regenerated, type gains currency?: string → mock server now returns the field, so consumers can test against it → gateway route response schema updated (feeds example S's route config) → catalog version bumped, subscribers notified None of these could disagree with each other, because none of them is maintained. The alternative — six places a human must remember to update — is six chances for the docs to start lying.
  • Spec-first or code-first are both defensible; maintaining both separately is not. Generating the spec from annotated code is equally valid — what matters is that exactly one of them is authored and the other derived.
  • The mock server is the underrated output — it lets a consuming team build and test against an API before the producing team has deployed anything, which removes a sequencing dependency between teams that would otherwise be real calendar time.
Key deep dive — governance without a review queue
FRPublish without a manual stepNFRPlatform team off the critical pathNFRStandards applied consistently
Thought process
  1. Write down the two requirements and notice they appear to contradict: standards must be enforced, and the team enforcing them must not be a bottleneck.
  2. Test manual review against arithmetic. Thirty teams, each shipping weekly, is roughly 120 reviews a month landing on eight people who also have their own roadmap. Review latency becomes every team's release latency.
  3. Test the opposite — publish anything, no checks. That produces exactly the inconsistency the platform exists to prevent: three date formats, four pagination styles, no two error envelopes alike.
  4. Notice what the contradiction actually rests on: the assumption that enforcement requires a human. Most real API standards are mechanically checkable — naming, pagination shape, error format, required metadata.
  5. Move enforcement into CI as executable rules, and keep humans for the genuinely ambiguous minority: is this the right resource model, does this belong in this API at all. Those are design conversations, not gates.

Automated enforcement is more consistent than human review, not just faster — a linter cannot be worn down at the end of a quarter, and it applies the same rule to the most senior team and the newest one.

Illustration — standards as executable rulesERROR (blocks the build) - every 4xx/5xx response uses the shared error envelope schema - every list endpoint declares cursor pagination, not page/offset - every operation declares required scopes (feeds example Q) - x-owning-team and x-support-contact present on the spec WARNING (visible, doesn't block) - operationId does not follow the house naming convention - description missing on a public field A rule's severity IS the governance decision. Promoting a rule from warning to error is how a standard becomes mandatory — a config change the platform team makes once, not a position they argue in each review. New rules ship as warnings first, so existing APIs aren't retroactively broken; they graduate to errors once the estate has caught up.
  • The paved road matters more than the rules — a template repository that already passes every check, with scaffolding and CI wired in, means the compliant path is also the fastest path. Standards enforced only by rejection produce resentment; standards that arrive pre-satisfied produce adoption.
  • Exceptions need a mechanism, not a culture of asking — a documented, expiring waiver recorded in the spec is far healthier than a rule everyone knows is negotiable over chat.

This is the same instinct as the API gateway's config validation, applied to organizational scale instead of runtime: encode the constraint once, apply it uniformly, and keep the humans for decisions that genuinely need judgment.

Key deep dive — detecting breaking changes programmatically
FRBlock changes that break consumersNFRKnown before merge, not from an incident
Thought process
  1. Start from how this usually goes wrong: a team removes a field they believe nobody uses, ships it, and finds out from a consumer's incident. The information needed to prevent that existed the whole time.
  2. Notice that "breaking" is not a judgement call — it has a precise definition. A change is breaking if a request that was valid before is now rejected, or a response a consumer could rely on no longer arrives.
  3. Since both the old and new spec are machine-readable, that definition is computable. Diff them and classify each difference against the rule.
  4. Wire the classification to the version number: a breaking change without a major-version bump fails the build. The team isn't blocked from making the change — they're blocked from making it silently.
  5. Close the loop with the subscription data, which already records who consumes what. A team proposing a breaking change can be shown the actual list of teams it would affect, turning an abstract rule into a concrete conversation.

Compatibility is usually treated as a matter of care and communication. It's mostly a diff, and treating it as one moves the discovery from production to the pull request.

Illustration — classifying a spec diffBREAKING — requires a major version bump - remove an endpoint, or a response field - add a required request parameter - narrow a type (string → enum) - tighten validation (maxLength 200 → 50) - remove an enum value the response could previously return - change an error code for an existing condition NON-BREAKING — safe in a minor version - add a new endpoint - add an OPTIONAL request parameter - add a response field (consumers ignoring unknown fields) - widen a type (enum → string) - relax validation (maxLength 50 → 200) - add a new enum value to a REQUEST enum The asymmetry is the whole rule: widening what you ACCEPT is safe, widening what you RETURN is not (a consumer may switch exhaustively on the values you previously promised). CI output on violation: ✗ BREAKING: removed response field 'invoice.legacy_id' version 2.4.0 → 2.5.0 is a minor bump required: 3.0.0 affected subscribers: billing-ui, partner-sync, reconciliation-job
  • Deprecation is the path a breaking change should usually take — mark the field deprecated in the spec, keep serving it, notify subscribers, and remove it in the next major only after usage telemetry shows nobody is calling it. The subscription list tells you who to warn; request metrics tell you when it's actually safe.
  • The rule is only as good as consumers tolerating unknown fields — adding a response field is only non-breaking if clients ignore what they don't recognise. Generated SDKs make that true by default, which is a real second-order argument for generating them rather than letting each team hand-roll a client.

Does it hold up?

RequirementHow
Platform team off the critical pathThe only gate is automated CI; the team's leverage lives in policy rules rather than in a review queue that scales with their headcount.
Standards applied consistentlyRules are executable and run identically for every team, so enforcement can't vary by relationship, seniority, or deadline pressure.
Docs never driftEvery downstream artifact is generated from the spec, so there's no second place for the truth to live and disagree.
Breaking changes caught before mergeThe spec diff is classified against a precise definition and checked against the version bump, with affected subscribers named in the failure.
APIs are discoverable with real ownershipOwnership metadata is an error-severity policy rule, so an API without a named owner cannot be published at all.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
Automated gates vs. human reviewScales to any number of teams and applies rules uniformly, at the cost of only catching what's mechanically expressible — genuine design problems ("is this the right resource model") pass CI cleanly and still need a human, just not as a gate.
Generate everything from the spec vs. hand-maintainMakes drift structurally impossible, at the cost of expressiveness — hand-written docs can explain context and intent that a generated page can't, which is why narrative guides usually live alongside generated reference.
Blocking breaking changes in CI vs. trusting reviewMoves discovery from a consumer's incident to the author's pull request, at the cost of occasional false positives on changes that are technically breaking but genuinely unused — which is what an expiring, recorded waiver is for.
Spec-first vs. code-first generationSpec-first makes the contract a deliberate design artifact reviewed before implementation; code-first guarantees the spec matches what actually runs. Either works — maintaining both by hand is the only genuinely wrong answer.

Tools you might reach for

ComponentReal-world options
Spec formatOpenAPI for REST, Protobuf for gRPC — both machine-readable enough to diff and generate from
Policy lintingSpectral with custom rulesets, or Buf lint for Protobuf
Breaking-change detectionoasdiff for OpenAPI, Buf breaking for Protobuf — both classify diffs rather than just showing them
SDK & mock generationOpenAPI Generator, Prism for mock servers
Portal & catalogBackstage for the service catalog, or a managed portal from the gateway vendor
Worked Example U — The Caller Is Not a Person

Design an MCP Gateway for Internal Tools

audience binding, not passthrough · step-up scopes · agent traffic shape
Click to expand

"Expose a company's internal APIs as tools an AI agent can call, over the Model Context Protocol. The agent acts on behalf of a real user, and must never be able to do more than that user could." The routing looks like the API Gateway example, but the caller changes three things that matter: the agent decides at runtime which tools to invoke rather than following a fixed integration, it can call far more aggressively than any human, and a token that leaks through it grants an autonomous process access to real systems. The protocol's own answer to the middle problem — an MCP server is an OAuth resource server, not its own auth system — is what makes this connect cleanly to the OAuth Authorization Service example rather than reinventing it.

1
Requirements
Functional
  • Expose selected internal API operations as MCP tools, with machine-readable input schemas an agent can reason about.
  • Authenticate the calling agent and authorize each tool invocation against the acting user's permissions.
  • Tell a client exactly which additional permissions an operation needs when its current token is insufficient, rather than failing opaquely.
  • Record every invocation — which agent, which user, which tool, which arguments — as an audit trail.
Non-functional
  • A token presented to the gateway must be valid for the gateway specifically, and must never be forwarded onward to a backend.
  • An agent must never be able to invoke an operation the acting user could not perform directly.
  • An agent looping or retrying aggressively must not be able to exhaust a backend's capacity.
  • Tool results must be bounded in size, since an unbounded response consumes the agent's context window rather than just bandwidth.

Worth asking: does the agent act on behalf of a user, or as itself? It changes the authorization model completely. We'll assume on behalf of a user — the harder and more common case, and the one where the confused-deputy risk below is real.

2
Core Entities
  • ToolDefinition (name, description, input_schema, required_scopes, target_operation) — one exposed capability; required_scopes is what turns a tool call into an authorization decision.
  • ProtectedResourceMetadata (canonical_uri, authorization_servers[], scopes_supported) — how a client discovers which authorization server issues tokens for this gateway.
  • AccessToken (audience, subject, scopes, expires_at) — audience is the load-bearing field: it binds the token to this gateway and nowhere else.
  • InvocationRecord (id, agent_client_id, acting_user, tool_name, arguments_digest, outcome, at) — the audit trail, which matters more here than for human-driven APIs because the caller is autonomous.
3
API

The protocol defines the surface; the gateway implements it as a resource server:
GET /.well-known/oauth-protected-resource → metadata pointing at the authorization server
POST /mcp → tool listing and invocation, with Authorization: Bearer required on every request
Failures are specific: 401 when a token is missing or invalid, 403 with a scope challenge when it's valid but insufficient.

4
Data Flow — genuinely earns its place here
  1. An unauthenticated call returns 401 with a pointer to the gateway's protected-resource metadata.
  2. The client discovers the authorization server, completes an OAuth flow, and requests a token whose audience is this gateway's canonical URI.
  3. The client calls a tool; the gateway validates the token's signature, expiry, and — critically — that its audience is this gateway.
  4. If the token's scopes don't cover the tool, the gateway returns 403 naming the scopes required, and the client re-authorizes for the union of old and new scopes.
  5. On success the gateway exchanges the agent's token for a separate backend credential, calls the internal API, bounds the result, and records the invocation.
5
High-Level Design

The token the agent holds and the credential the backend sees are deliberately never the same thing — diagrammed below.

6
Deep Dives

Three below: (a) audience binding and why passthrough is forbidden, (b) step-up authorization for runtime scope gaps, and (c) what changes when the caller is an autonomous agent.

The red boundary is the point of the design: the agent's token is validated and then stopped. What reaches the internal API is a separately minted credential scoped to that backend, so a token stolen from the agent cannot be replayed against anything behind the gateway.
An agent invokes a tool it's allowed to use
  1. It presents a bearer token whose audience is this gateway's canonical URI.
  2. The gateway verifies the signature against cached keys, checks expiry, and confirms the audience matches itself.
  3. The tool's required scopes are satisfied, so the gateway mints a separate backend credential carrying the acting user's identity.
  4. The internal API is called, the result is truncated to a bounded size, and the invocation is recorded with agent, user, tool, and argument digest.
An agent reaches for a tool it lacks scope for
  1. The token is valid — correct audience, unexpired — but its scopes don't cover invoices:write.
  2. The gateway returns 403 with insufficient_scope and names every scope the operation needs, in one challenge.
  3. The client re-authorizes for the union of what it already had and what was just named — so existing permissions aren't dropped.
  4. It retries once with the upgraded token, under a retry limit, and treats repeated failure as permanent rather than looping.
Key deep dive — audience binding, and why token passthrough is forbidden
NFRToken valid for this gateway onlyNFRNever forwarded to a backend
Thought process
  1. Start with the design a gateway naturally suggests: take the caller's token and forward it to the backend, so the backend sees the real user. It's less code and it preserves identity.
  2. Ask what that token now is. It's a credential that a backend accepts, held by an autonomous process that decides its own next action — and any component it touches can replay it.
  3. Name the specific attack: a malicious or compromised MCP server handed a token for a different service can present it there and act as the user. The gateway becomes a confused deputy, lending its trust to a caller that shouldn't have it.
  4. Close it at the token itself, not with policy: bind every token to exactly one audience, and have each resource server verify the token was issued for it. A forwarded token then fails at the next hop by construction.
  5. Accept the consequence — identity must be re-established at each hop deliberately, by exchanging the validated token for a new backend credential rather than passing the original along.

This is the protocol's sharpest requirement and the one a naive gateway violates first: a token presented here must have been issued for here, and must not be passed onward.

Illustration — passthrough vs. exchangePASSTHROUGH (forbidden) agent token aud = "https://mcp.internal/mcp" gateway forwards it verbatim to the billing API → billing API must now accept tokens minted for something else → any component holding that token can call billing as the user → a compromised tool server replays it elsewhere: confused deputy EXCHANGE (required) agent token aud = "https://mcp.internal/mcp" ← validated, then STOPS gateway mints: backend token aud = "https://billing.internal" sub = the acting user scopes = only what THIS tool needs → billing only ever accepts tokens minted for billing → the agent's token is worthless anywhere but the gateway → a stolen backend token is scoped to one operation, not the user's whole permission set The audience check is what makes the boundary real. Without it, "don't forward tokens" is a convention; with it, forwarding simply fails.
  • Downscope at exchange, don't just re-sign — the backend credential should carry only the scopes this specific tool needs, so a leak at the backend hop is narrower than the agent's own grant.
  • The acting user must survive the exchange — the whole authorization model rests on the agent being unable to exceed the user, so the new credential carries the user as subject, not the gateway's own service identity.

Same instinct as the auth service example keeping private keys off the gateway: the component in the middle should hold as little transferable authority as the design allows.

Key deep dive — step-up authorization for runtime scope gaps
FRName the permissions an operation needsNFRAgent can't exceed the acting user
Thought process
  1. Notice what's different about an agent: a normal integration is written against a known set of endpoints, so its permissions can be granted once at setup. An agent chooses tools at runtime based on a task nobody enumerated in advance.
  2. Consider granting every scope upfront to avoid the problem. That hands an autonomous process the user's entire permission surface for a task that may need one read — the opposite of least privilege.
  3. Consider the other extreme: a bare 403. The agent knows it failed but not what would fix it, so it either gives up or guesses — and guessing means retry loops against a wall.
  4. Make the failure informative instead: return the specific scopes this operation requires, so the client can request exactly those and nothing more.
  5. Catch the subtle bug that follows — re-authorizing for only the newly named scopes silently drops previously granted ones. The client must request the union, or fixing one tool breaks another.

Least privilege is usually a static decision made at integration time. With an agent choosing its own path, permissions have to be acquired incrementally at runtime — which only works if failures say what they need.

Illustration — the challenge, and the union that must followAgent holds: scope = "invoices:read" Agent attempts: create_invoice (needs invoices:write) Gateway responds: HTTP/1.1 403 Forbidden WWW-Authenticate: Bearer error="insufficient_scope", scope="invoices:write", resource_metadata="https://mcp.internal/.well-known/ oauth-protected-resource" Client re-authorizes for the UNION: "invoices:read invoices:write" ✓ keeps what it had NOT just "invoices:write" ✗ silently loses read access, breaking the next read call Then retries ONCE, under a limit. Repeated insufficient_scope on the same tool is a permanent failure, not something to loop on — an agent that retries indefinitely turns a permissions bug into a traffic incident. Servers should name ALL scopes an operation needs in a single challenge. Trickling them out one at a time forces a fresh authorization round trip per missing scope, and each one may prompt the user.
  • Scope hierarchies must be honoured on the server side — if a broader scope implies a narrower one, the gateway has to treat the broader grant as sufficient, or it will challenge for permissions the client already effectively holds.
  • Every challenge is potentially a user prompt — for an agent acting on someone's behalf, a scope upgrade may surface as a consent screen, which is why batching scopes into one challenge is a user-experience decision and not only a protocol nicety.
Key deep dive — what changes when the caller is an autonomous agent
NFRAggressive retries can't exhaust a backendNFRTool results bounded in sizeFRFull audit trail
Thought process
  1. Compare the traffic shape to a human-driven client. A person clicks, waits, reads, and thinks; the gap between their requests is measured in seconds and bounded by attention.
  2. Remove that pacing. An agent in a reasoning loop can issue calls as fast as they return, and a loop that isn't converging doesn't get bored — it retries at machine speed until something stops it.
  3. Notice the existing per-consumer rate limit is the wrong unit here: one agent session behaving pathologically is a legitimate consumer by that measure, so the limit needs to key on the session, not just the client.
  4. Find the cost that's unique to this caller: a large tool result doesn't just consume bandwidth, it consumes the agent's context window — so an unbounded response degrades the agent's ability to function, not just the network.
  5. Raise the stakes on auditing accordingly. When a caller both chooses its own actions and acts for someone else, reconstructing what happened afterwards is a requirement, not an operational nicety.

Routing and auth carry over from the API gateway example. What doesn't carry over is the assumption of a caller paced by human attention.

Illustration — three limits a human-facing gateway wouldn't need1. PER-SESSION call budget (not just per-client) one agent session: max 50 tool calls per task → a non-converging reasoning loop stops itself → per-CLIENT limits alone can't see this: the client is legitimate, it's this one session that's pathological 2. BOUNDED tool results list_invoices returns 40,000 rows → truncate to 100 + total_count + a cursor to continue → the constraint is the agent's context window, not bandwidth. An unbounded result doesn't slow the agent down, it crowds out the reasoning it needed the data for. 3. CONFIRMATION on destructive tools delete_customer, issue_refund → flagged as requiring explicit human approval before execution, regardless of scope → scope answers "may this user do it"; confirmation answers "did a person actually intend it, this time"
  • Tool descriptions are an attack surface, not just documentation — the agent chooses what to call based on what the description says, so a tool whose text can be influenced by untrusted data is a path to steering the agent. Descriptions should be authored and reviewed like code, not generated from user-controlled content.
  • Argument digests, not raw arguments, in the audit log — full fidelity is tempting for debugging, but tool arguments routinely contain customer data, and an audit log is one of the least-guarded places for it to accumulate.
  • The stateless protocol core is what makes ordinary scaling work — with no session affinity required, gateway instances stay interchangeable behind a normal load balancer, exactly like the API gateway's stateless data plane.

The general lesson worth carrying: the limits a system needs come from the caller's behaviour, not the protocol. Rate limiting keyed on the wrong unit looks like protection while providing none.

Does it hold up?

RequirementHow
Token valid for this gateway onlyAudience is verified against the gateway's own canonical URI on every request; a token minted for anything else is rejected.
Never forwarded to a backendThe agent's token is validated and stops there; a separately minted, downscoped credential carrying the acting user reaches the backend instead.
Agent can't exceed the acting userEvery tool declares required scopes, checked against the user's granted scopes; the exchanged credential carries the user as subject, never the gateway's own identity.
Aggressive retries can't exhaust a backendPer-session call budgets on top of per-client limits, plus the API gateway's per-backend bulkheads underneath.
Results boundedTool responses are truncated with a total count and a continuation cursor, because the binding constraint is context window rather than bandwidth.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
Token exchange vs. passthroughContains a leaked token to a single hop and lets each backend credential be narrowly scoped, at the cost of an exchange step per call and identity plumbing the gateway must get right.
Incremental step-up vs. granting all scopes upfrontKeeps the agent at least privilege for the task it's actually doing, at the cost of mid-task authorization round trips that may prompt the user.
Bounded tool results vs. returning everythingProtects the agent's context window, at the cost of the agent needing to paginate deliberately — and of occasionally truncating something it genuinely needed, which is why the total count matters as much as the cursor.
Human confirmation on destructive toolsPrevents an autonomous process from executing an irreversible action on a misread intent, at the cost of breaking full automation exactly where automation would be most valuable.

Tools you might reach for

ComponentReal-world options
ProtocolMCP over HTTP — the gateway implements the server role as an OAuth 2.1 resource server
Authorization serveran existing IdP (Okta, Auth0, Keycloak) — the spec deliberately keeps the AS out of scope so you reuse rather than build
Token exchangeRFC 8693 token exchange against the same authorization server
Per-session budgetsRedis counters keyed by session, layered on the per-client limits from the Rate Limiter example
Audit trailappend-only storage with restricted access — Kafka into a warehouse, or a write-once log store
Worked Example V — Within Your Limit, Still Not Fair

Design Multi-Tenant Rate Limiting

quota ≠ rate · oversubscription and fairness · hierarchical limits
Click to expand

"A shared platform serving hundreds of tenants — internal teams and external customers — off the same backend capacity. Each has an agreed allowance. No tenant may degrade another's experience." The Rate Limiter example already settled how to count: which algorithm, where the counter lives, what happens when the counter store fails. None of that is what makes multi-tenancy hard. What's hard is that allowances are deliberately oversubscribed, which means a tenant can sit entirely inside their own limit and still starve everyone else — so per-tenant limits, the obvious answer, turn out not to deliver the thing they appear to promise.

1
Requirements
Functional
  • Enforce a short-term rate (requests per second) and a long-term quota (requests per billing period) per tenant.
  • Apply limits at several levels — organisation, team, and individual API key — with a request needing to pass all of them.
  • Tell a rejected caller which limit they hit, what it is, and when it resets.
  • Let a tenant burst above their nominal share when the platform is otherwise idle.
Non-functional
  • No tenant operating within its own limit may cause another tenant's requests to fail.
  • Limit checks must add negligible latency, since every request pays them.
  • Quota accounting must be accurate enough to bill against; rate limiting need only be approximately right.
  • A parent-level limit must be enforceable without every child funnelling through one contended counter.

Worth asking: is the sum of all tenant limits within actual capacity? Almost never — platforms oversubscribe deliberately, because tenants are rarely busy simultaneously. That single fact is what makes the fairness deep dive necessary rather than theoretical.

2
Core Entities
  • Tenant (id, parent_id, tier, rate_limit, quota_limit) — parent_id is what makes limits hierarchical; tier drives priority when capacity is contended.
  • RateCounter (scope_key, window_start, count) — short-lived, approximate, discarded every window.
  • QuotaLedger (tenant_id, period, consumed, reconciled_at) — durable and billable; a different storage decision entirely from the rate counter.
  • CapacityAllocation (tenant_id, guaranteed_share, current_usage) — the floor a tenant is entitled to when the platform is contended, distinct from the ceiling their limit describes.
3
API

Enforcement is inline, not a service call, but limits need a management surface:
GET /tenants/{id}/usage → current rate consumption and quota remaining
PUT /tenants/{id}/limits → adjust allowances
On rejection the response carries 429 with X-RateLimit-Limit, X-RateLimit-Remaining, X-RateLimit-Reset, and which scope was breached

4
Data Flow — genuinely earns its place here
  1. After authentication the tenant is known, so the request resolves to its full hierarchy — key, team, organisation.
  2. Rate counters are checked at every level against locally held state, with rejection at the first level that's exhausted.
  3. If the platform is currently contended, the tenant's guaranteed share is consulted — a tenant below its floor is admitted ahead of one above it.
  4. Quota is decremented on the durable ledger asynchronously, since billing accuracy tolerates a short delay but request latency does not.
5
High-Level Design

Two mechanisms that look similar and are storage-wise opposites, plus a fairness layer neither provides alone — diagrammed below.

6
Deep Dives

Three below: (a) why quota and rate are different mechanisms, (b) oversubscription and the noisy neighbour, and (c) hierarchical limits without a contended parent counter.

Rate and quota take deliberately opposite storage decisions — one in-memory and disposable, the other durable and reconciled — because approximate is fine for protecting infrastructure and unacceptable for billing. Step 3 is the part per-tenant limits don't give you: a tenant within its own ceiling can still be taking capacity another tenant was promised.
An ordinary request
  1. Authentication has already resolved the caller, so the tenant hierarchy — key, team, organisation — is known.
  2. Rate counters are checked at each level in ascending cost order; the first exhausted level rejects, naming which one it was.
  3. The platform isn't contended, so the fairness check is a no-op and the tenant may burst above its nominal share.
  4. The request is forwarded, and quota consumption is recorded asynchronously against the durable ledger.
Under contention
  1. Tenant B bursts hard — still inside its own configured rate limit, so no per-tenant check rejects it.
  2. Aggregate backend utilisation crosses the contention threshold, which activates the fairness layer.
  3. B is now well above its guaranteed share while A and C are below theirs, so B's requests are shed first.
  4. A and C continue to be served at their guaranteed floors, and B returns to full burst capacity the moment contention clears.
Key deep dive — quota and rate are different mechanisms
FREnforce both rate and quotaNFRQuota billable, rate approximate
Thought process
  1. Notice both are "count requests and compare to a limit," which is exactly why they get built as one mechanism with a configurable window.
  2. Ask what each one is actually protecting. Rate protects infrastructure from a burst it can't absorb; quota enforces a commercial agreement about total consumption.
  3. Derive the accuracy requirement from that. Rejecting one request too many at 1,000/sec is invisible; miscounting a month's usage shows up on an invoice and in a support ticket.
  4. Derive the storage from the accuracy requirement. Approximate and seconds-lived means in-memory and disposable; billable and month-lived means durable and reconcilable — opposite decisions.
  5. Derive the failure behaviour too. If the rate counter store is unavailable, serving traffic un-limited for a moment is usually right; if the quota ledger is unavailable, silently not charging is a revenue problem, so it buffers and reconciles rather than dropping the record.

They look like one mechanism with two window sizes. They are two mechanisms with opposite requirements on accuracy, storage, and what to do when the store is down.

Illustration — same shape, opposite decisions RATE QUOTA purpose protect infrastructure enforce the contract window 1s – 1min a billing period accuracy needed approximate exact enough to bill storage in-memory, disposable durable, reconciled on counter loss fail open (serve) buffer and replay — a brief gap in — never silently drop protection is a billable event survivable enforcement synchronous, inline asynchronous decrement, with a periodic check against the ledger Building one mechanism for both forces a single answer to every row. Whichever you pick is wrong for the other: durable storage on the hot path makes rate limiting slow, and in-memory counters make quota unbillable.
  • Quota enforcement can lag slightly and still be correct — a tenant marginally exceeding their monthly allowance before the async decrement catches up is a rounding error against a month, not a breach worth paying hot-path latency to prevent.
  • The two failures should be communicated differently — a rate rejection is transient and should encourage retry after a reset; a quota rejection is not retryable until the period rolls or the plan changes, and saying so saves the caller a pointless retry loop.
Key deep dive — oversubscription and the noisy neighbour
NFRNo tenant degrades anotherFRBurst when the platform is idle
Thought process
  1. Start from what per-tenant limits actually guarantee: a ceiling on any single tenant's consumption. Ask whether that's the same as the requirement, which is that no tenant degrades another.
  2. Check whether the limits even sum to capacity. They don't — platforms oversubscribe on purpose, because provisioning for everyone's ceiling at once would mean paying for capacity that sits idle almost always.
  3. Follow that to its consequence: if the sum exceeds capacity, there exists a state where every tenant is inside its own limit and the backend is still saturated. Per-tenant limits cannot detect this, because no limit has been breached.
  4. Consider the naive fix — shrink every limit until they sum to capacity. That destroys the thing oversubscription bought: nobody can ever burst, and the platform runs mostly idle to guarantee a worst case that rarely happens.
  5. Separate ceiling from floor. A limit is the most a tenant may use; a guaranteed share is the least they're entitled to when capacity is contended. Enforce the ceiling always, and the floor only when contended.

The requirement and the mechanism don't match: per-tenant limits bound each tenant individually, while fairness is a property of how they interact — and the gap between the two only becomes visible under contention.

Illustration — everyone inside their limit, platform saturatedBackend capacity: 1,000 req/s Tenant limits: A 400 B 400 C 400 (sum = 1,200 — oversubscribed) Normal hour: A 120 B 90 C 140 = 350 total → nobody notices and any of them could burst to 400 freely. This is the whole point of oversubscribing. Bad hour: A 120 B 400 C 140 = 660 → still fine B 400 is legal. No limit breached. Worse: A 380 B 400 C 390 = 1,170 > 1,000 capacity → EVERY tenant is inside its own limit → the backend is saturated anyway → whoever's request lands last simply fails → per-tenant limits detected nothing, because nothing was over its limit FAIRNESS LAYER — only activates above a contention threshold: guaranteed shares: A 333 B 333 C 333 (sum = capacity) at saturation, shed from whoever is furthest ABOVE their share → B at 400 is +67 over; A at 380 is +47; C at 390 is +57 → B sheds first, then C, then A — proportional, not first-come → below the threshold the layer is inert and bursting is unrestricted
  • Work-conserving is what makes this acceptable — capacity reserved for an idle tenant isn't held empty; it's lent to whoever wants it and reclaimed when the owner returns. A tenant only loses burst capacity at the moment someone else genuinely needs their floor.
  • Tier is how commercial reality enters the algorithm — guaranteed shares need not be equal. A paid tier's floor can exceed a free tier's, which makes "fair" a business decision expressed as a parameter rather than an assumption baked into the mechanism.
  • The contention signal must come from the backend, not the limiter — the limiter counts requests, which says nothing about whether the backend is struggling. Real utilisation or latency is the trigger, or the fairness layer activates at the wrong moments.

Same structure as the API gateway's bulkheads: bounding each consumer individually isn't sufficient, because the resource they contend for is shared. The gateway's answer was per-backend compartments; here it's a floor that only binds when the shared resource is actually scarce.

Key deep dive — hierarchical limits without a contended parent counter
FRLimits at org, team, and key levelNFRParent limit without one hot counterNFRNegligible latency
Thought process
  1. Write down what hierarchy requires: a request consumes budget at every level it belongs to, and any exhausted level rejects it.
  2. Notice the structural problem that creates. The organisation counter is incremented by every request from every key beneath it, so the top of the hierarchy is by construction the hottest key in the system.
  3. Reject the obvious response of sharding that counter — the caching playbook's split-the-key trick works for a hot key, but here the contention is inherent to the hierarchy rather than incidental, so it reappears one level down.
  4. Question whether the parent counter needs to be exact. It doesn't: rate limiting was already established as approximate, so a parent limit enforced to within a small margin is entirely adequate.
  5. Use that. Each limiter instance leases a slice of the parent budget, spends it locally with no coordination, and returns for more when it runs low — turning one counter hammered per request into one occasionally-refilled lease per instance.

Hierarchy is easy to specify and quietly creates the worst possible access pattern: the least granular counter receives the most traffic.

Illustration — leasing a slice instead of sharing a counterOrg limit: 10,000 req/s, across 20 limiter instances NAIVE — one shared counter every request from every key in the org increments org:acme → 10,000 increments/sec on one key, plus cross-instance coordination → the hottest key in the system is the one with the least granularity LEASED — each instance checks out a slice instance 1 leases 500 req/s for the next 2 seconds → spends it locally, zero coordination per request → at ~80% consumed, leases the next slice in the background → the org counter sees ~20 lease requests/sec, not 10,000 increments Overshoot: bounded by (instances × unspent lease) at worst — a few hundred requests over 10,000. For rate limiting, that's noise. For a QUOTA this margin would be unacceptable, which is exactly why quota is kept on the durable ledger rather than leased. Idle instances return unused lease early, so a fleet with skewed traffic doesn't strand budget in instances that aren't receiving requests.
  • Check levels cheapest-first — the key-level counter is local and uncontended, so checking it before the org-level lease means most rejections never touch shared state at all, the same ordering instinct as the API gateway's filter chain.
  • Name the breached level in the response — "you hit your team's limit, not your own" is the difference between a caller escalating internally and one filing a support ticket against the platform.

Does it hold up?

RequirementHow
No tenant degrades anotherGuaranteed shares enforced only under contention — per-tenant ceilings alone can't deliver this, since oversubscription makes "everyone legal, backend saturated" reachable.
Burst freely when idleThe fairness layer is inert below the contention threshold, and unused capacity is lent out rather than held empty.
Quota billable, rate approximateSeparate mechanisms: durable reconciled ledger decremented asynchronously, versus disposable in-memory counters checked inline.
Negligible latencyRate checks read local state; the parent-level budget is leased in slices rather than coordinated per request.
Parent limits without a hot counterInstances lease and spend slices locally, so the org counter sees lease requests rather than every individual increment.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
Oversubscribing vs. limits summing to capacityLets tenants burst and keeps hardware usefully utilised, at the cost of a reachable state where everyone is legal and the backend is saturated — which is precisely the state the fairness layer exists to handle.
Separate quota and rate mechanisms vs. one unified limiterEach gets storage and failure behaviour matched to its actual requirement, at the cost of two systems to operate where teams instinctively expect one.
Leased parent budget vs. an exact shared counterRemoves per-request coordination on the hottest key, at the cost of bounded overshoot — acceptable for rate, which is why quota deliberately doesn't use it.
Fairness only under contention vs. always enforcedPreserves bursting almost all the time, at the cost of behaviour that changes under load — a tenant's effective throughput depends on what everyone else is doing, which is harder to explain than a fixed limit.

Tools you might reach for

ComponentReal-world options
Rate countersin-process for the local slice, Redis for lease issuance and cross-instance state
Quota ledgerPostgreSQL fed by a durable stream (Kafka) so no billable event is lost if the writer is briefly down
Enforcement pointthe gateway's filter chain from the API Gateway example — after authentication, since tenant identity is required
Contention signalbackend utilisation or latency from Prometheus / Datadog, not request counts from the limiter itself
Fair queueingweighted fair queueing or deficit round robin, where each tenant's weight is its guaranteed share
Worked Example W — Shipping Is Easy, Retiring Is Hard

Design API Versioning & Migration

where the version lives · N versions, one implementation · actually retiring one
Click to expand

"A platform with hundreds of APIs and thousands of consumers needs to evolve its contracts without breaking anyone — and without accumulating versions forever." The Developer Portal example already settled how to detect that a change is breaking: diff the spec, classify it, gate the merge. This one starts after that gate. A breaking change is going to ship eventually, which means two or more versions run at once, and the interesting problems are all in that overlap: where the version identifier lives, how you avoid maintaining N copies of the same service, and how a version ever actually goes away — which is the part that reliably doesn't happen.

1
Requirements
Functional
  • Serve several major versions of an API concurrently from one deployment.
  • Let a consumer pin to a version and get stable behaviour until they choose to move.
  • Signal deprecation and a sunset date in-band, so a consumer learns from the API itself rather than an email they missed.
  • Report, per version, exactly which consumers are still calling it.
Non-functional
  • Supporting an additional version must not mean an additional copy of the business logic.
  • Version dispatch must add negligible latency, since every request is dispatched.
  • A version may only be retired against evidence of zero usage, never against a date alone.
  • The number of live versions must be bounded — unbounded accumulation is the failure mode this design exists to prevent.

Worth asking: how many concurrent major versions are acceptable? Deciding "two, plus a deprecated third during migration" up front turns retirement into a forcing function. Leaving it open means the answer becomes however many exist, forever.

2
Core Entities
  • ApiVersion (product_id, major, status, sunset_at, spec) — status is Active, Deprecated, or Retired; sunset_at is published, not internal.
  • VersionAdapter (from_version, to_version, request_transform, response_transform) — the translation that lets one implementation serve an older contract.
  • ConsumerUsage (consumer_id, product_id, version, last_called_at, call_count) — the evidence retirement decisions are made against.
  • SunsetPolicy (min_notice_period, max_concurrent_versions) — the commitments that make deprecation predictable rather than arbitrary.
3
API

GET /v2/invoices → version in the path, dispatched at the gateway
GET /products/{id}/versions → which versions exist, their status, and sunset dates
GET /products/{id}/usage?version=v1 → who is still calling a deprecated version
Deprecated responses carry Deprecation and Sunset headers on every call

4
Data Flow — genuinely earns its place here
  1. A request arrives carrying a version identifier; the gateway dispatches on it without the service needing to know.
  2. If the request targets an older version, an adapter transforms it into the current internal shape.
  3. One implementation — the current one — handles it, with no version-specific branching inside the business logic.
  4. The response is transformed back into the older contract's shape on the way out.
  5. The call is recorded against that consumer and version, which is what later makes retirement an evidence-based decision.
5
High-Level Design

Old versions live in translation layers at the edge, not in branches inside the service — diagrammed below.

6
Deep Dives

Three below: (a) where the version identifier belongs, (b) serving N versions from one implementation, and (c) why retirement fails and what makes it work.

Every old version is a translation layer at the edge, never a branch inside the service. That containment is what makes the cost of an extra version proportional to how much the contract changed, rather than to the size of the service behind it.
A consumer still on v1
  1. The request arrives at /v1/invoices; the gateway dispatches to the v1 adapter.
  2. The adapter renames fields, supplies defaults for parameters v1 never had, and calls the service in its current shape.
  3. The response is transformed back — fields v1 never knew about are dropped, renamed ones restored.
  4. Deprecation and Sunset headers are attached, and the call is recorded against this consumer so they show up in the migration report.
Retiring v1
  1. Usage telemetry shows three consumers still calling v1, named — not an anonymous count.
  2. Each is contacted directly with what they specifically need to change, since the diff between v1 and v2 is known precisely.
  3. Usage reaches zero and stays there through a full business cycle, covering monthly and quarterly jobs that a short window would miss.
  4. v1 returns 410 Gone for a grace period — an explicit, greppable failure — before the adapter is finally deleted.
Key deep dive — where the version identifier belongs
FRConsumers pin to a versionNFRDispatch adds negligible latency
Thought process
  1. List the places a version can live: the URL path, a custom header, the Accept header via content negotiation, or a query parameter.
  2. Judge them against who has to use them, not against protocol purity. A consumer debugging a failure needs to see the version in a log line, a curl command, and a bug report.
  3. Notice the practical asymmetry: a path segment is visible everywhere by default — browser, proxy logs, CDN cache keys, screenshots in tickets. A header is invisible unless someone thought to capture it.
  4. Weigh the standard objection to paths — that a URL should identify a resource, and the resource didn't change just because its representation did. It's correct, and it loses to operability.
  5. Settle it by scope: a major version in the path, because it changes the contract; minor evolution as additive changes needing no identifier at all.

The theoretically cleanest option and the one that survives production debugging are different options, and this is a case where the pragmatic answer is worth defending explicitly.

Version in the path — /v1/invoices

Visible in every log, cache key, and bug report without anyone configuring anything.

  • Trivially cacheable — different versions are different URLs, so no Vary header subtleties.
  • A consumer can paste a URL and it means one unambiguous thing.
  • Theoretically impure: the resource didn't change, only its representation.
Version in a header

Keeps URLs stable and resource-oriented, with the version as metadata about the representation.

  • Honest about what changed — a cleaner model on paper.
  • Invisible by default: absent from logs and bug reports unless deliberately captured.
  • Caching needs Vary handling, which is a recurring source of subtle bugs at the CDN layer.

The deeper decision underneath either choice: only major versions get an identifier at all. Versioning every additive change produces a version explosion where nothing is ever retired, which is the failure this whole example is organised around avoiding.

Key deep dive — serving N versions from one implementation
FRSeveral versions concurrentlyNFRNo duplicated business logic
Thought process
  1. Start with the approach teams reach for first: fork the service, or branch on version inside the handlers. Follow it forward a year.
  2. Trace the forked-service cost. A bug fix or security patch must now be applied, tested, and deployed N times, and the copies drift until behaviour differs in ways nobody intended.
  3. Trace the branching cost. Every handler accumulates version conditionals, so the cost of an old version is smeared across the whole codebase and nobody can see what removing it would involve.
  4. Notice what's actually different between versions: almost always the shape of the contract, not the behaviour. Field names, defaults, error formats — translation, not logic.
  5. Push the difference to the edge as an explicit adapter. The service implements the current contract only, and each old version is one reviewable, deletable unit.

The goal isn't just avoiding duplication — it's making an old version a discrete object you can point at and delete, rather than a condition scattered through code nobody wants to touch.

Illustration — the same change, three waysv1 called it "customer_name"; v2 splits it into "first_name" / "last_name". FORKED SERVICE invoices-v1/ ← full copy, own deploy, own dependencies invoices-v2/ ← every security patch applied twice, and they drift BRANCHING INSIDE if version == "v1" { resp.CustomerName = first + " " + last } ...repeated in every handler that touches a name → removing v1 means auditing the whole codebase for conditionals ADAPTER AT THE EDGE // adapters/v1/invoice.go — the ONLY file that knows v1 exists func ToV1(r Invoice) V1Invoice { return V1Invoice{ CustomerName: r.FirstName + " " + r.LastName } } func FromV1(req V1Request) Request { first, last := splitName(req.CustomerName) return Request{ FirstName: first, LastName: last } } → the service never knows v1 exists → retiring v1 is deleting one directory and one route → the adapter is small enough to actually review
  • Adapters compose across versions — a v1 request can translate to v2 and then to v3 rather than needing a direct v1-to-current mapping. Each step stays small, at the cost of a little latency through the chain.
  • The pattern's limit is worth naming honestly — it works when versions differ in shape. When they differ in behaviour — a genuinely different calculation, not a different field name — no translation layer helps, and that's the signal the change deserves a separate endpoint rather than a version.
  • Test old versions against their published spec — contract tests replayed through the adapter catch the failure mode that matters, where a refactor of current logic silently changes what an old adapter produces.
Key deep dive — why retirement fails, and what makes it work
FRSignal deprecation in-bandFRReport who still calls a versionNFRRetire on evidence, not a dateNFRBounded version count
Thought process
  1. Start from the observed outcome rather than the intended process: most platforms accumulate versions and retire almost none. Ask what actually blocks the last step.
  2. Notice it's rarely technical. Deleting an adapter is easy; the blocker is nobody being willing to assert that no one is still calling it.
  3. Trace that to its cause — without per-consumer usage data, retiring is a gamble on an unknown blast radius, and the rational move is always to wait.
  4. Invert it: make usage per consumer a first-class, queryable fact. Retirement stops being a risk decision and becomes a checklist of named teams to contact.
  5. Add the in-band signal, since the out-of-band one fails predictably. An email goes to whoever set the integration up years ago; a Sunset header reaches whoever is running it today.
  6. Guard the last step against the sampling error that bites here specifically: monthly and quarterly jobs are invisible in a two-week window of zero traffic.

Retirement fails for an informational reason, not a technical one — the deletion is trivial, and knowing it's safe is the entire problem.

Illustration — the retirement sequence1. DEPRECATE — status changes, and every v1 response now carries: Deprecation: Sat, 01 Nov 2025 00:00:00 GMT Sunset: Sat, 01 May 2026 00:00:00 GMT Link: ; rel="successor-version" → reaches whoever operates the integration TODAY, not whoever originally set it up and has since changed teams 2. IDENTIFY — usage telemetry names them: billing-ui 12,400 calls last: 2 min ago partner-sync 840 calls last: 1 hour ago legacy-reconciler 3 calls last: 40 days ago ← the dangerous one 3. MIGRATE — each is contacted with their specific diff, not a generic "please upgrade" 4. VERIFY — wait for zero usage across a FULL BUSINESS CYCLE. legacy-reconciler at 3 calls in 40 days is probably a quarterly job. Two weeks of silence proves nothing about it. 5. BRICK — v1 returns 410 Gone for a grace period before deletion. A loud, greppable, unambiguous failure beats a 404 that looks like a routing bug and gets debugged for a day. 6. DELETE — remove the adapter and the route. The forcing function: max_concurrent_versions. Shipping v3 REQUIRES v1 to be retired first, so retirement is a precondition for progress rather than cleanup that never reaches the top of anyone's backlog.
  • The bound is what actually drives retirement — without a hard ceiling on live versions, deprecation is unbounded in practice, because there is never a quarter where cleaning up an old version beats shipping something new.
  • 410 Gone rather than 404 — it says the resource existed and was intentionally removed, which points a debugging consumer at the migration guide instead of at their own routing config.
  • Brownout before blackout, for the stubborn cases — failing v1 for a few minutes at a scheduled hour surfaces the consumers who ignored every notice, while they're still able to react, rather than at final deletion.

Same shape as the developer portal's governance dive: the fix for a process that relies on goodwill isn't more reminders, it's a mechanism that makes the right outcome structural — there, a CI gate; here, a version ceiling.

Does it hold up?

RequirementHow
No duplicated business logicOne implementation of the current contract; every old version is a translation adapter at the edge.
Negligible dispatch latencyThe gateway routes on a path segment — the same in-memory route matching as the API Gateway example.
Consumers pin and stay stableA major version in the path is explicit and unambiguous, and additive changes never require a new one.
Retire on evidencePer-consumer usage telemetry names who is still calling, and zero usage must hold across a full business cycle.
Bounded version countA hard ceiling makes retiring the old version a precondition for shipping the new one.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
Version in the path vs. in a headerVisible everywhere by default and trivially cacheable, at the cost of a URL that identifies a representation rather than strictly a resource — operability beating purity, deliberately.
Adapters vs. forking the serviceOne codebase to patch and an old version you can delete in one commit, at the cost of translation logic that must itself be tested against the old contract.
Hard ceiling on live versions vs. open-ended deprecationForces retirement to actually happen, at the cost of occasionally pressuring a consumer to migrate on the platform's schedule rather than their own.
Evidence-based retirement vs. a fixed sunset dateAvoids breaking a low-frequency but critical consumer, at the cost of a retirement date that can slip — which is exactly why the version ceiling exists to bound the slippage.

Tools you might reach for

ComponentReal-world options
Version dispatchthe gateway's route table — a version is just a path prefix mapping to an adapter or straight through
Deprecation signallingRFC 8594 Sunset header, Deprecation header, and a successor-version Link
Usage telemetrygateway access logs aggregated by consumer and version into Datadog or a warehouse
Contract teststhe published spec from the Developer Portal example, replayed through each adapter in CI
Adaptersplain code in the service's own repo, or gateway-level transforms for purely structural changes
Worked Example X — You Cannot Ask Whether It Worked

Design an Idempotent Payment API

idempotency keys · double-entry ledger · saga compensation
Click to expand

"Move money between accounts, through an external processor, exactly once — under network timeouts, client retries, and partial failures." Every other example on this page tolerates approximation somewhere: a view count can drift, a registry entry can be stale, a rate limit can be off by a few. This one can't. A duplicated transfer is money that didn't exist, and the hard part is that the thing you need to know — did that request take effect? — is precisely the thing a timeout refuses to tell you.

1
Requirements
Functional
  • Submit a payment and receive a definitive outcome: succeeded, failed, or still processing.
  • A retried submission must never move money twice, however many times it is retried.
  • Every balance change is auditable — what moved, between which accounts, caused by what.
  • A payment spanning several services either completes fully or leaves no partial effect.
Non-functional
  • Exactly-once effect on balances, even when the client cannot tell whether its request arrived.
  • Balances must always be derivable from recorded history, never only from a mutable current value.
  • An in-flight payment must reach a terminal state without human intervention, including after a crash.
  • A processor's response arriving twice, or out of order, must not corrupt state.

Worth asking: must the caller get a synchronous yes or no? External processors take seconds and can be slow or unreachable, so holding a request open couples your availability to theirs. We'll accept the payment synchronously and settle it asynchronously — which makes the state machine below the core of the design rather than a detail.

2
Core Entities
  • IdempotencyRecord (key, request_fingerprint, status, response_body, expires_at) — the fingerprint is what makes key reuse with different content detectable rather than silently wrong.
  • Payment (id, idempotency_key, amount, currency, source, destination, state, processor_ref) — state is the lifecycle, not a flag.
  • LedgerEntry (id, transaction_id, account_id, direction, amount, created_at) — append-only; a balance is the sum of these, never a column someone updates.
  • SagaStep (payment_id, step, status, compensation_status) — what has been done, and what would need undoing if a later step fails.
3
API

POST /payments with Idempotency-Key → 201 with the payment and its state, or the original response if the key was seen before
GET /payments/{id} → current state, so a client that timed out can ask rather than retry blindly
POST /payments/{id}/refund with its own idempotency key — a refund is a payment, not an edit
POST /webhooks/processor → settlement callbacks, which must themselves be idempotent

4
Data Flow — genuinely earns its place here
  1. A request arrives with an idempotency key; the service atomically claims that key or discovers it already exists.
  2. An existing key with a matching fingerprint returns the stored response without re-executing anything.
  3. A new key creates the Payment in Pending and writes balanced ledger entries in the same transaction.
  4. The processor is called asynchronously; the payment stays Pending until a settlement callback arrives.
  5. A failure after the ledger was written triggers compensating entries — the original entries are never deleted.
5
High-Level Design

The idempotency key is claimed before any work happens, and money is only ever recorded as paired, append-only entries — diagrammed below.

6
Deep Dives

Three below: (a) what an idempotency key must actually guarantee, (b) why balances are derived rather than stored, and (c) compensating a distributed payment that can't be rolled back.

The key is claimed before any work begins, so a retry is recognised even if the original attempt crashed mid-flight. The ledger write shares a transaction with the payment record; the processor call deliberately does not, because nothing outside your database can be rolled back by it.
A client retries after a timeout
  1. The first request created the payment and wrote ledger entries, but the response never reached the client.
  2. The client retries with the same idempotency key — correctly, since it genuinely cannot tell what happened.
  3. The gate finds an existing record with a matching fingerprint and returns the stored response verbatim.
  4. No second payment, no second ledger entry. The retry was safe precisely because the key was claimed before the work, not after.
The processor declines after the ledger was written
  1. Ledger entries exist, the payment is Pending, and the processor returns a decline.
  2. Nothing is deleted — the entries are historical fact, and the ledger is append-only.
  3. Compensating entries are appended, reversing the effect while preserving the record that it happened.
  4. The payment moves to Failed; the balance is back where it started, and the audit trail shows both the attempt and its reversal.
Key deep dive — what an idempotency key must actually guarantee
FRA retry never moves money twiceNFRExactly-once effect under uncertainty
Thought process
  1. Start from why retries exist at all. A client that times out has learned nothing — the request may have succeeded, failed, or still be running. Not retrying risks losing a payment; retrying blindly risks doubling one.
  2. Consider the naive implementation: do the work, then record the key. Trace a crash between those two steps — the work happened, no key was recorded, and the retry does it all again.
  3. Invert the order. Claim the key first, atomically, so the record exists before any side effect. A retry then finds the claim regardless of how far the original attempt got.
  4. Handle the in-flight case that creates: a retry can now arrive while the original is still running. Returning "not done yet" is correct and honest; guessing an outcome is not.
  5. Close the misuse case. A key reused with different request content is a client bug, and silently returning the old response would hide it — so fingerprint the request and reject a mismatch loudly.

An idempotency key isn't deduplication after the fact — it's a claim staked before any work happens, which is the only ordering that survives a crash between the work and the record of it.
