Illustration — claim first, and the three cases that followWRONG — record the key after the work charge card → CRASH → key never recorded retry arrives → no record found → charges AGAIN RIGHT — claim the key first, in one atomic step INSERT INTO idempotency_records (key, fingerprint, status) VALUES (:key, :fp, 'in_progress') ON CONFLICT (key) DO NOTHING; → inserted 0 rows means someone else already claimed it Three cases on a retry: status = in_progress, fingerprint matches → 409 Conflict, "request in flight, retry shortly" → do NOT guess an outcome, and do NOT start a second attempt status = completed, fingerprint matches → return the STORED response body verbatim, 200 → identical to what the first call returned fingerprint does NOT match → 422 — the same key was reused for different content. This is a client bug; surfacing it beats silently returning a response for a different payment. Keys expire (24h is common) — they're a retry window, not a permanent uniqueness constraint.
  • The client supplies the key, not the server — a server-generated key can't help, because the client must be able to present the same key on a retry, which means it has to exist before the first attempt.
  • Storing the response body is what makes replay exact — a retry should be indistinguishable from the original call, including the payment id. Recomputing the response risks it differing subtly from what the client already may have received.

Same shape as the meeting room's conditional insert: an atomic claim that either succeeds or reveals someone got there first, rather than a check followed by an act.

Key deep dive — why balances are derived, not stored
FREvery balance change is auditableNFRBalances derivable from history
Thought process
  1. Start with the obvious schema: a balance column, updated on each transfer. Ask what it can tell you when a customer disputes a figure — only what it is now, never how it got there.
  2. Notice the sharper problem: an in-place update destroys evidence. If a bug once subtracted the wrong amount, the wrong number is now indistinguishable from a correct one.
  3. Record the movements instead of the result. A balance becomes the sum of an append-only history, so every figure can be explained by replaying what produced it.
  4. Add the structural invariant that makes errors visible: record each movement as a paired debit and credit that sum to zero, so money is always moving between accounts and never appearing or vanishing.
  5. Accept the cost — summing history is slower than reading a column — and handle it with periodic snapshots, which is a performance optimisation over the truth rather than a replacement for it.

Double-entry is centuries old and survives because it makes a whole class of bug structurally detectable: if the entries don't sum to zero, something is wrong, and you find out without needing to already suspect it.

Illustration — a mutable balance vs. an append-only ledgerMUTABLE BALANCE UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE id = 'alice'; UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance + 100 WHERE id = 'bob'; → if the second statement fails, 100 has vanished from the system → nothing records WHY alice's balance changed → a past bug is now indistinguishable from correct data APPEND-ONLY, DOUBLE ENTRY — one transaction, two entries txn_9f3 alice DEBIT 100 txn_9f3 bob CREDIT 100 ---- sum of every entry in txn_9f3 = 0 ← the invariant balance(alice) = SUM(credits) - SUM(debits) Reversal is an append, never a delete: txn_9f3 alice DEBIT 100 ← the original stays, always txn_9f3 bob CREDIT 100 txn_a12 bob DEBIT 100 ← compensation, referencing txn_9f3 txn_a12 alice CREDIT 100 → balance is back to where it started → history shows the payment AND its reversal, which is what an auditor, a support agent, and a customer all actually need A continuous check that every transaction's entries sum to zero catches an entire class of bug without knowing to look for it.
  • Snapshots keep reads fast without weakening the model — store a periodic balance-as-of and sum only the entries since. The derived value stays authoritative; the snapshot is a cache that can be rebuilt.
  • Integer minor units, never floating point — 10 cents as 10, not 0.10. Binary floating point cannot represent most decimal fractions exactly, and the error accumulates precisely where it's least acceptable.
  • The ledger write and the payment record share one transaction — a payment marked succeeded with no entries, or entries with no payment, are both states that should be impossible rather than merely rare.
Key deep dive — compensating what you cannot roll back
FRAll-or-nothing across servicesNFRAlways reaches a terminal stateNFRDuplicate callbacks are safe
Thought process
  1. Try the familiar tool first: wrap everything in one database transaction. It works right up to the external processor, which is not in your database and has no rollback.
  2. Reject distributed two-phase commit for the same reason it's rare in practice — it needs every participant to support it, and a third-party payment processor does not.
  3. Give up on atomicity and target a weaker property that's actually achievable: every step either completes or has a compensating action that undoes its effect.
  4. Notice compensation isn't rollback. A charge that reached the processor happened; the compensating action is a refund, which is a new forward transaction that appears in history alongside the original.
  5. Make every step and every compensation idempotent, since the recovery path is itself retried — a compensation that runs twice must not refund twice.
  6. Guarantee progress with a sweeper. A payment stuck in Pending because the process died must be picked up and driven to a terminal state, or it sits ambiguous forever.

The honest framing is that you cannot have a transaction across systems you don't control — so you design for eventual consistency with explicit, recorded compensation instead of pretending atomicity exists.

Illustration — a saga, and the step that can't be undoneSTEP COMPENSATION 1. reserve funds (internal) release reservation 2. debit source (ledger) append reversing entries 3. charge processor (EXTERNAL, not reversible) → issue a refund 4. credit destination append reversing entries Failure at step 4, after step 3 already succeeded: → the processor charge HAPPENED. It cannot be un-happened. → compensate in reverse: reverse step 2's entries, then issue a refund for step 3, then release the reservation → final state: Failed, balances restored, and the history shows a charge AND a refund — which is the truth, not a cover-up Webhook idempotency — the processor WILL deliver twice: each callback carries a processor event id → INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING on that id → a duplicate is recognised and dropped before it can settle the same payment a second time Out-of-order delivery is the subtler one: a "settled" callback arriving after a "failed" callback for the same payment must be evaluated against the state machine, not applied blindly — terminal states do not accept new transitions. The sweeper: any payment Pending beyond a threshold is queried directly against the processor and driven to a terminal state. Without it, a crash between steps leaves money ambiguous with nothing scheduled to ever resolve it.
  • Order steps so the irreversible one is as late as possible — every reversible step completed before it is one you can compensate cheaply, and the expensive-to-undo external call should be the last thing you commit to.
  • Compensation can fail too — a refund that won't go through needs a dead-letter path and a human, which is unsatisfying but correct. The requirement is that it's visible and queued, never silently abandoned.
  • The state machine is the contract — explicit allowed transitions, with terminal states that reject further changes, is what makes duplicate and out-of-order callbacks safe by construction rather than by careful coding in each handler.

Same instinct as the flash sale's reservation expiry and the ticketmaster hold sweeper: anything that can be left half-finished by a crash needs something scheduled that will eventually finish it.

Does it hold up?

RequirementHow
Exactly-once effect under uncertaintyThe idempotency key is claimed atomically before any side effect, so a retry is recognised regardless of where the original attempt died.
Balances derivable from historyAppend-only paired ledger entries; the balance is a sum, and snapshots are a cache over that truth rather than a substitute.
All-or-nothing across servicesA saga with an explicit compensating action per step — compensation as a new forward transaction, since external effects can't be rolled back.
Always reaches a terminal stateA sweeper queries the processor for anything Pending past a threshold and drives it to Settled or Failed.
Duplicate or out-of-order callbacks are safeCallbacks deduplicate on the processor's event id, and transitions are validated against a state machine whose terminal states reject further changes.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
Async settlement vs. a synchronous yes/noDecouples your availability from the processor's, at the cost of a client that must handle a Pending state and poll or await a webhook rather than getting a simple answer.
Derived balances vs. a stored balance columnFull auditability and a structurally detectable class of bug, at the cost of reads that must sum history — mitigated by snapshots, which add their own reconciliation duty.
Saga compensation vs. distributed transactionsWorks with third parties that will never support two-phase commit, at the cost of windows where the system is genuinely inconsistent and compensation logic that must itself be correct and idempotent.
Rejecting a reused key with different contentSurfaces a client bug loudly instead of returning a response for the wrong payment, at the cost of a hard failure for a client that generated keys carelessly.

Tools you might reach for

ComponentReal-world options
Idempotency recordsPostgreSQL with a unique constraint on the key — the claim must be atomic and durable, which rules out a cache alone
LedgerPostgreSQL append-only tables with a zero-sum check per transaction, or a purpose-built ledger like TigerBeetle
Saga orchestrationTemporal for durable execution, or a state machine plus an outbox and a sweeper worker
Reliable event emissionthe transactional outbox pattern — write the event in the same transaction as the state change, publish from the outbox
ProcessorStripe, Adyen — all of which expose idempotency keys of their own, for exactly the reasons above
Worked Example Y — A Log Nobody Can Quietly Edit

Design Audit Logging at Scale

tamper evidence · capture without coupling · retention vs. erasure
Click to expand

"Record every action taken against financial data — who did what, to which record, when — in a form that satisfies an auditor, survives for seven years, and can't be altered by anyone, including the engineers who run it." Ordinary logging is an operational convenience: if some lines are lost during an incident, that's unfortunate. An audit log is evidence, which inverts the priorities. The genuinely hard parts aren't volume — they're proving nothing was removed, capturing reliably without coupling every service to the logger, and reconciling a seven-year retention duty against a legal right to erasure.

1
Requirements
Functional
  • Record every read and write against regulated data: actor, action, target, timestamp, and outcome.
  • Answer investigative queries — everything one actor did, or everything that touched one record — over years of history.
  • Demonstrate to a third party that no entry has been altered or removed since it was written.
  • Support a legal erasure request without invalidating the integrity guarantee.
Non-functional
  • No engineer or administrator may modify or delete an entry, regardless of their access level.
  • Audit capture must not add meaningful latency to, or be able to fail, the business operation it records.
  • An entry that was accepted must never be silently lost, even if downstream storage is unavailable.
  • Storage cost must stay sane across seven years of retention.

Worth asking: may an audit write ever block a business operation? If losing an audit entry is unacceptable, blocking is the honest answer — but it makes the logger a dependency of everything. We'll take durable async capture, and say precisely where that trade sits below.

2
Core Entities
  • AuditEntry (id, sequence, actor, action, resource_type, resource_id, outcome, at, payload_ref, prev_hash, entry_hash) — the two hashes are what make removal detectable rather than merely discouraged.
  • PayloadBlob (ref, encrypted_body, key_id) — sensitive detail held separately from the entry, so it can be destroyed without touching the chain.
  • Checkpoint (sequence, chain_hash, published_at, external_anchor) — a periodically published root that pins history, so even an insider who rewrites the chain can't match the anchor.
  • RetentionPolicy (resource_type, duration, legal_hold) — a legal hold must be able to override expiry, or routine deletion destroys evidence mid-investigation.
3
API

Deliberately write-once and read-many — there is no update or delete endpoint at all:
POST /audit/entries → append (internal only, from the capture pipeline)
GET /audit/search?actor=&resource=&from=&to= → investigative query
GET /audit/verify?from=&to= → recompute the chain and report any break
POST /audit/erasure → destroy a payload's key, leaving its entry and hash intact

4
Data Flow — genuinely earns its place here
  1. A service performs an action and writes an audit event to its outbox inside the same database transaction.
  2. A publisher ships outbox rows to a durable stream — if it's down, rows accumulate rather than being lost.
  3. The audit writer assigns a sequence number, chains each entry's hash to its predecessor, and appends to write-once storage.
  4. Sensitive payloads are encrypted and stored separately, with only a reference in the entry itself.
  5. Periodically the chain head is published externally, so any later rewrite of history becomes provably inconsistent with it.
5
High-Level Design

Capture is coupled to the business transaction; everything after it is asynchronous and append-only — diagrammed below.

6
Deep Dives

Three below: (a) proving nothing was removed, (b) capturing without coupling, and (c) retention against the right to erasure.

Two stores, deliberately. Entries are immutable and chained, so removal is detectable; payloads are encrypted separately with per-subject keys, so an erasure request destroys the content without breaking the chain that proves the entry existed.
An action is recorded
  1. A service updates a record and writes an audit event to its outbox in the same transaction — both land or neither does.
  2. A publisher ships the outbox row to the durable stream; a slow or down audit writer causes backlog, not loss.
  3. The writer assigns the next sequence number and computes this entry's hash over its content plus the previous entry's hash.
  4. The entry goes to write-once storage; its sensitive payload goes to the vault, encrypted under the data subject's key.
An auditor checks integrity
  1. They request verification over a range, and the service recomputes each entry's hash from its content and predecessor.
  2. A removed or edited entry breaks the chain at that point — every subsequent hash fails to match.
  3. The recomputed chain head is compared against the externally published checkpoint for that period.
  4. Matching both the internal chain and the external anchor is what makes the claim checkable by someone who doesn't trust the operator.
Key deep dive — proving nothing was removed
FRDemonstrate no entry was alteredNFRNot even an administrator can edit
Thought process
  1. Start with the usual control: restrict write access and review the permissions. Ask who it actually protects against — not the database administrator, and not anyone who compromises their credentials.
  2. Notice the requirement is stronger than prevention. An auditor needs to verify nothing changed, which access control can never demonstrate — absence of evidence isn't evidence of absence.
  3. Make alteration self-revealing instead. Chain each entry's hash to its predecessor, so editing one entry invalidates every hash after it and cannot be patched locally.
  4. Catch the remaining hole: someone with full control can recompute the entire chain from the edit forward, and the result verifies cleanly. Internal consistency alone proves nothing against an insider.
  5. Close it by putting a value outside their reach. Publish the chain head periodically somewhere you cannot retract it, and a rewritten history no longer matches what was published.

Access control makes tampering hard; hash chaining makes it visible. Only the external anchor makes it visible against someone who controls the system itself — which is precisely the party an auditor is there to check.

Illustration — chaining, and why it isn't sufficient aloneentry_hash(n) = SHA256( content(n) || entry_hash(n-1) ) seq 1001 actor=alice action=view_account hash=a3f... prev=— seq 1002 actor=bob action=update_limit hash=7c2... prev=a3f... seq 1003 actor=alice action=export_report hash=e91... prev=7c2... Delete 1002 to hide it: → 1003's prev_hash points at 7c2..., which no longer exists → verification fails exactly there, and cannot be patched without recomputing every hash after it But an insider CAN recompute all of them: → renumber, rehash 1003 onward, and the chain verifies perfectly → internal consistency proves nothing against whoever runs the system ANCHOR — publish the chain head where you can't take it back: daily: "as of seq 1002, chain head = 7c2..." → to a separate account with a different trust root, a notary, a regulator's endpoint, or a public ledger → any rewrite of entries at or before 1002 now contradicts a value already published outside your control The strength of the guarantee is exactly the independence of where the anchor is published. Anchoring to a system the same team controls adds nothing.
  • Storage-level immutability is the complementary control — object-lock or WORM storage means the delete simply isn't permitted by the platform, so tampering requires defeating the storage provider rather than just the application.
  • Verification has to be cheap enough to run often — a chain only checked during an annual audit gives you a year of undetected exposure. Continuous verification over recent ranges, plus checkpoint comparison, catches it in hours.
Key deep dive — capturing without coupling
NFRNever adds latency or fails the operationNFRAn accepted entry is never lost
Thought process
  1. Write down the two requirements and notice they conflict: the audit write must not be able to fail the business operation, and an audit entry must never be lost.
  2. Test synchronous logging. It satisfies durability and violates the first — the audit service is now a hard dependency of every regulated write in the company.
  3. Test fire-and-forget. It satisfies the first and violates the second — the operation succeeds while its audit record silently disappears, which is the worst outcome of the three for evidence.
  4. Find the framing that dissolves the conflict: the guarantee needed is that the record is durable, not that it has reached its final store. Those are different things.
  5. Write the audit event into the service's own database, inside the same transaction as the business change. It's now as durable as the thing it records, with no second system involved on the hot path.
  6. Ship it onward asynchronously. A downstream outage becomes backlog rather than loss, and the business operation never waited on anything external.

The apparent conflict rests on assuming durability means arriving at the audit store. Making the record durable locally first satisfies both requirements at once.

Illustration — the outbox resolves the conflictSYNCHRONOUS BEGIN; update_account(); audit_service.write(); COMMIT; → audit service down = the business operation FAILS → you have coupled every regulated write to the logger's uptime FIRE AND FORGET BEGIN; update_account(); COMMIT; go audit_service.write() ← no one is watching this → process dies before it sends: the action happened, the record did not. Silent, and unrecoverable. OUTBOX — one local transaction, both rows BEGIN; UPDATE accounts SET ... ; INSERT INTO audit_outbox (event, created_at) VALUES (...); COMMIT; ← atomic: both or neither A separate publisher reads audit_outbox and ships to the stream, marking rows sent. If the stream is down, rows pile up locally and are delivered when it returns. → the business write never waits on the audit system → an audited action cannot exist without its outbox row → downstream failure is delay, not loss The residual risk is honest: entries can be BEHIND, so a query during an incident may not yet show the most recent seconds. That is a far better failure than an entry that never existed.
  • Capture at the service, not the gateway — a gateway sees the request but not what actually changed, and an operation that failed a business rule after passing authorization looks identical from outside. Audit needs outcome, which only the service knows.
  • Reads often need auditing too — for regulated data, who looked is frequently as significant as who changed it, and read volume is typically far higher, which is what drives the retention decisions below.

Same pattern as the payment example's outbox — anywhere a state change must reliably produce an event, making both durable in one local transaction beats trying to coordinate two systems.

Key deep dive — retention against the right to erasure
FRSupport erasure without breaking integrityNFRStorage cost sane over seven years
Thought process
  1. State the contradiction plainly: financial regulation requires keeping records for years, and privacy regulation gives individuals a right to have their data erased. Both apply to the same log.
  2. Reject the two naive resolutions. Refusing erasure ignores one law; deleting entries breaks the hash chain and destroys the integrity guarantee the whole design exists for.
  3. Separate what each rule actually demands. The regulator needs proof that an action occurred and by whom; the individual's right concerns their personal data, not the fact of the event.
  4. Split the record along that line. Keep actor, action, timestamp, and hash in the immutable chain; put personal detail in a separate encrypted payload.
  5. Erase by destroying the encryption key rather than the data. The ciphertext remains, the chain still verifies, and the content is unrecoverable — which satisfies erasure in substance.
  6. Handle cost separately, since retention is measured in years: tier ageing entries to cheaper storage, and keep hot indexes only over the window investigations actually use.

Crypto-shredding resolves what looks like a direct legal conflict, by separating the fact that something happened from the personal detail of what it contained.

Illustration — crypto-shredding, and the cost tiering it enablesIMMUTABLE ENTRY — never deleted, keeps the chain valid seq 44_102 actor u_8831 action export_customer_report resource customer:c_5567 at 2026-03-11T09:14:22Z payload_ref blob_7a2f ← pointer only prev_hash 9d1... entry_hash 4b8... ENCRYPTED PAYLOAD — separately erasable blob_7a2f encrypted under key_c_5567 (per data subject) { full name, address, account numbers, ... } Erasure request from customer c_5567: destroy key_c_5567 → every payload ever encrypted under it becomes unreadable forever → entries, hashes, and the chain are untouched — verification still passes → the regulator keeps proof the export happened, by whom, and when → the individual's personal data is genuinely unrecoverable Cost over 7 years — driven by read auditing, which dominates volume: 0–90 days hot, fully indexed ← nearly all investigations 90d–1 year warm, indexed on actor and resource only 1–7 years cold archive, restore on request legal hold overrides expiry entirely — routine deletion must never destroy evidence in an open investigation
  • Key granularity is the design decision — one key per data subject makes erasure precise. A key shared across many subjects means erasing one destroys others' records too, which fails the regulator instead.
  • Key destruction must itself be audited — and in a chain that doesn't depend on the key being destroyed, or you cannot later prove you honoured the request.
  • Legal hold has to outrank the retention policy — automated expiry running during an investigation is a compliance failure created by your own tooling, which is a particularly bad way to find out the ordering was wrong.

Does it hold up?

RequirementHow
No one can edit or delete an entryWrite-once storage with object lock, no update or delete endpoint, and hash chaining so an alteration is detectable rather than merely prohibited.
Provable to a third partyChain verification plus a periodically published external anchor, so the claim doesn't rest on trusting the operator.
Never adds latency or fails the operationThe audit event is written to a local outbox in the business transaction; everything downstream is asynchronous.
An accepted entry is never lostDurability is achieved locally at commit time — downstream outage produces backlog, not loss.
Erasure without breaking integrityPersonal detail lives in separately encrypted payloads; destroying the per-subject key erases content while entries and hashes remain verifiable.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
Async capture vs. synchronous audit writesThe logger can never fail a business operation, at the cost of entries lagging by seconds — so a query during a live incident may not show the most recent activity.
Hash chaining plus external anchor vs. access control aloneMakes tampering detectable even by whoever runs the system, at the cost of verification infrastructure and a genuine external publication dependency.
Crypto-shredding vs. deleting rowsSatisfies erasure and retention simultaneously, at the cost of per-subject key management — and an argument that must be defensible to a regulator, since the ciphertext does still exist.
Auditing reads as well as writesAnswers "who looked at this customer's data," which is often the actual investigative question, at the cost of an order of magnitude more volume and the tiering that requires.

Tools you might reach for

ComponentReal-world options
Append-only storeS3 Object Lock in compliance mode, or QLDB-style ledger databases with built-in verification
Durable streamKafka with long retention — the buffer that turns a downstream outage into backlog
Query layerOpenSearch over the hot window, with cold data queried via Athena or an equivalent over archived objects
Key managementAWS KMS or Vault with per-subject keys, since erasure granularity is exactly key granularity
External anchora separate account under a different trust root, a notarisation service, or a public ledger — the value is in the independence, not the technology
Worked Example Z — Collecting Everything Tells You Nothing

Design Observability & Alerting

cardinality is the cost driver · alert on symptoms · sampling that keeps the failures
Click to expand

"Hundreds of services, thousands of instances. Engineers need to know when something is wrong, and be able to find out why." The instinct is to collect everything — every metric, every log line, every trace. That instinct produces a bill that grows faster than traffic, dashboards nobody reads, and an on-call rotation that has learned to ignore pages. The interesting constraints are all consequences of one fact: the volume is large enough that what you choose not to keep is as much of a design decision as what you collect.

1
Requirements
Functional
  • Collect metrics, logs, and traces from every service through one consistent pipeline.
  • Alert a human when users are actually affected, and not otherwise.
  • Let an engineer move from an alert to the specific failing requests without changing tools or guessing.
  • Retain enough history to compare against last week and to substantiate an SLO.
Non-functional
  • Instrumentation must not meaningfully slow the services being measured.
  • The observability system must not fail when the thing it observes fails — that is precisely when it's needed.
  • Cost must scale sub-linearly with traffic, or it eventually rivals the platform it watches.
  • Alert volume must stay low enough that every page is worth waking someone for.

Worth asking: what fraction of pages currently lead to action? If most resolve themselves, the problem isn't detection — it's that alerts are firing on causes rather than on user impact, which is what the second deep dive is about.

2
Core Entities
  • MetricSeries (name, label_set, datapoints[]) — one unique label combination is one series, which is the unit that drives cost.
  • Trace (trace_id, spans[], sampled, duration, has_error) — sampled is a decision, ideally made after the outcome is known.
  • SLO (service, indicator, target, window, error_budget_remaining) — the budget is what converts a threshold into a decision about whether to page.
  • AlertRule (name, condition, severity, runbook_url) — a rule with no runbook is a rule whose response nobody has thought through.
3
API

Ingestion is push from agents; the useful surface is query and control:
POST /v1/metrics|traces|logs → OTLP ingestion from collectors
GET /query?expr=&from=&to= → time-series query for dashboards and rules
GET /traces?trace_id= → the exact requests behind a metric spike
GET /slo/{service}/budget → error budget remaining, which drives paging decisions

4
Data Flow — genuinely earns its place here
  1. Services emit through a shared instrumentation library, so every signal carries the same trace and service identifiers.
  2. A local collector aggregates and batches, so the application never blocks on the observability backend.
  3. The collector enforces limits before data leaves the host — dropping high-cardinality labels and deciding what to sample.
  4. Metrics, traces, and logs land in stores suited to each, linked by trace id so one can lead to another.
  5. Rules evaluate against metrics; only user-impacting conditions page, and everything else is a ticket or a dashboard.
5
High-Level Design

Cost is controlled at the collector, before data is shipped — not at query time, when it has already been paid for — diagrammed below.

6
Deep Dives

Three below: (a) cardinality as the real cost driver, (b) alerting on symptoms with error budgets, and (c) sampling that keeps the interesting traces.

The collector is where cost is decided, because dropping a label or a trace after it has been transmitted and stored has already cost you the money. Everything downstream is shaped by what was allowed through here.
From alert to root cause
  1. Checkout success rate drops below its SLO; burn rate is high enough that the budget will be gone in hours, so it pages.
  2. The alert links to a dashboard showing which dependency's latency moved at the same moment.
  3. Exemplar trace ids attached to the metric lead directly to real failing requests, not a search.
  4. The trace shows the failing span; its logs are already linked by trace id, so no manual correlation is needed.
A cause that should not page
  1. One instance out of forty runs hot on CPU — a classic cause-based alert would fire immediately.
  2. Success rate and latency are unaffected, because the load balancer is already routing around it.
  3. No user is affected and no error budget is burning, so it becomes a ticket rather than a page.
  4. If it does start affecting users, the symptom alert fires on its own — the cause-based page would have added nothing except an interruption.
Key deep dive — cardinality is the real cost driver
NFRCost scales sub-linearly with trafficNFRInstrumentation doesn't slow services
Thought process
  1. Start from the assumption most teams hold implicitly: observability cost tracks request volume, so twice the traffic means roughly twice the bill.
  2. Check it against how a time-series database actually stores data. It doesn't store requests — it stores series, one per unique combination of label values, each carrying its own datapoints and index entries.
  3. Follow the consequence: adding one label with many possible values multiplies the number of series. A user id as a label turns one series into as many series as you have users.
  4. Notice the shape of the resulting failure. Cost grows with the product of label cardinalities, so a single well-intentioned label added by one team can multiply the bill without any traffic increase at all.
  5. Enforce the limit where it's cheapest — at the collector, before data is transmitted. Rejecting a label after it has been shipped, indexed, and stored has already cost you everything it was going to cost.
  6. Keep the high-cardinality data, but in a store designed for it: identifiers belong on traces and logs, which are queried by id, not on metrics, which are aggregated.

The word "cardinality" gets treated as an implementation detail. It's the single variable that decides whether a metrics bill is predictable or grows without anyone shipping more traffic.

Illustration — one label, three orders of magnitudehttp_requests_total{service, endpoint, status, region} 10 services × 20 endpoints × 5 statuses × 3 regions = 3,000 series → entirely fine Someone adds user_id, reasonably wanting per-user debugging: 3,000 × 500,000 users = 1,500,000,000 series → the traffic did not change AT ALL → the bill and the query latency changed by six orders of magnitude The rule that follows: a metric label must have BOUNDED, SMALL cardinality. Values must come from a known, finite set. SAFE service, endpoint (templated), status_code, region, environment, version NEVER user_id, request_id, trace_id, email, session_id, full URL path, raw error message Note "endpoint (templated)": /users/{id} is one series. /users/12345 as a raw path is one series PER USER — the same explosion, arriving through the back door. The high-cardinality data is still available, just elsewhere: need per-user debugging? → traces and logs, queried by id need per-user aggregates? → the warehouse, not the metrics store Enforce at the collector: allow-list metric labels, drop the rest before transmission, and alert the owning team on rejection so it's visible rather than silent.
  • Exemplars bridge the two worlds — attach a handful of sample trace ids to a metric series, and you can jump from an aggregate spike straight to concrete failing requests without ever putting an id in a label.
  • The collector is also what keeps instrumentation cheap — the application writes to a local process and returns immediately, so a slow or unavailable backend never becomes application latency.
Key deep dive — alerting on symptoms, with an error budget
FRAlert when users are affectedNFREvery page worth waking someone
Thought process
  1. Start from how alerts usually accumulate: each incident ends with a new rule on whatever was abnormal that day. CPU, memory, queue depth, replica lag.
  2. Follow that forward. Every rule fires sometimes without users noticing, so the page rate climbs and the proportion of pages that matter falls.
  3. Name the actual failure that produces: on-call stops reading pages carefully, and a real incident is missed inside the noise. More alerting has made detection worse.
  4. Separate the two categories. High CPU is a cause — it may or may not affect anyone. Elevated error rate is a symptom — by definition someone is affected.
  5. Page only on symptoms. Causes remain collected and dashboarded, because they're how you diagnose once paged — they just aren't reasons to wake someone.
  6. Refine further: even a symptom crossing a threshold briefly may not warrant a night-time page. Measure consumption against an error budget, so urgency reflects how fast reliability is actually being spent.

The instinct after an incident is to add an alert. Done repeatedly, that instinct produces a system that pages constantly and detects nothing, because the signal is buried in alerts nobody trusts.

Illustration — causes, symptoms, and burn rateCAUSE-BASED (collect, don't page) CPU > 80% one instance of forty; LB routed around it memory > 90% a cache filling up, exactly as designed queue depth > 1000 a batch job running, drains in 5 minutes a pod restarted that is what orchestrators do → each of these fires regularly with ZERO user impact SYMPTOM-BASED (page) checkout success rate < 99% users cannot buy p99 latency > 2s for 5 minutes users are waiting error budget burning 14x normal the month's reliability will be gone in ~2 days BURN RATE is what turns a threshold into a decision: SLO 99.9% over 30 days → budget = 43 minutes of downtime burn 14x, sustained → budget exhausted in ~2 days → PAGE NOW burn 2x, sustained → exhausted in ~15 days → ticket burn 1x → exactly as planned → nothing The same 0.5% error rate is an emergency on a 99.99% service and business-as-usual on a 99%. The budget encodes that difference instead of requiring a hand-tuned threshold per service. Every paging rule needs a runbook. If nobody can write down what the recipient should DO, the rule isn't ready to wake anyone.
  • Multi-window burn rates avoid both failure modes — a fast window catches sudden severe outages, a slow window catches gradual degradation, and requiring both to agree suppresses the brief spikes that would otherwise page at 3am and resolve themselves.
  • The error budget also decides non-urgent things — budget exhausted means stop shipping risky changes and spend the effort on reliability. That converts an argument about priorities into a number both sides already agreed to.

Same instinct as the developer portal's severity levels: the mechanism only works if the noisy tier genuinely doesn't demand attention, or people learn to ignore the tier that does.

Key deep dive — sampling that keeps the interesting traces
FRReach the specific failing requestsNFRCost scales sub-linearly
Thought process
  1. Establish the constraint: tracing every request at full fidelity is prohibitively expensive at scale, so some form of sampling is unavoidable.
  2. Consider the simple approach — keep a fixed percentage, decided when the request starts. It's cheap, stateless, and easy to reason about.
  3. Test it against the reason traces exist. Errors are rare, so uniform sampling keeps them in proportion to their rarity — exactly 1% of the traces you most needed.
  4. Notice the ordering problem: at request start, you don't yet know whether it will error or be slow. The information needed to sample well doesn't exist when the decision is being made.
  5. Move the decision later. Buffer spans until the trace completes, then decide with the outcome known — keep every error, every slow request, and a small sample of ordinary ones.
  6. Account for what that costs: buffering every trace until completion requires memory and consistent routing, which is real infrastructure rather than a configuration change.

Uniform sampling is cheap and discards precisely the traces worth keeping. Deciding after the outcome is known inverts that, at the cost of having to hold traces in flight.

Illustration — head vs. tail sampling on the same traffic100,000 requests, 500 of them errors (0.5%) HEAD SAMPLING — decide at request start, keep 1% kept: ~1,000 traces, of which ~5 are errors → 495 of 500 failures have NO trace → the on-call engineer investigating those failures finds nothing, because the decision was made before the outcome existed TAIL SAMPLING — buffer, decide on completion keep 100% of errors → 500 traces keep 100% of p99-slow requests → ~1,000 traces keep 1% of ordinary successful requests → ~985 traces → ~2,485 traces, barely more volume than head sampling → and EVERY failure is present The cost: spans for a trace must be buffered until it completes, which means memory, a decision window, and all spans of one trace routed to the same collector — the same partition-by-key discipline as the stock price alert system, applied to trace id. Pragmatic middle ground: head-sample ordinary traffic to control volume, but force-keep any trace already known to be interesting — an error status propagated in the trace context turns the sampling flag on for the rest of the request.
  • Sampling must be consistent across services — if each service decides independently, a trace ends up with gaps where some hops sampled in and others out. The decision propagates in the trace context so the whole request is kept or dropped together.
  • Logs deserve the same treatment — full-fidelity logs for sampled traces, aggressive sampling for the rest, which keeps detail exactly where an investigation will look for it.
  • The pipeline must survive the failures it observes — an incident is when telemetry volume spikes and when the system is most needed. Collectors need their own rate limits and backpressure, or observability fails precisely when it matters.

Does it hold up?

RequirementHow
Cost scales sub-linearlyMetric labels are allow-listed to bounded cardinality at the collector, and traces are sampled by outcome rather than kept wholesale.
Instrumentation doesn't slow servicesApplications write to a local collector and return; batching and shipping happen out of process.
Every page worth waking someoneOnly user-visible symptoms page, gated by error-budget burn rate so urgency reflects real reliability spend.
Alert to root cause without guessingExemplar trace ids on metrics lead to concrete requests; trace id links traces to their logs with no manual correlation.
Survives the failures it observesCollectors apply their own backpressure and rate limits, so a telemetry spike during an incident doesn't take the pipeline down.

Key trade-offs

DecisionThe trade-off
Bounded metric cardinality vs. rich labelsKeeps cost predictable and queries fast, at the cost of not being able to slice metrics by user or request — which is why exemplars and traces exist to cover that need.
Symptom-based paging vs. alerting on causesKeeps pages meaningful and trusted, at the cost of occasionally detecting a problem slightly later than a cause-based alert would have.
Tail sampling vs. head samplingKeeps every error and slow request, at the cost of buffering infrastructure and consistent routing of all spans in a trace to one collector.
Local collector vs. direct exportDecouples applications from backend availability and centralises cost controls, at the cost of another component per host to deploy and monitor.

Tools you might reach for

ComponentReal-world options
Instrumentation & collectionOpenTelemetry SDKs plus the OTel Collector — vendor-neutral, and the cardinality and sampling controls live here
MetricsPrometheus, Mimir, or Datadog for long retention at scale
TracesTempo, Jaeger, or a managed APM — tail sampling typically in the collector tier
LogsLoki or OpenSearch, indexed by trace id so logs join traces without correlation work
Alerting & SLOsAlertmanager or PagerDuty, with burn-rate rules defined as code alongside the service
Worked Example ★ — The Platform Layer, End to End

Design a Microservices Platform: One Request Through Every Layer

north-south vs east-west · control vs data plane · L4 vs L7
Click to expand

"Design the platform layer a few hundred microservices run on — the shared infrastructure every team inherits rather than builds." The components are ones already covered individually: registry, gateway, sidecar, auth server, rate limiter, logging, observability. What's different here is that they have to work as one system, which raises questions none of the individual examples could answer. Who owns which decision, where does each component physically sit, what actually travels on the wire between them, and what keeps working when each piece fails. The spine of the answer is a single request traced from the internet to a database and back.

1
The seven components, one line each
  • Internet edge before the gateway — DNS, CDN, WAF and an L4 load balancer. Four hops that absorb volumetric attacks and cache static responses so the gateway only ever sees traffic worth authenticating.
  • API Gateway edge · data plane · ingress — the first component that is yours, and the ingress point itself rather than something sitting behind one. Terminates TLS, routes, authenticates the caller, applies per-consumer limits, starts the trace.
  • Sidecar proxy every pod · data plane — intercepts all traffic in and out of one service. Service-to-service encryption, authorization, retries, per-dependency protection — and egress control for calls leaving the cluster entirely.
  • Service Registry control plane — the source of truth for which instances of which service are alive right now.
  • Mesh Control Plane control plane — computes endpoints, traffic and authZ policy, and workload certificates, then pushes them to every sidecar. Together with those sidecars it is the service mesh — the one component spanning two planes.
  • Authorization Server control plane — issues and revokes tokens, publishes the public keys used to verify them. Never on the request path.
  • Rate Limiter both planes — not one component. A per-consumer quota at the edge, and a per-dependency concurrency guard in each sidecar.
  • Telemetry Collector every host · telemetry plane — receives metrics, logs, and traces locally, enforces cost limits, ships onward.
  • Observability Backends telemetry plane — store and query the signals; evaluate SLOs and decide what pages a human.
2
Two axes that explain the whole picture
Direction — who handles this traffic
  • North-south — traffic entering or leaving the cluster. Owned by the gateway. The caller is untrusted, the identity is a user or partner, and the job is establishing who they are before anything internal sees the request.
  • East-west — traffic between services inside the cluster. Owned by sidecars. The caller is another service with a cryptographic identity, and the job is deciding whether that service may call this one.
Plane — is it on the request path
  • Data plane — gateway and sidecars. Every request passes through these, so their latency is user-visible and their failure is an outage.
  • Control plane — registry, auth server, mesh control plane. These configure the data plane out of band and are deliberately never called during a request.
  • Telemetry plane — collectors and backends. Fed by the data plane asynchronously; a total outage here loses visibility, not traffic.

Nearly every "why is auth in two places?" or "don't the gateway and the sidecar do the same thing?" question resolves by locating the component on both axes at once.

3
What actually travels on the wire

Three distinct identities coexist on a single request, and conflating them is the most common design error here:

Authorization: Bearer <jwt> — the end user's identity, presented at the edge and verified there. It stops at the gateway.
mTLS client certificate — the calling service's identity, established per hop between sidecars. Never leaves the pair of pods it connects.
x-user-id / x-user-scopes — the end user's identity as a claim, propagated inward by the gateway after verification.
traceparent — the correlation id, minted at the edge and carried through every subsequent hop.

4
Data Flow — one request, every hop
  1. DNS resolves to the CDN; the CDN serves what it can from cache and forwards the rest. A WAF inspects the request, and an L4 load balancer hands the connection to a gateway instance.
  2. The gateway terminates TLS and matches a route.
  3. The gateway applies a cheap anonymous limit, verifies the JWT against locally cached keys, then applies the per-consumer quota now that identity is known.
  4. It mints a trace id, strips the bearer token, attaches user claims as headers, and forwards to Service A.
  5. Service A's sidecar terminates mTLS, checks the gateway is permitted to call this service, records a span, and hands plain HTTP to the app on localhost.
  6. Service A calls Service B by name; its own sidecar resolves that name from endpoints the control plane pushed earlier, opens mTLS to B's sidecar, and applies B's concurrency budget.
  7. B's sidecar authorizes the call by A's certificate identity, and B's app serves it.
  8. If B needs a third party, its sidecar handles that as egress — a different policy, a different failure model, outside the trust boundary.
  9. Every proxy and app emits spans, metrics, and logs to the local collector, all stamped with the same trace id.
5
High-Level Design

Three horizontal bands — control plane above, request path in the middle, telemetry below — with solid lines only where a request actually travels.

6
Deep Dives

Ten below: (a) what sits in front of the gateway, (b) where load balancing actually happens, (c) why auth and rate limiting each appear twice, (d) three identities and how each propagates, (e) egress — the sidecar's other half, (f) how a trace survives a queue, (g) why retries compose into cascades, (h) deploys, draining and certificate rotation, (i) what survives when each component fails, and (j) telemetry and logs.

Note the three balancing layers marked along the bottom: L4 spreads connections at the edge, while the gateway and every sidecar balance requests — a distinction that only matters until you run gRPC, at which point it matters enormously. Solid lines are the only paths a user's request travels. Everything dashed is configuration pushed before the request arrives, and everything dotted is telemetry emitted after — which is why an outage in the top or bottom band degrades the platform without stopping it.

Reading the diagram — three things it doesn't label

Three questions this picture reliably provokes, because the answer in each case is "that isn't a box."

1 · Where is ingress?

Ingress isn't a component — it's a direction. Traffic coming in is ingress; traffic going out is egress. The API Gateway is the ingress point, and the sidecar is the egress point, which is why neither appears as a separately labelled box.

The confusion is legitimate, because Kubernetes overloaded the word. There, "Ingress" is also a specific API object, implemented by an Ingress Controller — itself an L7 proxy, usually NGINX or Envoy. That overlaps heavily with what a gateway does:

Illustration — ingress controller vs. API gatewayINGRESS CONTROLLER API GATEWAY TLS termination TLS termination host / path routing host / path routing ── and that is mostly it ── + authN, per-consumer quota + request/response transformation + trace origination + token stripping, claim injection a CLUSTER concern: a PRODUCT concern: "get traffic to the right "is this caller allowed, and within service" what they were sold?" Three arrangements you will actually meet: gateway only the gateway IS the ingress — one L7 hop (what this diagram shows) controller only fine until you need auth or quotas, at which point every service reimplements them both, stacked ingress controller → gateway → service two L7 proxies, overlapping jobs — sometimes justified (managed TLS, platform-team ownership boundary), often the redundant L7 hop from the load-balancing deep dive The Kubernetes Gateway API exists largely because this split confused people, and deliberately merges the two concepts.
2 · Where is the service mesh?

The mesh isn't one box — it's the only component here that lives in two planes at once. The Mesh Control Plane computes and distributes configuration; every sidecar enforces it. "Where is the mesh" therefore means "that top-right box plus every sidecar in every pod," which is exactly why it looks absent from whichever plane you're currently looking at.

Illustration — what the control plane pushes, and what the sidecar does with itMESH CONTROL PLANE (control plane — computes, never serves a request) endpoints ← sourced from the Service Registry traffic policy ← timeouts, retries, canary weights authZ policy ← which service may call which workload certs ← issued and rotated continuously │ pushed out of band, continuously ▼ SIDECAR (data plane — enforces, on every single request) picks a pod from the endpoints it already holds opens mTLS using the cert it was already issued allows or denies by the policy it already has applies the timeout and retry budget it was already given Every verb in the sidecar's list is in the past tense on purpose: it acts on configuration it received BEFORE the request arrived. That is what makes the control plane skippable at request time.
3 · Why is the control plane never called during a request?

Three separate reasons, worth keeping distinct because they'd each justify the design on their own:

Illustration — three reasons, one techniqueLATENCY a lookup per request adds a round trip to EVERY HOP, and one request crosses several hops AVAILABILITY if the gateway called the auth server per request, the auth server's outage becomes a TOTAL outage → cached, the same outage degrades only the issuing of NEW tokens, and traffic keeps flowing RATE MISMATCH the one most often missed. Config changes a few times a day; requests arrive thousands of times a second. Serving a per-second workload from a per-day source is wasteful by orders of magnitude, regardless of whether it would be fast enough. Same technique answers all three: push ahead of time → cache locally → accept bounded staleness Which is why the Auth Server has exactly two interactions, and neither is during a request: 1. BEFORE the client authenticates and gets a token — a separate flow, possibly hours earlier 2. PERIODIC the gateway refreshes cached JWKS public keys during the request the gateway verifies the signature LOCALLY, against those cached keys, and contacts nobody. That is why the line to it is dashed rather than solid.

The cost of the technique is the thing every dashed line in this diagram has in common: cached data can be briefly wrong. Every staleness window here — key cache, endpoint list, policy, certificates — is a deliberate choice rather than an accident, which is the point the failure deep dive below returns to.

One real request, start to finish — GET /profile

Everything above describes components. This follows a single request through all of them, in order, in plain language — a user opening their profile page, where Service A owns profiles and calls Service B for billing status.

The journey — nine hops, roughly 40msWHAT THE USER DID tapped "My Profile" in the app GET https://api.example.com/v1/profile Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGci... ← they logged in hours ago ① DNS → CDN ~0ms "Is this cacheable?" No — it's personal data. Pass it through. TLS terminates here, close to the user. ② WAF ~1ms "Does this look like an attack?" No. Pass. ③ L4 LOAD BALANCER ~0ms "Which gateway instance?" Picks one. It only sees packets — it has no idea this is a profile request. ④ API GATEWAY ~3ms Now something actually reads the HTTP. a. anonymous IP limit — cheap, runs first. Under the limit. OK. b. verify the token — checks the signature using keys it cached earlier. Does NOT call the auth server. Token is valid, user is u_8831. c. per-consumer quota — now that it knows WHO, checks their quota. Fine. d. route — /v1/profile belongs to service-a. e. pick a pod — L7 choice across service-a's 6 pods. f. start the trace — mints trace id 4bf92f... g. REWRITE the request: ✗ Authorization: Bearer ... ← REMOVED. Goes no further. ✓ x-user-id: u_8831 ← added ✓ x-user-scopes: profile:read ✓ traceparent: 00-4bf92f...-00f067...-01 ⑤ SERVICE A — SIDECAR (inbound) ~1ms Terminates mTLS. The certificate says "the gateway", and policy allows the gateway to call service-a. Records a span. Hands plain HTTP to the app over localhost. ⑥ SERVICE A — APP ~2ms Reads x-user-id and loads the profile it owns. Needs billing status, so it calls http://service-b/billing — plain HTTP, no TLS, no token. It just names the service. ⑦ SERVICE A — SIDECAR (outbound) ~1ms Intercepts that call. "Where is service-b?" It already holds the endpoint list, pushed earlier. Picks one of B's 4 pods. Checks its budget for B — 12 of 40 in flight, room to go. Opens mTLS, presenting service-a's own certificate. ⑧ SERVICE B — SIDECAR (inbound) → APP ~2ms Verifies the certificate says "service-a", and policy allows service-a → service-b. Hands plain HTTP to B's app. ⑨ SERVICE B — DATASTORE ~25ms B's app queries its OWN database, through its own connection pool. The sidecar is not involved — this is not HTTP. ← the slowest hop by far, and where the time actually went THE RESPONSE COMES BACK THE SAME WAY B's app → B's sidecar → A's sidecar → A's app (merges profile + billing) → A's sidecar → gateway → L4 → CDN → user. Each proxy it passes back through records how long its half took and what status came back — which is what makes step ⑨ visible as the culprit without anyone adding instrumentation to find it.

Three things worth noticing about that journey, because each one is a design decision rather than an accident:

  • The user's token existed for exactly one hop. It was verified at the gateway and deleted. Services A and B know who the user is — they just never hold anything they could replay as that user.
  • Neither app wrote a line of networking code. Service A called http://service-b/billing in plain HTTP. Discovery, load balancing, mTLS, retries, and timeouts all happened in the sidecar beside it. Swapping the load-balancing algorithm is a config push, not a code change in either service.
  • Nothing in the top band was contacted. No call to the auth server, the registry, or the mesh control plane. Every decision used information pushed before this request existed — which is the whole reason a control-plane outage wouldn't have stopped it.
The same journey when something goes wrongSay one of service-b's four pods has gone bad. ⑦ A's sidecar picks pod-2 and opens mTLS. ⑧ pod-2 times out at 200ms. A's sidecar retries — on a DIFFERENT pod, not the same one: pod-3 answers in 2ms. ✓ The user waits ~200ms instead of ~40ms and sees nothing wrong. Service A's code was never told any of this happened. Meanwhile, quietly: pod-2's failures push it toward outlier ejection — the sidecar stops selecting it entirely for a cooldown period. Its readiness probe fails; the control plane drops it from the endpoint list; every OTHER service's sidecar stops choosing it too, without anyone being paged. If ALL of B's pods were failing, the retry budget caps how many retries the fleet is allowed in total — which is what stops this from becoming the retry storm in the deep dive below.
Key deep dive — what sits in front of the gateway
Thought process
  1. Notice the gateway is usually drawn as the first thing traffic touches, and that it isn't. Between the client and the gateway sit several layers, each owned by someone else.
  2. Ask what would reach the gateway without them. Every scraper, every volumetric flood, every request for a static asset — all of it arriving at the component that does the most expensive per-request work.
  3. Order the layers by the same principle the gateway's own filter chain uses: reject as early and as cheaply as possible, and let each layer only see what the one before it couldn't handle.
  4. Separate what each layer can actually judge. A CDN sees HTTP and can serve a cached response without any origin at all. A WAF inspects payloads for attack patterns. An L4 balancer sees only packets, so it can spread load but cannot reason about content.
  5. Note what none of them can do: decide whether this specific customer is within their quota. That needs identity, which needs the token verified — so it stays at the gateway, and the layers in front exist to make sure the gateway is only asked that question for plausible traffic.

Each layer in front of the gateway is cheaper per request than the one behind it, and the whole chain exists so that expensive work happens on the smallest possible share of traffic.

Illustration — five hops before your code runsCLIENT │ DNS resolution — often geo-aware, steering to the nearest region ▼ CDN caches static and cacheable responses │ absorbs most traffic without an origin call │ terminates TLS at the edge, close to the user ▼ WAF / DDoS volumetric floods absorbed at L3/L4 │ L7 attack patterns (SQLi, XSS) rejected │ bot scoring, geo blocking ▼ L4 LOAD BALANCER spreads connections across gateway instances │ health-checks them; knows packets, not content ▼ API GATEWAY ← the first component that is YOURS authN, per-consumer quota, routing, tracing Why the order is not arbitrary: a cached CDN response costs no origin capacity at all a WAF rejection costs no routing or token verification an L4 decision costs no HTTP parsing → by the time a request reaches the gateway, it is plausible traffic for a real endpoint, which is when spending a signature verification on it becomes reasonable What none of these layers can do: "is customer acme within the 1,000 req/s they bought?" → requires identity, which requires the verified token → which is exactly why that check lives at the gateway and nowhere in front of it
  • The boundary of ownership matters more than the boundary of the network — CDN, WAF and L4 balancer are typically managed services. The gateway is usually the first hop your team can change, debug, and be paged for.
  • The client's real IP survives only if you carry it — by the gateway, the connection's source address is the load balancer. Anything IP-based (the anonymous rate limit, geo rules, audit records) depends on a forwarded-for header the edge sets and the gateway is configured to trust from that source, and from nowhere else.
Key deep dive — where load balancing actually happens
Thought process
  1. Ask the obvious question the diagram provokes: there's an L4 load balancer at the edge, so where is the L7 one?
  2. Notice it isn't missing — it's the gateway. A gateway is an L7 proxy that reads HTTP and picks a backend; the policy features are bolted onto that, not separate from it.
  3. Then notice a second one that's easy to miss entirely: when Service A calls Service B, A's sidecar chooses which instance of B receives the request. That is also L7 balancing, happening once per hop, in every pod.
  4. Reframe accordingly — L4 and L7 aren't positions in the stack, they're capabilities, and the same traffic gets balanced several times for different reasons at different layers.
  5. Find the distinction that actually has consequences: L4 balances connections, L7 balances requests. For short-lived HTTP/1.1 connections those are nearly the same thing.
  6. Test that against a long-lived multiplexed protocol and it stops being nearly the same thing at all — which turns out to be one of the strongest practical arguments for a mesh existing.

"Where is the L7 load balancer" has three answers in this diagram, and the interesting part is why balancing the same traffic repeatedly isn't redundant.

Illustration — three balancing layers, three different jobsLAYER WHAT IT SEES WHAT IT BALANCES DECIDES L4 LB TCP packets, CONNECTIONS across "which gateway (edge) IPs and ports gateway instances instance?" no HTTP at all API GATEWAY full HTTP: path, REQUESTS across "which service, (L7) host, headers, that service's pods and which of method its pods?" SIDECAR full HTTP, plus REQUESTS across "which pod of (L7, per pod) the mesh's view the callee's pods service-b?" of healthy pods Same traffic, balanced up to three times — not redundant, because each layer is choosing between a different set of things. THE FAILURE MODE THAT MAKES THIS MATTER — gRPC and HTTP/2 HTTP/1.1: many short connections L4 balancing works fine — each new connection is a new chance to pick a different backend, so load spreads naturally. gRPC / HTTP/2: ONE long-lived connection, multiplexing hundreds of concurrent requests L4 picks a backend ONCE, at connect time, and every request on that connection goes there — for the life of the connection. client ──one TCP conn──▶ [L4 LB] ──▶ pod-1 ← ALL traffic pod-2 ← idle pod-3 ← idle → the balancer is working exactly as designed, and your load distribution has collapsed anyway → adding pods does nothing; they receive no traffic → classic symptom: one pod pinned at 100% CPU while the rest of the deployment sits idle, and autoscaling makes it worse The fix is L7, which re-decides PER REQUEST: app ─▶ sidecar ─┬─▶ pod-1 ├─▶ pod-2 ← each request routed └─▶ pod-3 independently This is why a mesh is frequently adopted the moment a fleet moves to gRPC, and why Kubernetes Services alone (kube-proxy is effectively L4) disappoint people who expect them to balance gRPC traffic evenly.
  • Watch for redundant L7 hops — a cloud ALB (L7) in front of an API gateway (L7) is two proxies doing overlapping work. Sometimes justified for TLS offload or managed WAF integration; often it's one hop of latency and one more thing to configure, collapsible into the gateway.
  • Only the L7 layers can do the interesting algorithms — least-request, zone-aware routing, and outlier ejection all need per-request visibility. An L4 balancer can only do round-robin or hashing over connections, because a connection is the only unit it can see.
  • The sidecar's balancing is why the registry matters at runtime — its pod list comes from the control plane, which got it from the registry. L4 at the edge is comparatively static; the per-request layer is the one that must react as pods come and go.
Key deep dive — why auth and rate limiting each appear twice
Thought process
  1. Notice the apparent duplication: the gateway authenticates and the sidecar authorizes; the gateway rate limits and the sidecar rate limits. It looks like one job done twice.
  2. Ask what each is protecting against, and the answers turn out to be different threats entirely — not one threat handled redundantly.
  3. At the edge the caller is unknown and untrusted, so the question is "who are you, and are you within what you were sold." That needs a user token and a per-consumer quota.
  4. Between services the caller was already authenticated at the edge, so the question changes to "may this service call that one, and is that dependency healthy enough to take more load." That needs a workload identity and a concurrency budget.
  5. Test whether either could be dropped. Remove the sidecar layer and a compromised internal service can call anything it likes — the edge check proved a user's identity, never what a service is permitted to do.

They aren't redundant layers of one control; they're different questions that happen to share a vocabulary.

Illustration — the same two words, four different jobs AT THE EDGE (gateway) BETWEEN SERVICES (sidecar) AUTH authN: "who is this user?" authZ: "may service-a call verify JWT signature and service-b at all?" expiry against cached JWKS verify the mTLS workload certificate, then check policy identity = a person or identity = a service, proven partner application cryptographically, per hop RATE per-consumer QUOTA per-dependency CONCURRENCY LIMIT "this customer bought "no more than 40 in-flight 1,000 req/s" calls to service-b from here" a commercial construct a stability construct — → 429 Too Many Requests the bulkhead from example S → shed fast, protect the caller Drop the edge layer → anyone calls anything, no commercial control Drop the sidecar layer → one compromised service reaches every other, and one slow dependency stalls all its callers
  • The sidecar layer is what makes "zero trust" concrete — being inside the network grants nothing, because every hop is independently authenticated and authorized regardless of where it originated.
  • Neither layer calls a remote service to decide — the gateway uses cached keys, the sidecar uses pushed policy. Both checks are local, which is what keeps them off the latency budget.
Key deep dive — three identities, propagated three different ways
Thought process
  1. Start from the tempting simplification: verify the user's token at the edge and forward it inward, so every service knows who the user is.
  2. Trace what that token becomes once inside. It's a credential any internal service can replay against any other service that accepts it — the confused deputy problem from the MCP example, now fleet-wide.
  3. Separate the two things the token was doing. Proving the user's identity is one job; authorizing this specific hop is another, and only the first needs to travel.
  4. Strip the token at the edge and forward the verified claims instead. Downstream services learn who the user is without holding anything they could replay.
  5. Give the hop its own identity, independent of the user: a workload certificate issued to the service, verified per connection.
  6. Add the third identity, which isn't security at all — the trace id, which must survive every hop unchanged so the telemetry plane can stitch the request back together.

Three identities answer three different questions, and the design error is letting one of them try to answer all three.

Illustration — what each hop carriesCLIENT → GATEWAY Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGci... ← the USER's identity → verified against cached JWKS, then STRIPPED. Goes no further. GATEWAY → SERVICE A (mTLS: gateway-identity → service-a) x-user-id: u_8831 ← user identity, as a CLAIM x-user-scopes: invoices:read traceparent: 00-4bf92f...-00f067...-01 ← correlation, minted here (no Authorization header — deliberately) SERVICE A → SERVICE B (mTLS: service-a → service-b) x-user-id: u_8831 ← same claim, passed along x-user-scopes: invoices:read traceparent: 00-4bf92f...-b7ad6b...-01 ← same TRACE id, new SPAN id (mTLS proves it is service-a; nothing about the user is re-proven) Why the token stops at the edge: forwarded → every service holds a replayable user credential, and any one of them can act as that user anywhere stripped → services know WHO the user is, and hold nothing they could present elsewhere The trade: downstream services must TRUST the gateway's claims. That trust is only sound because the sidecar verified the connection came from the gateway — which is why the mesh layer is load-bearing rather than decorative.
  • Claims must be un-spoofable, which the sidecar provides — a header is trivially forged by anything that can reach the service, so the guarantee comes from mTLS proving the sender is the gateway, not from the header itself.
  • The trace id is the only thing that never changes — span ids differ per hop and identities differ per hop, but one trace id spans the whole request, and that is what lets a metric spike lead to a log line.
Key deep dive — egress, the sidecar's other half
Thought process
  1. Notice the sidecar has been described entirely in terms of inbound and east-west traffic, and that it necessarily sees outbound traffic too — a service calling a payment processor goes through it just the same.
  2. Ask what changes when the destination is outside the cluster. Every assumption the mesh relies on breaks: no workload certificate to verify, no pushed endpoints, no control over their latency or their uptime.
  3. Recognise what that makes egress: not "east-west with a longer network path" but a third traffic class with its own rules, which is why it deserves its own policy rather than inheriting internal defaults.
  4. Find the security property worth having. If all outbound traffic must pass a sidecar that enforces an allow-list, a compromised service cannot exfiltrate to an arbitrary host — the proxy simply refuses a destination nobody approved.
  5. Apply the resilience patterns that already exist elsewhere, since an external dependency is the most likely thing in the system to be slow: timeouts, circuit breaking, and a concurrency budget, exactly as with an internal dependency but tuned far more conservatively.

Egress is the traffic class most often left undesigned, and it's the one where the caller has the least control and the most exposure.

Illustration — three traffic classes, three sets of rules INBOUND (n-s) EAST-WEST EGRESS client → you you ↔ you you → them who they are user token mTLS workload a TLS server cert at the gateway certificate you did not issue endpoints from route config mesh control DNS you don't control plane push if it is slow they wait your bulkhead THEIR problem becomes sheds load YOUR outage unless you bound it aggressively policy question "who are you?" "may A call B?" "may ANY service reach this host at all?" Egress allow-list, enforced by the sidecar: service-b → api.stripe.com:443 ALLOW service-b → api.partner.example:443 ALLOW service-b → * DENY → a compromised service-b cannot post your data to an arbitrary host, because its own proxy refuses to open the connection → every external dependency becomes a declared, reviewable fact instead of whatever a library decided to call Tune external calls far more conservatively than internal ones: internal timeout ~200ms, retry twice external timeout ~2s, retry ONCE and only if idempotent → the payment example's compensation logic exists precisely because a retried external call is not free
  • A dedicated egress gateway is the common escalation — routing all outbound traffic through a small set of nodes gives you a stable set of source IPs to give partners for their allow-lists, and one place to hold outbound credentials.
  • Retrying egress is a correctness question, not a latency one — an internal read can be retried freely; a charge cannot. Which is why the idempotency keys from the payment example are what make an external retry safe at all.
Key deep dive — how a trace survives a queue
Thought process
  1. Notice everything described so far is synchronous request-response, and that a real platform does a large share of its work through queues instead.
  2. Ask what the sidecar can do for a message on a queue. Very little — it proxies connections, and the producer's connection to the broker ends long before the consumer's begins. There is no hop for it to sit in.
  3. Follow what that costs. The trace ends at the producer, the user's identity ends at the producer, and the consumer starts with a message and no context at all.
  4. Recognise why this hurts most exactly where it's hardest: a failure in the consumer is disconnected from the request that caused it, so nobody can answer "which user action led to this."
  5. Carry the context inside the message rather than around it. Trace context and the acting user become message headers, written by the producer and read by the consumer to continue the same trace.
  6. Be precise about what the consumer's span is. It is not a child running concurrently with the producer's — it runs later, after the parent has finished, which is a link between traces rather than ordinary nesting.

The mesh's guarantees are per-connection, and a queue deliberately breaks the connection. Everything the sidecar provided for free has to be re-established in the message payload itself.

Illustration — what a message must carry that a connection gave you freeSYNCHRONOUS HOP — the sidecar handles all of it service-a ──mTLS──▶ service-b identity : proven by certificate, per connection trace : traceparent header, forwarded automatically authZ : policy evaluated at connect time ACROSS A QUEUE — the connection ends; nothing is carried for you service-a ──▶ [ topic ] ──▶ service-c (minutes later, maybe) identity : no connection, so no certificate. Gone. trace : producer's span ended at publish. Gone. authZ : broker ACLs govern who may publish or consume a TOPIC — not which user the message concerns So the message carries it explicitly: headers: traceparent: 00-4bf92f3577b34da6-a1b2c3d4-01 ← same TRACE id x-user-id: u_8831 ← who it is FOR x-user-scopes: invoices:read payload: { "invoice_id": "inv_991", ... } consumer reads traceparent → starts a span LINKED to that trace → one trace now spans the API request AND the work it triggered, minutes apart, across services that never shared a connection Two things worth stating plainly: 1. The consumer's span is a LINK, not a child. The parent finished long ago; they are causally related, not concurrent. Treating it as a normal child produces traces with impossible durations. 2. A message header is NOT proof. On a connection, mTLS proved who the sender was. Here, x-user-id is an assertion by whoever published — which is only trustworthy because broker ACLs control who may publish to that topic at all.
  • Publish inside the transaction, or the trace lies — the outbox pattern from the payment and audit examples applies directly. A message published after a commit that then fails leaves a trace showing work that was never actually caused.
  • Consumer lag is the metric with no synchronous equivalent — there is no caller waiting and timing out, so a stalled consumer produces no errors and no latency alert. Lag is the symptom, and it needs an SLO of its own.
Key deep dive — retries compose, and that is how you cascade
Thought process
  1. Notice what has been described so far without comment: the gateway retries, the caller's sidecar retries, and the callee's sidecar retries. Each one is individually sensible and configured by a different team.
  2. Multiply them. Three attempts at each of three layers is up to twenty-seven requests reaching a service for one user action — and nobody chose that number, it emerged.
  3. Find when that multiplication actually fires. Not during normal operation, when nothing retries; only when a service is already slow or failing. The amplification arrives precisely when the target has the least capacity to absorb it.
  4. Follow the feedback loop to its conclusion: retries add load, added load increases latency, increased latency triggers more retries. A service recovering from a blip cannot recover, because the retries won't let it.
  5. Reject the obvious fix of "turn off retries" — they exist because a single packet loss shouldn't surface as a user-visible error, and removing them trades one failure mode for another.
  6. Bound the total instead of the per-layer count: cap retries as a fraction of overall traffic, and retry at one layer rather than at every layer.

Every layer in this architecture retries, each configured by a different team, and none of them can see the others. The composition is what kills you.

Illustration — one user click, twenty-seven requestsService C starts responding slowly. Each layer does the sensible thing: user click gateway retries 3× ─┐ sidecar-a outbound retries 3× ├─ 3 × 3 × 3 = 27 requests sidecar-c inbound retries 3× ─┘ arriving at a service that is ALREADY struggling → C gets 27× its normal load at its weakest moment → C slows further, so MORE requests time out, so MORE are retried → the retries are now the outage. C never recovers on its own. THE FIX IS A BUDGET, NOT A COUNT retry_budget: ratio: 0.1 # retries may add at most 10% of normal traffic min_retries_per_sec: 3 → under normal conditions retries are rare, so the budget is never touched and a lost packet is still retried transparently → when C degrades and everything wants to retry at once, the budget is exhausted almost immediately and further retries are simply not attempted → load on C is capped at ~1.1× normal no matter how bad it gets TWO MORE RULES THAT MATTER RETRY AT ONE LAYER ONLY pick the sidecar (it knows which pods are healthy and can pick a DIFFERENT one) and disable retries at the gateway for that route. Retrying the same failing pod is pointless anyway.
