BUDGET YOUR TIMEOUTS DOWNWARD gateway timeout 3000ms service-a 2500ms ← must be LESS than its caller's service-b 2000ms service-c 1500ms → if every layer uses the same timeout, the outermost gives up first and every inner layer keeps working on a request whose answer nobody is waiting for — capacity burned on nothing
  • Jitter is not optional — retrying after a fixed delay means every client that failed together retries together, producing a synchronised second wave. Randomised exponential backoff spreads the retry over time instead of concentrating it.
  • Circuit breakers and retry budgets solve different halves — the breaker stops calling a service that is clearly down; the budget limits the damage while it is merely degraded, which is the longer and more dangerous state.
  • Only idempotent operations may be retried at all — the mesh cannot know whether an endpoint is safe to repeat, so this is a per-route configuration, and getting it wrong on a payment is the failure the idempotency keys from example X exist to prevent.
Key deep dive — deploys, draining, and rotating certificates
Thought process
  1. Notice everything so far describes the platform serving traffic in steady state, and that a platform spends its whole life changing — new versions ship, pods come and go, credentials expire.
  2. Ask what the sidecar makes possible that wasn't possible before it existed. It already decides which pod receives each request, so "send 5% of traffic to the new version" is a routing rule rather than a deployment strategy.
  3. Separate deploying from releasing. Starting new pods is a deploy; sending users to them is a release. Once routing is a control-plane decision, those stop being the same event, and a bad version is reverted by changing a weight rather than by shipping again.
  4. Work out how a pod leaves cleanly. An instance that simply stops drops whatever it was serving, so it needs to fail readiness first, let callers stop choosing it, finish in-flight work, and only then exit.
  5. Find the piece that runs continuously and is easy to forget: the workload certificates every mTLS hop depends on are short-lived, so something must reissue them constantly — and if it stops, the mesh stops trusting itself.

Steady state is the easy half. Everything in this section that makes a request work has a counterpart that makes change safe, and it's the same control plane doing both.

Illustration — three lifecycle mechanisms the mesh already has the position to provide1 · TRAFFIC SHIFTING — deploy and release become separate events route: service-b v1 weight 95 ← stable v2 weight 5 ← canary, real users, real traffic watch v2's OWN error rate and latency, not the aggregate — 5% of traffic failing barely moves a combined number → healthy: 5 → 25 → 50 → 100 over minutes → unhealthy: set v2 to 0. Instant. No redeploy, no rollback build, no waiting for images to pull. Because the sidecar already picks the pod, this is a weight in a config push — not a new capability, just one it was always positioned to offer. 2 · READINESS AND DRAINING — how a pod leaves without dropping work WRONG SIGTERM → process exits → in-flight requests die → callers see connection resets they must retry, which is exactly how a routine deploy becomes the retry storm from the previous deep dive RIGHT 1. readiness probe starts failing 2. control plane removes the pod from endpoints 3. callers' sidecars stop selecting it — but existing connections keep working 4. pod finishes in-flight requests (drain window) 5. only now does the process exit LIVENESS asks "is this process broken, restart it?" READINESS asks "should traffic come here right now?" Conflating them means a pod that is merely warming up gets killed instead of simply not being sent traffic yet. 3 · CERTIFICATE ROTATION — the thing that must never quietly stop Workload certs are deliberately short-lived — often hours. Short life is the point: a stolen certificate expires on its own, so there is no revocation list to distribute. The control plane reissues them continuously, and each sidecar swaps its cert without dropping connections. The failure mode worth naming: if rotation silently stops, NOTHING breaks for hours — and then every mTLS handshake in the fleet starts failing at roughly the same time, because the certs were all issued on a similar schedule. Certificate expiry is one of the few failures that is simultaneously fleet-wide and entirely predictable, which makes "time until the soonest expiry" one of the highest-value alerts in the whole platform.
  • Canary judgement needs per-version telemetry, which the sidecar already emits — the version has to be a label on the metrics the proxy produces, or you cannot tell a failing canary from a slightly worse aggregate. This is one of the rare labels genuinely worth its cardinality.
  • The drain window and the client timeout must agree — a 5-second drain against a 30-second client timeout still kills long requests at shutdown. The drain should outlast the slowest request the service is expected to serve.
  • Rotation applies to more than certificates — signing keys behind the gateway's JWKS cache rotate on the same principle, which is why that endpoint serves several keys at once: a token signed by the outgoing key must still verify until it expires.
Key deep dive — what survives when each component fails
Thought process
  1. Take the separation of planes seriously and test it, rather than treating it as a diagram convention.
  2. Go component by component and ask the same question: if this is completely down, can a user's request still be served?
  3. Notice the pattern that emerges — every control-plane component was made survivable by the same technique, which is caching its output in the data plane ahead of time.
  4. Name what that costs. Cached data goes stale, so each component accepts a staleness window in exchange for not being a hard dependency.
  5. Find the components where the honest answer is "traffic stops," and confirm those are the ones mitigated by redundancy instead.

The recurring move across every example on this page shows up here as one property of the assembled system: the request path holds a local copy of everything it needs, so the systems that produce that information can fail without stopping traffic.

Illustration — failure by componentAUTH SERVER DOWN → gateway keeps verifying tokens with cached JWKS → existing tokens work; NEW tokens cannot be issued → degraded at the edges, traffic flows SERVICE REGISTRY DOWN → sidecars keep using last-pushed endpoints → existing instances reachable; scale-up is invisible until it returns → this is WHY the registry is AP rather than CP (example R) MESH CONTROL PLANE DOWN → sidecars keep the config they already hold and keep proxying → policy changes do not propagate; traffic is unaffected TELEMETRY COLLECTOR DOWN → the app's writes fail fast locally and are buffered or dropped → you lose VISIBILITY, not traffic — and losing it during an incident is exactly when it hurts, hence local buffering OBS BACKENDS DOWN → collectors buffer; alerting is blind → the real risk is an outage nobody is paged for SIDECAR DOWN (one pod) → that pod is unreachable; the registry evicts it and callers route around it — an ordinary instance failure GATEWAY DOWN (all instances) → external traffic STOPS. There is no cache that saves you. → the genuine single point of failure, which is why it is stateless, horizontally scaled and multi-AZ — mitigated by redundancy, because it cannot be mitigated by caching
  • The asymmetry is deliberate — control plane failures degrade change (new tokens, new endpoints, new policy) while leaving steady state working, and steady state is the overwhelming majority of the time.
  • Staleness windows should be explicit, not incidental — how long a sidecar keeps stale endpoints, or a gateway keeps stale keys, is a decision to make deliberately rather than a value someone left at its default.
Key deep dive — telemetry and logs through every hop
Thought process
  1. Start from the question an on-call engineer actually asks: this request was slow — which hop caused it?
  2. Recognise what answering that requires: every component the request touched must emit a signal, and those signals must be joinable after the fact.
  3. Find the cheapest place to get most of it for free. The sidecar already sees every byte in and out of its service, so it can emit latency, status and a span without the application being modified at all.
  4. Identify what the sidecar genuinely cannot know — why a request was slow inside the app — and accept that in-process instrumentation is needed for that layer specifically.
  5. Make the signals joinable by propagating one trace id from the edge through every hop, so proxy spans, app spans and log lines share a key.
  6. Keep it off the hot path: everything goes to a local collector that batches and ships, so no application ever waits on a telemetry backend.

The sidecar turns observability from something every team must implement into something the platform provides by default — with a clearly bounded gap that in-process instrumentation fills.

Illustration — one trace, assembled from every layertrace 4bf92f3577b34da6 span 1 gateway 212ms ← from the gateway itself route match, authN, quota span 2 sidecar-a inbound 208ms ← FREE, from the proxy span 3 service-a app 205ms ← in-process instrumentation span 4 sidecar-a outbound 190ms ← FREE span 5 sidecar-b inbound 188ms ← FREE span 6 service-b app 185ms ← in-process span 7 └─ db query 180ms ← in-process. THE CULPRIT. Spans 2, 4 and 5 cost the application teams nothing — the proxy is already in the path, so latency, status codes and retries come for free across every service in the fleet, in a consistent format. What the sidecar CANNOT see: which SQL statement was slow, which code path was taken, what a cache hit ratio was. Proxy telemetry tells you WHICH HOP; only in-process instrumentation tells you WHY. The chain that makes it work: gateway mints the trace id → forwards it in traceparent → sidecar reads it, emits its span under it, forwards unchanged → app reads it, emits its span, attaches it to every log line → all three signals join on one key in the backend

Logs deserve separating out, because they're the one signal the sidecar mostly can't produce for you, and the one with obligations the other two don't have.

Illustration — what makes a log line useful rather than just presentUNSTRUCTURED — grep-only, joins to nothing 2026-09-19 08:14:22 ERROR failed to charge customer 5567: timeout STRUCTURED — queryable, and joins to the trace above { "ts": "2026-09-19T08:14:22.118Z", "level": "error", "service": "service-b", "trace_id": "4bf92f3577b34da6", ← THE join key "span_id": "b7ad6b7169203331", "user_id": "u_8831", "event": "charge_failed", "reason": "processor_timeout", "invoice": "inv_991" } → filter by level, service, or event without regex → one trace_id pivots from a latency spike to this exact line → aggregate "how often is charge_failed happening" as a metric Three rules that matter more at fleet scale than in one service: LEVELS ARE A BUDGET debug in production is a cost decision, not a preference. Sample or disable it per service, and raise it temporarily during an incident. NEVER LOG SECRETS tokens, card numbers, passwords. A log OR PERSONAL DATA CASUALLY — logs are replicated, widely readable, and retained, which makes them the least guarded place personal data accumulates. Log an id; put the detail in the audited store from example Y, where erasure is possible. LOGS FOLLOW THE TRACE full fidelity for sampled traces, aggressive sampling for everything else — detail exactly where an investigation will go looking for it.
  • Consistency is the underrated benefit — sidecar-emitted metrics carry identical names and labels across every service in the fleet, so a dashboard works for a service the team that built it has never seen.
  • The collector is where cost is controlled — the cardinality caps and outcome-based sampling from example Z apply to this combined firehose, which is larger than any single service's output.
  • Audit logs are not application logs — they look similar and have opposite requirements. Application logs are disposable, sampled, and short-lived; audit entries are evidence, must never be lost, and are governed by the retention and erasure rules from example Y. Keeping them in one pipeline gets one of the two wrong.

Responsibility map

ComponentPlane · positionOwns
CDN · WAF · L4 LBData · internet edgeCaching, volumetric and L7 attack absorption, and spreading connections across gateway instances — everything rejectable before identity is known. Usually managed services, not yours to debug
API GatewayData · edge (north-south)An L7 proxy: TLS termination, routing, per-request balancing across a service's pods, user authN, per-consumer quota, trace origination, token stripping and claim injection
Sidecar proxyData · every pod (east-west)Also an L7 proxy: mTLS both directions, service-to-service authZ, per-request balancing across the callee's pods, retries, per-dependency budgets, egress allow-listing, free telemetry
Service RegistryControlWhich instances are alive; consumed by the mesh control plane, never called during a request. Readiness is what removes a draining pod from this set
Auth ServerControlToken issuance and revocation, JWKS publication; cached by the gateway so it stays off the request path
Rate limitingBothCommercial quota at the edge; stability-oriented concurrency budgets in the sidecars — different constructs sharing a name
Message brokerData · asyncCarries work across a connection boundary — which means trace context and acting user must travel in the message, because the sidecar cannot follow them there
CollectorTelemetry · every hostLocal buffering, cardinality caps, sampling decisions — the cost control point
Obs backendsTelemetryStorage and query for metrics, traces and logs; SLO evaluation and paging decisions

Key trade-offs of the assembled platform

DecisionThe trade-off
Sidecar per pod vs. a library in each serviceLanguage-agnostic, upgradeable without touching application code, and telemetry comes free — at the cost of a proxy process per pod, its memory and latency overhead, and a harder debugging story when the proxy itself misbehaves.
Strip the user token at the edge vs. forward it inwardNo internal service holds a replayable user credential, at the cost of downstream services having to trust gateway-asserted claims — which only holds because mTLS proves those claims came from the gateway.
Cache everything in the data plane vs. query the control plane liveControl plane outages degrade change rather than stopping traffic, at the cost of every cached thing having a staleness window that must be chosen deliberately.
A gateway at all vs. exposing services directlyOne consistent place for auth, quota and observability, at the cost of a genuine single point of failure that only redundancy can mitigate — no cache saves you if every gateway instance is down.
Per-request L7 balancing in sidecars vs. relying on L4 aloneLoad actually spreads for gRPC and HTTP/2, and enables least-request and outlier ejection — at the cost of a proxy in every pod that must hold and refresh an accurate view of every callee's endpoints.
Traffic shifting in the mesh vs. deploy-and-prayDeploy and release become separate events, so a bad version is reverted by changing a weight rather than shipping again — at the cost of per-version telemetry to judge the canary by, and a routing config that is now part of your release process.
Egress through a sidecar allow-list vs. letting services call out freelyA compromised service can't exfiltrate to an arbitrary host, and every external dependency becomes a declared fact — at the cost of a list that must be maintained, and an outage shaped like "we forgot to allow-list the new vendor."
Propagating context through messages vs. accepting broken tracesOne trace spans an API request and the async work it triggered, at the cost of producers and consumers both having to handle headers correctly — and a header that is an assertion rather than the proof mTLS gave you.
This whole architecture vs. a monolithIndependent deploys, per-service scaling and blast-radius containment — at the cost of every piece above. Worth stating plainly: a small team serving modest traffic will ship faster without any of it.

Every box in the diagram, and what you'd actually run there

One row per component. "Managed" means someone else operates it; "self-hosted" means you do. The right column is the part worth reading — most of these choices are decided by constraints rather than by the tool being better.

BoxManagedSelf-hosted / OSSHow the choice usually gets made
CDN Cloudflare Fastly CloudFront Akamai — practically never self-hosted You cannot self-host presence in 300 cities. This one is bought, and usually bundled with the WAF below.
WAF / DDoS Cloudflare AWS WAF + Shield Akamai ModSecurity + NGINX, Coraza Volumetric attacks must be absorbed upstream of your capacity, so the DDoS half is inherently a managed service. Self-hosted WAF rules are viable for L7 filtering only.
L4 load balancer AWS NLB GCP Network LB Azure LB HAProxy (L4 mode), IPVS, MetalLB On a cloud you inherit this from a Service type=LoadBalancer and never think about it. MetalLB exists because bare metal has no such thing.
API Gateway AWS API Gateway Apigee Azure APIM Kong Envoy Gateway APISIX Traefik Tyk NGINX Managed gateways bill per request and get expensive at volume, which is the usual reason teams move off them. On Kubernetes this box is often an ingress controller — the overlap covered in the diagram notes above.
Sidecar proxy — comes with the mesh you pick Envoy (Istio, Consul), linkerd2-proxy (Rust, much lighter) You rarely choose the proxy directly; you choose the mesh and inherit its proxy. Sidecarless modes now exist, which trade per-pod overhead for a shared node-level component.
Mesh control plane GKE/EKS managed Istio Istio Linkerd Consul Cilium Kuma Istio is the most capable and the most to operate; Linkerd trades features for being genuinely simple. Cilium does it at the eBPF layer instead of with proxies. Pick by how much operational surface you can carry.
Service Registry — part of your orchestrator Kubernetes (etcd + EndpointSlice), Consul etcd Eureka Nacos On Kubernetes you already have one and adding another is a mistake. Consul and Eureka matter when you're on VMs, or spanning clusters and clouds.
Auth Server Auth0 Okta Cognito Entra ID Keycloak Ory Hydra Zitadel Authentik Managed is right until per-user pricing collides with your user count, or a compliance rule says identity data stays in your own infrastructure. Both cases arrive suddenly.
Workload identity — usually the mesh's built-in CA SPIFFE/SPIRE, cert-manager, Vault PKI The mesh issues workload certs by default. SPIRE earns its place when identity must span clusters, clouds, or workloads that aren't in the mesh at all.
Datastore RDS Aurora Cloud SQL DynamoDB Spanner PostgreSQL MySQL Cassandra Redis Owned by one service, not shared — that boundary matters far more than the engine. Self-hosting a database on Kubernetes is a real commitment; managed is the default for good reason.
Message broker SQS/SNS Google Pub/Sub MSK Confluent Kafka RabbitMQ NATS Pulsar Kafka when you need a replayable log; RabbitMQ or SQS when you need a work queue. Choosing Kafka for a task queue is one of the most common over-engineering mistakes here.
Local Collector Datadog Agent Grafana Alloy OpenTelemetry Collector, Fluent Bit (logs), Vector The OTel Collector is the vendor-neutral default, and the reason it's worth running even with a managed backend: switching backends becomes a config change rather than a re-instrumentation project.
Obs backends Datadog New Relic Honeycomb Grafana Cloud Prometheus/Mimir (metrics), Tempo/Jaeger (traces), Loki/OpenSearch (logs) Usually three systems, not one — the cardinality and retention profiles differ so much that one store serves all three badly. Managed APM is billed on ingest, which is exactly why the collector's caps matter.
Alerting PagerDuty Opsgenie Alertmanager Grafana OnCall Rules belong in version control next to the service. The paging tool only handles escalation and rotations.
Third-party API Not a choice — it's whoever you integrate with The design decisions here are yours regardless: egress allow-list, conservative timeouts, and idempotency on anything that costs money.
Two stacks that actually exist, and the order to adopt them inIF YOU ARE ON KUBERNETES AND AWS — the path of least resistance CDN + WAF CloudFront + AWS WAF, or Cloudflare in front L4 NLB, inherited from Service type=LoadBalancer gateway Envoy Gateway or Kong, in-cluster mesh Linkerd if you want it simple, Istio if you need it registry Kubernetes itself — do NOT add a second one auth Cognito or Keycloak datastore RDS/Aurora, one per owning service telemetry OTel Collector → Prometheus + Tempo + Loki, or → Datadog if you would rather pay than operate IF YOU ARE ON VMs, NO KUBERNETES registry Consul — this is the case it was built for mesh Consul Connect, or none and use client libraries gateway NGINX or Kong on dedicated hosts everything else largely unchanged THE ORDER THAT MATTERS MORE THAN THE TOOLS You do not need all of this, and adopting it in the wrong order is how platform teams lose a year. 1. gateway + auth the first real need: one door, one identity 2. telemetry you cannot operate what you cannot see — and this pays off before anything below it 3. registry comes free with Kubernetes; otherwise Consul 4. mesh LAST. Adopt it when you have the problems it solves — mTLS everywhere, gRPC load balancing, per-service authZ, canary routing. Adopting a mesh before you have those problems buys you a large operational surface and a debugging story your team has not yet needed. A monolith with a managed database and good telemetry beats this entire diagram for most teams, most of the time.
Before You Walk In

Interview-Day Checklist

A self-grading rubric — run your own mock interviews against this before the real one.

DimensionWhat good looks likeRed flag
ScopingExplicit FR/NFR split, a clear line on what's in and out.Jumping straight to architecture with no stated requirements.
Entities & API3–5 clean endpoints, mapped 1:1 to functional requirements.A full CRUD API for every entity "just in case."
Architecture walkthroughTraces an actual request through the diagram, once per use case.A static diagram presented with no data flow narrated.
Deep-dive rigorTies a specific NFR number to a specific bottleneck and fix.Generic scaling buzzwords with no connection to this system.
Trade-off articulationStates the cost of every choice, not just the benefit.Every decision presented as free / obviously correct.
CommunicationThinks out loud, checkpoints scope and direction with the interviewer.Long silent stretches, or an uninterrupted monologue.
Time managementRequirements + API ≈ 25% of time; deep dives get the largest share.20 minutes perfecting a schema, 5 minutes on deep dives.
AdaptabilityFollows the interviewer's steer into unplanned areas without losing the thread.Rigidly sticking to a memorized script regardless of where they lean in.
Blueprint — a personal reference framework for system design interview prep. Six steps, run the same way every time: Requirements → Core Entities → API/Interface → Data Flow (optional) → High-Level Design → Deep Dives.
